### ⭐ [Error-corrected function estimation advantage in multiparameter Hamiltonians](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16226v1)

**Highlighted author(s):** Alexey V. Gorshkov  
**Authors:** Erfan Abbasgholinejad, Lorcán O. Conlon, Sean R. Muleady, Jacob Bringewatt, Ali Fahimniya, Yu-Xin Wang, Alexey V. Gorshkov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16226v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.16226_figures/2609.16226_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. (a) Schematic of multiparameter function estima- tion with Markovian noise. A multi-qubit sensor with un- known Hamiltonian parameters θ and jump operators L is coupled through a parameter-independent control Hamilto- nian Hc(t) to a noiseless ancillary quantum processor. The objective is to estimate a function q(θ) without reconstructing the full parameter vector. (b) Optimized control and error correction reduce the dynamics to an effective single-qubit, single-parameter sensing problem with logical Hamiltonian ∝q(θ)ZL and, in general, logical dephasing noise. The func- tional HNLS condition (see Theorem 1) determines the ab- sence or presence of this logical noise or,...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of precisely estimating a function of multiple unknown parameters within a noisy quantum system. By employing quantum error correction techniques, the authors derive fundamental limits on measurement precision. The key finding is that measuring the function directly offers a significant advantage over measuring each parameter separately, even when noise is present.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous theoretical tools for optimizing quantum sensing protocols in realistic noisy environments, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes the ultimate precision limits for estimating a function of multiple Hamiltonian parameters in the presence of Markovian noise.

**Main result.** It shows that direct function estimation can substantially outperform estimating all parameters individually, achieving an advantage that scales with both evolution time and the number of parameters.

**Method.** The authors use quantum error correction (QEC) techniques, deriving bounds on the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) by reducing the multiparameter problem to an effective single-parameter sensing problem.

**Model / system.** The system evolves under a Lindblad master equation governed by a Hamiltonian $H(s) = H_0 + H_c(s)$, where $H_0$ depends on unknown parameters $	heta$. The analysis focuses on the achievable precision in estimating a function $q(	heta)$.

**Key observables.** The variance of the estimator $	ext{var}(q_{	ext{est}})$ and the Quantum Fisher Information ($F_q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Evolution time ($t$), number of parameters ($m$), and the noise strength ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing optimal quantum error-correcting codes and assuming the ability to perform measurements that project the dynamics onto a code space.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the multiparameter function estimation process with noise, showing how optimized control and error correction reduce the dynamics to an effective single-qubit sensing problem.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general noisy multiparameter estimation problem, deriving the functional Hamiltonian-not-in-Lindblad-span condition for Heisenberg scaling, and finally constructing codes and analyzing the advantage $\eta$ over individual parameter estimation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish the ultimate precision limits for estimating a function of multiple Hamiltonian parameters in the presence of Markovian noise. By reducing multiparameter function estimation to an optimization over effective single-parameter embeddings, we derive tight bounds on the quantum Fisher information. We identify a necessary and sufficient functional Hamiltonian-not-in-Lindblad-span condition for Heisenberg-limited scaling in time. When this condition holds, we construct a code that simultaneously removes the noise and nuisance Hamiltonian parameters while preserving the target signal. When it fails, we derive the optimal standard quantum-limit coefficient and show that it is asymptotically attainable using approximate quantum error correction. Finally, we demonstrate that direct function estimation can substantially outperform estimating all parameters individually and subsequently evaluating the function. This advantage can scale with both the evolution time and the number of parameters.

</details>

### [Exact group invariant scar towers in two dimensional gauge theories](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16132v1)

**Authors:** João Barata, Kiryl Pakrouski, Andrey V. Sadofyev  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16132v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `scars & prethermalization` **5/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.16132_figures/2609.16132_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Top: Entanglement entropy for the middle cut. The top horizontal line indicates the maximum possible entangle- ment in the system. Entanglement of scars is in agreement with analytical expressions [58]. Bottom: Two-point func- tion ⟨η′† j=1η′ j=L⟩. In the scar states it is n(L−n)</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.16132_figures/2609.16132_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Mappings of the η′ scar tower under Shiba and Wilson line transformations.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides an analytical construction of exact, gauge-invariant many-body scar states in 2D gauge theories. These states are characterized by suppressed entanglement and exhibit coherent, Josephson-like dynamics governed by a conserved pseudo-spin symmetry. The findings suggest a universal mechanism for non-thermal behavior in quantum field theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of protected, non-thermal states (scars) is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated systems, offering an analytical handle on deviations from thermalization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the open question of how out-of-equilibrium states in quantum gauge field theories lose memory of initial conditions, focusing on the persistence of exceptional, athermal states known as quantum many-body scars.

**Main result.** The authors construct an exact, algebraically defined tower of gauge-invariant scar states in 2D gauge theories, showing they exhibit anomalously low entanglement and long-range correlations.

**Method.** The construction relies on identifying a symmetry-generating algebra (using an $\eta$-pairing operator) that annihilates the scar subspace, which is then analyzed using exact diagonalization and bosonization techniques.

**Model / system.** The primary model is two-dimensional gauge theories, specifically the massless two-flavor Schwinger model ($QED(q, -q)_2$), analyzed on a lattice.

**Key observables.** Anomalously low bipartite entanglement entropy, long-range pair correlations, and coherent flavor precession dynamics ($\dot{\vartheta} = \Delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermion masses ($m_f$), the chemical potential bias ($\Delta$), and the lattice spacing ($a$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a conserved pseudo-spin symmetry that protects the scar subspace, and it notes that finite fermion masses break this exact protection.

**Figures summary.** Figures numerically compare entanglement entropy and two-point correlation functions for scar states versus thermal averages, and map the $\eta'$ scar tower under various transformations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general problem of ETH violation to the specific construction of the scar tower using algebraic methods, analyzing its properties (low entanglement, correlations) and its dynamics (Josephson-like precession) under symmetry-preserving and symmetry-breaking perturbations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct exact many-body scar towers in two dimensional gauge theories with two flavors of massless fundamental fermions. For oppositely charged fermions, the scar subspace is generated by a gauge-neutral $η$-pairing operator, and admits a purely algebraic construction. Exact diagonalization methods reveal anomalously low entanglement and long-range pair correlations in the scar states. Charge conjugation maps the $η$-tower to a vector-flavor polarization sector of the equal charge model, yielding an interpretation as a coherent flavor mode with Josephson-like phase dynamics. Finite fermion masses mix the protected pair with an orthogonal channel and destroy the exact tower. The identified scar tower can be algebraically realized in a large family of lattice field theories, revealing a universal character of these states. Our results provide an analytical construction of invariant scar subspaces in a gauge theory with a nontrivial continuum interpretation.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes the indistinguishability of single photons emitted from trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ions via Raman scattering. It shows that spontaneous decay back to the initial state degrades the Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference visibility. The key finding is that the mean number of back-decays ($\langle N angle$) provides a measurable metric directly correlating with the achievable quantum interference visibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it tackles the fundamental decoherence mechanism (spontaneous decay) limiting the performance of solid-state and trapped-ion quantum memories and repeaters. The quantitative link between decay statistics and interference visibility is a key theoretical result.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how spontaneous decay processes affect the indistinguishability of single Raman photons generated from trapped ions, which is crucial for quantum networking applications.

**Main result.** The mean number of back-decays ($\langle N angle$) is found to be a measurable quantity that directly correlates with the achievable Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) visibility, demonstrating a clear trade-off with photon yield.

**Method.** The authors employ numerical simulations based on solving the master equation in Lindblad form, utilizing quantum jump decomposition to track decay paths.

**Model / system.** The system involves single trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ions undergoing Raman scattering driven by few-nanosecond pulses, modeled as a three-level $\Lambda$-system. The dynamics are governed by a master equation incorporating spontaneous decay jump operators.

**Key observables.** HOM Visibility ($V$), Mean number of back-decays ($\langle N angle$), Probability of zero back-decays ($P_0$), and Raman photon success probability ($P_{	ext{Raman}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Excitation pulse duration (few-nanosecond scale), decay rates ($\Gamma$), and the specific ion species ($^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the validity of the three-level model and the use of quantum jump decomposition to account for decay pathways.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the distributions of back-decay probabilities ($P_N$) for varying pulse lengths and demonstrate the quantitative correlation between $\langle N angle$ and $V_{	ext{HOM}}$ using derived inequalities.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using the master equation and quantum jump decomposition. It then calculates key observables like $\langle N angle$ and $V_{	ext{HOM}}$, deriving explicit mathematical relationships between them. Finally, it extends the analysis to compare different trapped-ion species for long-range entanglement swapping.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically investigate the indistinguishability of single photons generated from single trapped $^{40}$Ca$^+$ ions in a Raman scattering process driven by few-nanosecond excitation pulses. Of particular interest is how spontaneous decay back to the initial state affects Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) photon interference. Numerical simulations identify the mean number of back-decays as a measurable quantity that correlates with achievable HOM visibility. Optimization of the excitation pulse with respect to a trade-off between photon yield and indistinguishability is analyzed. Finally, we compare the performance of different trapped-ion species for long-range dual-rail entanglement swapping via HOM photon interference.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses large-scale quantum simulations on superconducting hardware to map the phase diagram of a dissipative spin-1/2 Heisenberg chain. By analyzing the non-equilibrium steady state using static structure factors, the authors resolve long-standing theoretical uncertainties regarding the system's magnetic ordering. The results confirm that quantum computers are a viable tool for investigating complex open quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the intersection of open quantum systems and quantum computation, demonstrating that quantum hardware can tackle complex, non-equilibrium physics that is analytically or numerically intractable.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to map the phase diagram of a non-equilibrium steady state (NESS) in a dissipative spin-1/2 Heisenberg chain, a system difficult to analyze due to its coupling to an environment.

**Main result.** The quantum simulations successfully map a rich non-equilibrium phase diagram across the Jx-Jy plane, confirming the feasibility of using quantum hardware to study dissipative quantum systems.

**Method.** The study employs quantum hardware simulations (using Lindblad dynamics via Stinespring dilation) and compares these results with theoretical methods like Tensor Network Simulations (TJM) and exact diagonalization.

**Model / system.** The system is the dissipative spin-1/2 Heisenberg chain, whose dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation. The Hamiltonian involves couplings $J_x, J_y, J_z$, and the dissipation is introduced via local spontaneous spin decay.

**Key observables.** Static structure factor $S(q)$, which is used to identify magnetic orders (FM, AFM, SDW, PM) based on the peak position $q^*$ and prominence $\Pi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The phase diagram is scanned across the $J_x–J_y$ plane, with fixed parameters like $J_z = \gamma = 1$. System sizes up to $L=50$ sites were simulated.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis must account for the limitations of the Trotter approximation, which can introduce spurious 'incipient' phases, and the need for specialized interpolation techniques (like phasors) to cross phase boundaries.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup on quantum hardware, the convergence of correlation functions to steady states, and the resulting phase diagrams mapping magnetic orders (FM, AFM, SDW) across the coupling parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by defining the physical model and the challenge of NESS computation, detailing the quantum hardware simulation approach, presenting the results via structure factor analysis, and finally comparing and refining the phase diagram using advanced interpolation techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A quantum many-body system coupled to an environment relaxes to a nonequilibrium steady state that can sustain order with no equilibrium counterpart. Computing such steady states is harder than closed-system dynamics as the density matrix problem squares the Hilbert-space dimension, and no free energy selects the steady state. The dissipative spin-1/2 Heisenberg chain is a benchmark example for nonequilibrium steady state physics; various methods have each calculated its phase diagram but do not agree, and a controlled determination at large system size has remained out of reach. Here we simulate the Lindblad dynamics of chains of up to 50 sites on the superconducting processor ibm_kingston -- 100 simultaneously active qubits at up to 1700 entangling-gate depths -- realizing the dissipation via Stinespring dilation. The system's dissipative evolution is a self-correcting mechanism that effectively erases errors, so hardware noise enters only as a weak competing dissipator. We measure static structure factors and resolve ferromagnetic, antiferromagnetic, spin-density-wave, and paramagnetic steady states, mapping the phase diagram with 117 quantum hardware data points across the $J_x$--$J_y$ plane. We uncover a rich non-equilibrium phase diagram of ordered phases with only remnants of the mean-field order, and where sharp transitions give way to the crossovers expected in one dimension. We also find the existence of an incipient (Trotter-induced) spin density wave phase, highlighting the potential of controlled Trotterization as a tool to engineer various magnetic phases in dissipative spin systems. Our quantum simulations largely settle the lingering uncertainty regarding the correct phase diagram of this benchmark system. Moreover, they show that quantum computers are now a feasible tool for addressing scientific questions involving dissipative quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to analyze the transient counting statistics of autonomous quantum clocks modeled by multi-level emitters. By employing a revised Large Deviation Principle, the authors quantify how initial conditions affect the count statistics in the few-quanta regime. This provides a new, complementary tool for understanding the fundamental limits of precision timekeeping in quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics, specifically quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it pushes the theoretical understanding of quantum measurement statistics into the non-trivial transient regime.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses characterizing the fundamental limits to precise timekeeping and quantum sensing using autonomous quantum emitters when operating in the few-quanta regime.

**Main result.** It develops a framework to characterize transient initial-state contributions to the counting statistics, providing complementary insights beyond the standard semiclassical or steady-state analyses.

**Method.** The analysis employs a revised Large Deviation Principle incorporating finite-time contributions, utilizing the moment generating function (MGF) derived from the evolution of the tilted density operator.

**Model / system.** The system models simple two- and three-level quantum emitters (lasers) acting as quantum coherent emitters, whose dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation and fueled by dephasing quantum noise.

**Key observables.** Transient counting statistics, average number of jumps $\langle N angle_t$, and the variance of the count record.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the few-quanta regime ($\langle N angle_t \sim 1$) in the temporal domain, contrasting with the semiclassical regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of radiative transitions in an artificial atom and develops a framework to retain transient initial-state contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates clock dynamics for both two-level and three-level emitters, detailing the decay and dephasing pumping mechanisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general framework of quantum emitter dynamics (Lindblad equation) to applying advanced statistical tools (LDP, MGF) to calculate transient counting statistics, culminating in discussions on timekeeping implications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

At the heart of every atomic clock is a quantum coherent emitter, the laser, that could in principle be treated autonomously. While their frequency or phase stability is typically studied in the semiclassical regime of a large number of emitted quanta, recent works on autonomous quantum clocks suggest that the few-quanta regime can offer statistical information in the temporal domain. This provides complementary means to probe the fundamental limits to precise timekeeping in the quantum regime. Motivated by these works, we consider simple models for two- and three-level quantum emitters subject to an athermal fueling through dephasing, which generically results in coherences in the steady state. A large deviation principle with finite-time contributions is used to identify the transient counting statistics and their dependence on the initial conditions. We conclude by discussing the implications for precise timekeeping and quantum sensing using autonomous quantum emitters as clocks in their transient regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a novel Krylov-space mechanism to exactly calculate the leading interaction correction to the diffusivity in a dissipative quantum spin chain. By mapping the dynamics onto a solvable semi-infinite Krylov chain, the authors derive a closed-form, microscopic expression for the transport coefficient in the strong-dissipation regime. This provides a powerful analytical tool for understanding quantum transport in open many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of Krylov subspace techniques to solve non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in the strong-dissipation limit provides a powerful, exact analytical tool for calculating transport properties in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the analytically challenging computation of transport coefficients, specifically the leading density-dependent correction to the diffusivity, in interacting quantum many-body systems.

**Main result.** The authors analytically determine this correction by mapping the dissipative dynamics onto a self-similar semi-infinite Krylov chain, yielding a closed-form expression for the diffusivity.

**Method.** The core method involves developing a novel Krylov-space mechanism applied to the Green-Kubo formula within the strong-dissipation regime, allowing for the exact solution of the resulting operator hierarchy.

**Model / system.** The system studied is a one-dimensional spin-1/2 XXZ chain undergoing Lindbladian evolution due to symmetric incoherent nearest-neighbor spin exchange. The model is mapped onto an interacting spinless-fermion chain.

**Key observables.** Diffusivity (D), the interaction-dependent correction to diffusivity ($D_{GK}$), and the boundary Green's function ($G_{m1}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong-dissipation regime ($\gamma \gg |J|, |U|$), hopping amplitude ($J$), interaction strength ($U$), and dissipation rate ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system supports only a diffusive mode and relies on the strong-dissipation limit for the perturbative expansion.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework using the Mori-Zwanzig formalism and the Green-Kubo formula. It then focuses on the strong-dissipation limit, where the problem is mapped onto an emergent Krylov chain structure. The solvability of this chain allows for the exact determination of the boundary Green's function, which yields the final result for $D_{GK}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The computation of transport coefficients in interacting quantum many-body systems is rarely analytically accessible. Here, we develop a new Krylov-space mechanism that makes the leading density dependent correction to Green-Kubo diffusivity \emph{exactly} calculable in the strong-dissipation regime of a one-dimensional noisy spin-$\frac{1}{2}$ XXZ chain. This is done by showing that the density-polarization-dressed bond coherence generates a Krylov subspace where repeated action of the dissipator closes exactly on an explicitly identifiable operator family. Within this subspace, the dissipative dynamics is then mapped onto a self-similar semi-infinite chain with a boundary defect. Surprisingly, \emph{all} Lanczos coefficients of the Krylov chain and its boundary Green's function can be determined exactly. The latter then determines the exact leading density-dependent correction to the diffusivity. Finally, we calculate analytically the full boundary-to-bulk Green's function and find that it decays exponentially along the emergent Krylov chain.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper studies how noise affects the classical simulability of quantum circuits by analyzing the optimal way to 'unravel' the noisy dynamics into stochastic trajectories. It discovers that noise can create extended 'classical phases' where the system behaves classically, but this classicality is an artifact of the measurement process, not the underlying channel itself. This work provides a resource-theoretic framework for understanding the limits of classical simulation in open quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource theory of quantum computation, showing how measurement choices (unraveling) can artificially induce classicality, which is highly relevant for understanding decoherence and measurement-induced phase transitions in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the boundary between quantum dynamics that are classically simulable and those that require true quantum computation, particularly in the presence of noise.

**Main result.** It identifies 'noise-induced classical phases'—parameter regions where the dynamics become fully disentangled by Clifford operations, depending crucially on the measurement scheme (unraveling) used.

**Method.** The authors analyze open quantum dynamics by unraveling them into stochastic quantum trajectories, simulating these using Clifford-augmented matrix product states, and minimizing a derived 'simulation cost.'

**Model / system.** The model involves random quantum circuits composed of Clifford gates, non-Clifford phase rotations, and local noise, studied via the evolution of the density matrix.

**Key observables.** Simulation cost ($ar{c}$), entanglement, nonstabilizerness, and the optimal unraveling choice.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($p$), non-Clifford density, and the circuit depth ($N_T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on finding an optimal convex decomposition of the quantum channel and assumes the ability to define an optimal unraveling that minimizes the simulation cost.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches the noisy circuit setup and a phase diagram showing three dynamical regions separated by phase boundaries. Other figures illustrate the geometric structure of the channel in the complex plane.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the problem using noisy random circuits, developing the resource-based simulation cost ($ar{c}$), analytically determining the optimal unraveling, and contrasting the results obtained from trajectory-based measurements versus the averaged density matrix.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the classical simulability of open quantum dynamics using random Clifford circuits doped with non-Clifford phase rotations and subject to local noise. We unravel the dynamics into stochastic quantum trajectories simulated with Clifford-augmented matrix product states, and introduce a simulation cost that quantifies the classical resources required. Optimizing this cost over stochastic unravelings, we identify noise-induced classical phases: extended parameter regions in which the dynamics can be fully disentangled by Clifford operations at arbitrary circuit depth. Their existence depends on both the noise model \emph{and} the unraveling. Using a geometric representation of quantum channels, we analytically determine optimal unravelings for a broad class of noise models, with numerical simulations confirming the predicted phase boundaries. We further relate the optimal cost to the unraveling-independent nonstabilizerness of the channel and show that, together with trajectory-resolved entanglement and nonstabilizerness, it classifies distinct dynamical regimes. Finally, we show that these classical phases disappear in the averaged density-matrix description, where no unraveling freedom remains. Our results show that the emergence of classicality in noisy random circuits depends on the measurement scheme adopted to probe it, and paves the way to further studies on the classical simulability of driven-dissipative dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes quantum information spreading in a kicked Ising chain by using partial projected ensembles (PPEs). It finds that spatial constraints cause a dramatic difference in how fast correlations build up between ergodic and tilt-constrained regimes. The research provides an information-theoretic tool to distinguish between the physical time needed for correlations to develop and the information that can actually be measured when parts of the system are unobserved.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics and information scrambling in systems exhibiting localization or strong disorder, which are central topics in condensed matter theory, particularly concerning Many-Body Localization (MBL) physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how spatial constraints affect the emergence of quantum information about many-body dynamics, specifically comparing the time required for correlations to develop versus the information accessible under partial observation.

**Main result.** Partial projected ensembles reveal a strong contrast in correlation onset times between ergodic and tilt-constrained regimes, with the latter showing a significantly delayed buildup. The study establishes that partial observation probes the distinction between correlation development time and accessible information.

**Method.** The authors use numerical simulations on a kicked Ising chain, employing partial projected ensembles (PPEs) and analyzing various information-theoretic diagnostics like quantum mutual information and moment distances.

**Model / system.** The system is a kicked Ising chain subjected to a spatially varying longitudinal field, governed by a Floquet evolution operator. The analysis involves partitioning the chain into three regions: a target region (A), an unobserved buffer (E), and a measured region (B).

**Key observables.** Conditional-state fluctuations, quantum mutual information, connected second moment, and the onset time ($t^*$) for correlation buildup.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control parameter $\Gamma$ (related to kick strength and Stark tilt), buffer length ($L_E$), and the comparison between the ergodic and tilt-constrained regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mathematical framework of projected ensembles and assumes the applicability of specific information-theoretic bounds ($\Delta \le \chi \le I_q$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of correlation onset times ($t^*$) in ergodic vs. Stark-constrained regimes, the decay of moment distances ($\Delta(k)$) with buffer length, and the evolution of mutual information ($I$) over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of PPEs to probe information beyond the reduced density matrix. It analyzes the dynamics in two distinct regimes (ergodic vs. tilt-constrained), deriving quantitative scaling laws for the onset of correlations and demonstrating the separation of phase accumulation and coherence loss using a solvable dephasing model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Projected ensembles reveal information about quantum many-body dynamics beyond the reduced density matrix. Here we investigate how spatial constraints affect the emergence of this information in a kicked Ising chain with a spatially varying longitudinal field. We consider partial projected ensembles, obtained by measuring a remote region while leaving an intervening buffer unobserved. Our numerical results reveal a pronounced contrast between nearly ballistic correlation onset in the ergodic regime and strongly delayed onset in the tilt-constrained regime, corroborated by quantum mutual information. Despite these different onset scales, the conditional-state fluctuations decrease approximately exponentially with buffer length in both regimes. Full projected ensembles additionally exhibit slow relaxation and persistent measurement-basis dependence in their higher moments. We establish an information-theoretic characterization of the connected second moment and use a solvable dephasing model with effective-basis measurements to illustrate the separation of phase accumulation and coherence loss. Within this model, a factorially suppressed coupling envelope yields sublogarithmic spreading consistent with the finite-distance onset trends. Our results identify partial projected ensembles as probes of the distinction between the time required for correlations to develop and the information that remains accessible under partial observation.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers developed a novel architecture by coupling a Josephson Voltage Standard (JVS) to a Josephson Photonics amplifier (ICTA) to solve the problem of phase noise. This integration successfully establishes a stable phase reference, leading to a significant gain enhancement and enabling the first demonstration of phase-sensitive quantum effects like squeezing in a dc-biased superconducting amplifier.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the challenge of phase coherence in superconducting quantum circuits, which is crucial for realizing high-fidelity quantum light sources and parametric processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional parametric pumping methods suffer from parasitic nonlinearities, and existing Josephson Photonics (JP) devices lack a stable phase reference necessary for phase-coherent operation.

**Main result.** By integrating a Josephson Voltage Standard (JVS) to provide a noise-tolerant phase reference, the authors achieved a 14-dB gain enhancement and enabled the first observation of phase-sensitive gain and squeezing in a dc-biased amplifier.

**Method.** The study involves characterizing the Interacting Cavity Transmission Amplifier (ICTA) using gain maps and phase-space analysis, comparing performance with and without the active JVS reference.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes Josephson Junctions forming an ICTA, which is coupled to a JVS. The physics is described by Josephson junction potentials and analyzed using quantum Langevin equations and phase-space diagnostics.

**Key observables.** Averaged gain (up to 27 dB), quantum-limited noise performance, phase-sensitive gain, and squeezing characteristics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** DC bias voltage, RF tone power ($P_{JVS}$), signal frequency ($f_S$), and the phase difference ($\Delta$) between the signal and the phase reference.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The phase reference from the JVS is assumed to be coherently transferred to the ICTA via the superconducting order parameter, and phase noise averaging is assumed when the JVS is off.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare ICTA gain and noise with and without JVS stabilization, showing phase-space distributions that transition from phase-preserving (JVS off) to phase-sensitive (JVS on).

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of JP devices, then introduces the JVS-ICTA architecture. It proceeds to characterize the gain and noise in different regimes (degenerate vs. non-degenerate), demonstrating the critical role of the JVS in stabilizing the phase reference and enabling phase-sensitive quantum effects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Parametric interactions are foundational to superconducting quantum technologies, yet conventional microwave-driven pumping introduces parasitic Kerr nonlinearities and higher-order harmonics that limit device performance. Josephson Photonics (JP) avoids these parasitics by utilizing dc-biased junctions but has remained constrained by high phase noise and the absence of a stable phase reference. Here, we overcome this limitation by integrating a Josephson voltage standard (JVS) to establish a noise-tolerant phase reference, and demonstrate that this reference is coherently transferred to an inelastic Cooper-pair tunneling amplifier (ICTA) via the superconducting order parameter.The resulting low-phase-noise architecture yields a 14-dB enhancement in averaged gain and better quantum-limited noise performance. Critically, the phase reference enables the first observation of phase-sensitive gain and sqeezing in a dc-biased amplifier. By reconciling clean, Kerr-free nonlinearities with phase-coherent drive, our architecture establishes a robust platform for high-purity parametric processes in superconducting circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents qdmag, a Python package designed to simulate the time evolution of magnetization in magnetic molecules. It solves the quantum master equation, incorporating complex spin Hamiltonians and spin-phonon coupling effects. The tool is valuable for studying non-equilibrium spin dynamics in molecular nanomagnets under realistic, time-varying field conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a specialized, robust numerical tool for simulating open quantum dynamics in molecular magnets, which is a core area of AMO physics and quantum magnetism.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the simulation of nonequilibrium magnetization dynamics in magnetic molecules subjected to time-varying external magnetic fields and spin-phonon coupling.

**Main result.** The authors introduce qdmag, a computational package capable of simulating magnetization over long timescales, providing a tool to bridge theory and numerical calculation for spin dynamics.

**Method.** The dynamics are governed by solving the generalized Lindblad quantum master equation, utilizing a staircase approximation for time-varying fields and constructing effective Hamiltonians for computational feasibility.

**Model / system.** The system involves magnetic molecules whose spin Hamiltonian includes magnetic exchange interactions and Zero-Field Splitting (ZFS) terms. The dynamics are treated as an open quantum system coupled to a phonon bath, described by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Nonequilibrium magnetization $M(t)$ and occupation numbers of energy levels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phonon bath spectral density parameters ($\alpha$), external magnetic field strength and time variation rate, and coupling constants ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The package assumes the external magnetic field direction remains unchanged during the sweep, and calculations for high-dimensional systems rely on constructing lower-dimensional effective Hamiltonians.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the staircase function for field approximation, and case studies show magnetization plateaus and Rabi oscillations in single-spin systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the theoretical framework (Hamiltonian decomposition, Lindblad equation), details the numerical methods (staircase approximation, effective basis construction), and validates the tool through multiple case studies (one-spin, two-spin, three-spin systems).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present qdmag, a computational utility for calculating the magnetization of magnetic molecules in a time-varying external magnetic field by solving a generalized Lindblad quantum master equation with spin-phonon coupling treated as a dissipation term. The package relies on the spin Hamiltonian formalism, which includes both the magnetic exchange interactions and the zero-field splitting (ZFS) interaction. Different types of magnetic exchange interaction and ZFS terms up to 12th order, which are written in terms of the extended Stevens operators, are supported. qdmag enables long-time evolution of magnetization for up to a few milliseconds, which is achieved through a staircase approximation of the magnetic field as a function of time. For high-dimensional spin Hamiltonians, a lower-dimensional effective Hamiltonian is constructed to make the calculations computationally feasible. Three case studies of one-spin, two-spin, and three-spin systems are presented to demonstrate the usage of qdmag. Our work offers a computational tool to bridge the gap between theoretical development and numerical calculations of spin dynamics in the presence of spin-phonon coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a quantum circuit algorithm to simulate Lindblad evolution, which models open quantum systems. By using a transducer framework, the authors achieve an optimal additive query complexity, resolving a long-standing question about the necessary scaling of resources. This result is significant for understanding the fundamental computational limits of simulating dissipative quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the resource scaling (query complexity) for simulating open quantum systems, which is crucial for quantum simulation algorithms in AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental query complexity required to simulate Lindblad evolution for open quantum systems over a given time $t$ to a precision $\epsilon$. It aims to close the gap between the known lower and upper bounds for this simulation.

**Main result.** The authors provide an algorithm achieving optimal additive query complexity, $O(	au + 	ext{polylog}(	au/\epsilon))$, for general Lindblad simulation in the block-encoding model. This proves that the multiplicative dependence on time previously suspected was not a fundamental necessity in this model.

**Method.** The approach utilizes a transducer framework, combining local dissipative transducers with Hamiltonian transducers, and employs linear combinations of reuse circuits to manage error accumulation.

**Model / system.** The system is an open quantum system whose dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation. The simulation is implemented using quantum circuits within the block-encoding model.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the approximation of the quantum channel $e^{Lt}$ representing the evolution of the density matrix $ho$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the evolution time $t$, the desired precision $\epsilon$, and the total evolution time $	au$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the block-encoding model, and the gate complexity of the resulting algorithm is noted to be higher than existing methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general problem of Lindblad simulation, details the use of transducers for composition, addresses error control (including catalyst-removal error), and extends the method to time-dependent generators.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For the problem of simulating Lindblad evolution for time $t$ to precision $ε$, Hamiltonian simulation provides an additive query lower bound, informally, $Ω(t + \mathrm{polylog}(1/ε))$. However, the best previously known algorithms for general Lindblad simulation achieve a multiplicative upper bound, informally, $\mathcal{O}(t\,\mathrm{polylog}(1/ε))$, in gate complexity. It has remained open whether this multiplicative dependence is necessary. In this paper, we close the gap in query complexity by giving an algorithm with optimal additive dependence on evolution time and precision in the block-encoding model. Our approach uses the transducer framework to reduce the query cost of composing first-order approximations to the evolution channel, together with linear combinations of reuse circuits of different lengths to suppress catalyst-removal error.   Although our additional gate complexity is higher than that of existing algorithms, our optimal query complexity resolves the question of how much oracle access is fundamentally necessary and identifies the remaining challenge to achieve the optimal gate complexity.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on achieving quantum-projection-noise-limited readout in a dense ensemble of NV centers in diamond. By using nuclear spin memory, they significantly improved readout fidelity, enabling the direct measurement of QPN. The key finding is the observation that intrinsic dipolar interactions cause the QPN to develop a measurable anisotropic profile, paving the way for solid-state entanglement sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial experimental realization of measuring collective quantum noise in a solid-state platform, directly linking fundamental many-body interactions (dipolar coupling) to measurable quantum metrological signatures (noise anisotropy).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to directly observe and characterize the quantum projection noise (QPN) in a strongly-interacting, two-dimensional ensemble of solid-state spins, specifically quantifying how intrinsic dipolar interactions modify this noise profile.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated QPN-resolved readout and directly observed that the dipolar interactions shear the QPN into a measurable anisotropic profile, opening pathways for solid-state entanglement sensing.

**Method.** The measurement technique involved leveraging repetitive readout via the NV's nuclear spin memory to reach the quantum projection noise limit, followed by measuring the time-dependent variance of the collective spin operator along different axes.

**Model / system.** The system is a dense, strongly interacting ensemble of Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, confined to a thin 2D layer. The dynamics are governed by dipolar interactions, which are analyzed using the collective spin Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Residual variance ($\sigma^2_{	ext{res}}$), Quantum Projection Noise ($\sigma^2_{	ext{QPN}}$), and the angular dependence of the noise ($\sigma^2_{	ext{res}}(t, \phi)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Readout noise ($\sigma_R$), number of spins ($N$), dipolar coupling strength ($J_0$), and the sensing time ($T_{	ext{sense}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that readout imperfections contribute isotropically to the measured variance, allowing the measured anisotropy to be attributed solely to the spin dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the improvement in readout fidelity via repetitive readout, the characteristic twice-per-Rabi-cycle oscillation of QPN, and the time evolution of the noise anisotropy, showing the shearing effect of dipolar interactions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the necessity of high-fidelity readout (using nuclear memory) to demonstrating the QPN signature, and finally, analyzing the time evolution of the noise variance to isolate and characterize the dipolar-induced anisotropy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The nitrogen-vacancy (NV) center in diamond is a prominent quantum-sensing platform. Combining readout at the quantum projection noise limit with strong dipolar interactions promises substantial gains in sensitivity. However, experimentally accessing this regime has remained a longstanding challenge. In this work, we demonstrate quantum-projection-noise-resolved readout of a strongly-interacting, two-dimensional ensemble of NV centers. Our approach leverages repetitive readout via the NV's intrinsic $^{15}$N nuclear memory at a moderate magnetic field ($\sim 0.3$ T), improving the readout fidelities by nearly an order of magnitude. This enables us to directly resolve the quantum projection noise of a coherent spin state and to watch the ensemble's intrinsic dipolar interactions shear this noise into an anisotropic profile. Our results open the door to direct measurements of spin squeezing and entanglement-enhanced sensing in the solid state.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates the realization of a programmable two-dimensional free Dirac Hamiltonian using circuit QED. By coupling a transmon qubit to a multimode cavity, the authors simulate the dynamics of massive Dirac particles, observing characteristic Zitterbewegung. This establishes the platform as a powerful tool for studying higher-dimensional relativistic quantum phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of quantum simulation in circuit QED, demonstrating control over complex, relativistic Hamiltonians (like the massive Dirac equation) in a solid-state platform, which is a major goal in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is the programmable quantum simulation of two-dimensional massive Dirac dynamics, a regime that has not been experimentally explored despite successes in 1D and massless 2D simulations.

**Main result.** The authors successfully realized a programmable 2D free Dirac Hamiltonian in circuit QED, observing rotational Zitterbewegung and confirming the platform's utility for higher-dimensional relativistic quantum dynamics.

**Method.** The simulation relies on engineering the Hamiltonian using a single transmon qubit coupled to two modes of a multimode cavity, with dynamics analyzed via time-dependent master-equation simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a circuit QED setup using a transmon qubit coupled to a multimode cavity. The dynamics are governed by an effective Dirac Hamiltonian, which is programmed by tuning the qubit-cavity couplings and detunings.

**Key observables.** Rotational Zitterbewegung of the Dirac particle, expectation values of cavity quadratures ($\langle\hat{X}_jangle$), and time-averaged fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Effective mass (tunable via detuning $\Delta\Omega$), Rabi frequencies ($\Omega_R$), cavity mode coupling strengths ($\chi_j$), and decoherence times ($T_{1,ho}, T_{2,ho}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis incorporates corrections beyond the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA), finite transmon anharmonicity, and measured decoherence effects, which are key limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy-level diagram, the schematic of the Dirac Hamiltonian dynamics, the experimental setup, and time evolution plots comparing experimental data to master-equation simulations for Zitterbewegung.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical realization of the 2D Dirac Hamiltonian, presents calibration procedures for drive amplitudes and phases, demonstrates the measurement protocol using characteristic functions, and finally analyzes the resulting Zitterbewegung dynamics while accounting for decoherence and anharmonicity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional massive Dirac systems feature a gapped Dirac-cone dispersion central to a broad range of phenomena in condensed-matter and topological physics. While quantum simulations have demonstrated one-dimensional Dirac dynamics and two-dimensional massless Weyl dynamics, programmable simulation of massive two-dimensional Dirac dynamics remains experimentally unexplored. Here, we realize a programmable two-dimensional free Dirac Hamiltonian in circuit QED, with independently tunable spin-momentum couplings and mass, using a single Rabi-driven qubit coupled to two modes of a multimode cavity. Using this Hamiltonian, we observe rotational Zitterbewegung of a two-dimensional massive Dirac particle and its dependence on the effective mass. Time-dependent master-equation simulations incorporating measured decoherence, corrections beyond the rotating-wave approximation (RWA), and anharmonicity of the transmon reproduce the observed dynamics. Our results establish multimode circuit QED as a compact, programmable platform for higher-dimensional relativistic quantum dynamics and provide a foundation for exploring dynamical and topological phenomena in gapped Dirac systems.

</details>

### [Analytical solutions to the non-equilibrium Green's functions in large-$q$ SYK models](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16438v1)

**Authors:** Jan C. Louw  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16438v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **4/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **3/5** · `scars & prethermalization` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.16438_figures/2609.16438_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. (a) Schematic of the quench protocol. The time axis is partitioned into I0, I1. The quench occurs at τ1 = 0. The quench strengths are encoded in the alignment of the coupling vectors, cab ≡⃗Ja · ⃗Jb/(JaJb). To simplify notation, we label functions by the intervals in which their time arguments lie, e.g. J (t1) = Ja or cab = c(t1, t2) for (t1, t2) ∈Ib ×Ib. (b) Partition of the (t1, t2)-plane into blocks Ib×Ib. The boundary conditions are imposed along the blue connecting lines.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work analytically solves the non-equilibrium Green's functions for the SYK model following a quench between non-commuting Hamiltonians. By finding closed-form solutions for all time blocks, the authors derive an exact temperature update rule. Crucially, they show that the system must heat up, providing a geometric manifestation of the second law of thermodynamics for this chaotic quantum system.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of non-equilibrium dynamics and the emergence of thermodynamic laws (like the second law) from microscopic, solvable models like SYK is highly relevant for understanding thermalization in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper provides analytical solutions for the non-equilibrium Green's functions in large-$q$ Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) models following a quench between non-commuting Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** Analytical solutions are found for all time blocks, establishing an exact temperature update rule and demonstrating geometrically that heating is inevitable for the closed system, confirming the second law.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Keldysh contour formalism and solves differential equations governing the Green's functions in the large-$q$ limit, utilizing SL(2) symmetry parametrization.

**Model / system.** The system is the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, a strongly interacting, chaotic quantum system. The study focuses on the dynamics after a quench between non-commuting $q/2$-body Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Non-equilibrium Green's functions, $G(t_1, t_2)$, and the resulting temperature update rule relating pre- and post-quench energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The large-$q$ limit, the coupling factor $c_{01}$ (related to the angle between coupling vectors $\vartheta$), and the initial/final energies $\epsilon_0, \epsilon_1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The solvability is established in the large-$q$ limit and at leading order in $1/q$. The analysis focuses on the Hermitian case ($\phi=0$) for real couplings.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the quench protocol by partitioning the time axis and the $(t_1, t_2)$-plane into distinct time blocks.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon known leading-order solutions, derives the dynamics for the off-diagonal Green's function blocks using differential equations, solves these equations analytically, and finally extracts the energy and temperature update rules.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It is known that the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model is exactly solvable at leading order in $1/q$; specifically, the instantaneously thermal time block after the quench is known in closed form. Thus far the remaining (inherently out of equilibrium) blocks have only been numerically accessible. Here we provide their analytical solutions for a quench between non-commuting $q/2$-body SYK Hamiltonians. We obtain the Green's functions in closed form for every time block. We extract a simple relation between the pre- and post-quench energies $ε_1 \propto ε_0$ leading to an exact temperature update rule. Via a Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we show that heating is inevitable for the closed system; hence, the second law emerges geometrically. The solution is used in a companion paper to address what ``instantaneous thermalization'' means at finite $q$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the geometric phase dynamics of a driven, dissipative qubit using advanced numerical techniques. It demonstrates that periodic driving allows for active control over how the environment deforms the quantum phase. The findings establish a crucial link between Floquet engineering, quantum dissipation, and the control of open quantum system dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the control of quantum coherence (GP) in realistic, open quantum systems by explicitly incorporating the effects of time-periodic driving, which is a major topic in modern quantum physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the interplay between periodic driving (Floquet dressing) and environmental dissipation affects the geometric phase (GP) accumulated by a driven qubit.

**Main result.** Periodic driving offers a mechanism to control the dissipative geometric phase, as the resulting dissipation pathways depend jointly on Floquet matrix-element weights and the bath spectral overlap.

**Method.** The study employs the numerically exact Process-Tensor Time-Evolving Matrix Product Operator (PT-TEMPO) method combined with Floquet theory to analyze the reduced density matrix dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a symmetric two-level system (qubit) modeled by the Spin-Boson Model (SBM), subjected to a longitudinal monochromatic drive and coupled to an Ohmic bosonic environment.

**Key observables.** Geometric Phase (GP), non-additive GP contribution ($\delta\gamma_D$), and the purity/Bloch vector trajectory of the reduced density matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive amplitude ($A$), drive frequency ($\Omega$), and the characteristic bath spectral density parameters ($\alpha, \omega_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis avoids assuming weak coupling or Markovian dynamics, using the PT-TEMPO method for accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the strengths of three coupling terms—Floquet matrix-element weights, bath spectral density, and their product—across different drive frequency regimes to determine the conditions for strong dissipation.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure introducing the model, reviewing the theoretical framework (including GP and Floquet theory), presenting numerical results analyzing the drive-bath interplay, and concluding with the implications for quantum control.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Building on recent work by Wang et al. on the dissipative geometric phase (GP) in the spin-boson model (SBM), the GP dynamics of a periodically driven symmetric SBM are investigated using the numerically exact process-tensor time-evolving matrix product operator (PT-TEMPO) method. A symmetric two-level system subjected to a longitudinal monochromatic drive along the same system coordinate that couples to an Ohmic bosonic environment exhibits rich GP dynamics arising from the interplay between driving and dissipation. To isolate cooperative drive-bath effects, a non-additive GP contribution is introduced that separates the driven dissipative response from its driven-unitary and undriven-dissipative components. Periodic driving is found to either suppress or enhance bath-induced GP deformation depending on the interplay between drive and bath timescales. These trends are interpreted through Floquet spectral steering, whereby the drive redistributes the coherent dynamics among quasienergy sidebands whose transition frequencies and Fourier-resolved system-bath matrix elements determine their coupling to the environmental spectrum. Floquet analysis shows that the dominant dissipative pathways are governed jointly by Floquet matrix-element weights and bath spectral overlap, rather than by either alone. These results connect Floquet engineering, quantum dissipation, and GP dynamics and establish periodic driving as a means of controlling the GP of open quantum systems (OQSs).

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically explores generating pure spin currents in an antiferromagnetic chain using nonadiabatic quantum pumping. By applying time-dependent potentials and analyzing spin-resolved currents via NEGF, the authors show that the spin current can be significantly enhanced while the net charge current is suppressed. This provides a promising pathway for designing energy-efficient spin-pumping spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer spin currents purely through time-dependent potentials in an antiferromagnet is highly relevant for spintronics, offering a mechanism for low-dissipation spin manipulation without relying on magnetic impurities or external magnetic fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the generation and control of pure spin currents in an antiferromagnetic chain without applying an external bias.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that by operating in the nonadiabatic pumping regime, a pure spin current can be generated and controlled by tuning the chemical potential and driving frequency, allowing for spin current flow with negligible net charge transfer.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Keldysh non-equilibrium Green's function (NEGF) formalism, treating the system as a tight-binding model driven by time-dependent potentials.

**Model / system.** The system is an antiferromagnetic atomic chain coupled to two non-magnetic leads. The Hamiltonian includes antiferromagnetic exchange interactions, and the driving is achieved via two time-dependent potentials applied to the chain ends.

**Key observables.** Pumped spin current ($I_s$), charge current ($I_c$), spin-up and spin-down spin-resolved currents.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving frequency ($\Omega$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and the antiferromagnetic exchange field ($h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations utilize the Keldysh NEGF formalism and often employ the first harmonic approximation in the Floquet representation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral current for spin-up/spin-down channels, showing spectral separation at high frequencies. Subsequent figures map the pumped DC currents ($I_c$ and $I_s$) as functions of $\mu$ and $\Omega$, highlighting the near-vanishing charge current while spin current persists.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the spin current response across different driving regimes: first showing spin symmetry in the adiabatic regime, then demonstrating frequency-induced spin-up/spin-down divergence in the nonadiabatic regime, and finally mapping the control landscape using $\mu$ and $\Omega$ to achieve pure spin pumping.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this study, quantum spin pumping in an antiferromagnetic chain driven by time-dependent potential is investigated. The aim is to explore the possibility of generating and controlling spin currents in the absence of external bias and to examine the role of exchange field and periodic driving in the separation of spin-up and spin-down currents. The system is described using a tight-binding model, and spin-resolved currents are calculated employing the Keldysh non-equilibrium Green's function formalism. Two time-dependent potentials with a specific phase difference are applied to the two ends of the chain, while the chemical potentials of both electrodes are set equal. The results demonstrate that in the adiabatic regime (low frequencies), the response of the two spin channels is nearly identical. However, as the driving frequency increases and the system enters the nonadiabatic regime, absorption and emission processes of energy quanta become activated, leading to significant differences between spin-up and spin-down currents. The pumped current exhibits strong dependence on the chemical potential, allowing for the control of both magnitude and direction of the spin current through its adjustment. With increasing frequency, the spin current enhances, and parameters can be tuned such that the charge current nearly vanishes while a considerable spin current persists. This finding indicates the feasibility of achieving nearly pure spin pumping without net charge transfer in the antiferromagnetic chain. The results provide a promising perspective for designing spin-pumping devices based on antiferromagnetic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This research presents a robust method to optically trap colloidal quantum dots by encapsulating them in a polymer matrix. This encapsulation enhances the mechanical trapping force, allowing for stable confinement measurements (fluctuations < 20 nm) while simultaneously confirming that the quantum properties, such as single-photon antibunching, remain fully preserved under the trapping laser illumination.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the critical engineering hurdle in quantum optics: how to physically manipulate fragile quantum emitters (like single-photon sources) in a controlled environment without destroying their quantum coherence or emission properties.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving stable optical confinement for solution-based colloidal quantum dots (CQDs) without compromising their delicate quantum optical properties.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated that encapsulating CQDs in a polymer matrix allows for robust optical trapping (fluctuations < 20 nm) while preserving single-photon emission characteristics ($g^{(2)}(0) \approx 0.225$).

**Method.** The study combines optical trapping experiments with advanced characterization techniques, including trajectory tracking, spectral measurements, and second-order photon correlation measurements.

**Model / system.** The system involves Colloidal Quantum Dots (CQDs) encapsulated in a transparent polymer matrix (PQDs), which are trapped using a focused 1064 nm laser beam. The analysis models the optical forces using T-matrix calculations to account for the increased particle size.

**Key observables.** Positional fluctuations ($\Delta x, \Delta y < 20 	ext{ nm}$), single-photon antibunching ($g^{(2)}(0)$), intrinsic emission spectra, and trapping potential depth relative to thermal energy ($k_B T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Effective particle diameter ($\sim 100 	ext{ nm}$), trapping laser wavelength (1064 nm), and the comparison between trapping potential depth and thermal energy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the particle motion can be approximated by fluctuations near an approximately harmonic potential, and the polymer encapsulation successfully isolates the quantum emitter from trapping-induced damage.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the custom micro-PL platform setup, reconstructed focal fields, calculated trapping potential energy landscapes (axial/radial), positional deviation histograms, and measurements confirming persistent antibunching ($g^{(2)}(0)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the trade-off between stability and quantum integrity, proposing polymer encapsulation as a solution, calculating the enhanced trapping potential using advanced force models, and finally validating the method by measuring both stable confinement (trajectories) and quantum emission (photon correlation).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

While optical manipulation of atomic arrays has reached a high degree of precision and scalability, the stable optical confinement of solution-based artificial atoms like colloidal quantum dots (CQDs) remains hindered by weak trapping forces and thermal fluctuations. High-intensity trapping often compromises the quantum properties of these emitters, creating a significant trade-off between mechanical stability and optical integrity. To overcome this, we propose encapsulating CQDs within a transparent polymer matrix, thereby increasing the effective interaction volume and optical restoring force without altering the emitters themselves. This strategy allows for stable spatial confinement under standard experimental conditions, as evidenced by the direct resolution of positional fluctuations through photoluminescence imaging and trajectory tracking. With averaged position fluctuations below 20 nm, the intrinsic emission spectra and photoluminescence decay dynamics remain largely unaffected, and photon-correlation measurements confirm the full preservation of single-photon emission. These findings establish a robust method for the controlled confinement of colloidal quantum emitters and in-situ quantum-optical measurements for future advancements in quantum-optical manipulation of artificial atoms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework using Schwinger-Keldysh effective actions to describe thermal relaxation in systems exhibiting complex spectral features like branch cuts. By extending the model from discrete relaxation modes to continuous spectra, the authors derive transport coefficients and show how these non-hydrodynamic features constrain the system's dynamics. This provides a powerful tool for analyzing non-equilibrium quantum transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous treatment of non-equilibrium dynamics, especially the incorporation of continuous spectral features (branch cuts) into effective actions, is crucial for understanding transport in strongly coupled, non-equilibrium quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper constructs Schwinger-Keldysh effective actions to rigorously incorporate non-hydrodynamic poles and branch cuts that govern the relaxation dynamics of thermal systems.

**Main result.** The theory successfully models the transition from discrete relaxation spectra to continuous branch cuts, providing closed-form expressions for transport coefficients like conductivity.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism, utilizing Gaussian effective actions and analyzing the resulting spectral properties via complex frequency continuation.

**Model / system.** The system models a thermal medium undergoing relaxation of conserved densities, extended from a simple U(1) current to include additional vector fields (Maxwell-Cattaneo type extension).

**Key observables.** Retarded thermal correlators, optical conductivity ($\sigma(\omega, \vec{k})$), and the structure of the effective action $S_{MC}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relaxation time density ($	au$), complex frequency ($\omega$), and momentum ($\vec{k}$); the analysis distinguishes between hydrodynamic and non-hydrodynamic poles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the Gaussian order of the effective action, and approximations like the energy-diagonal Relaxation-Time Approximation (RTA) are used for microscopic realization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of branch cuts parameterized by relaxation time ($	au$) and show the derived transport coefficients for transverse and longitudinal responses.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first setting up the SK action for relaxation fields, then generalizing the spectrum from discrete poles to a continuous density ($	au$), and finally deriving the transport coefficients and the effective action using techniques like the Schur complement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Retarded thermal correlators, analytically continued to complex frequency, exhibit non-hydrodynamic poles and branch cuts that constrain hydrodynamics. We construct Gaussian Schwinger-Keldysh effective actions in which branch cuts arise from continua of relaxation poles and response functions are governed by Stieltjes transforms of relaxation-time densities. Dynamical KMS symmetry, positivity and stability constrain the spectra, while endpoint behaviour controls the local branch structure and near-edge pole scaling. The illustrative examples of uniform and power-law densities exhibit respectively beyond-all-orders pole-endpoint separation and a continuous interpolation between pole-pole and pole-branch-point termination. Finally, we show that an energy-dependent relaxation-time approximation in kinetic theory provides a microscopic realization of our branch cuts at zero momentum.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous criterion for determining when a quantum process between preparation and post-selection can be described by a 'classical' state via quantum state smoothing. It shows that this classicality is guaranteed if and only if the forbidden subspace respects the initial state's symmetries. Violations of this symmetry lead to measurable contextuality, quantified by the negativity of the smoothed effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical link between fundamental concepts like contextuality, quantum measurement theory, and the mathematical structure of quantum states, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions under which a quantum system undergoing state smoothing between preparation and post-selection admits a 'classical' state, linking this to the concept of contextuality.

**Main result.** The admissibility of the smoothed state (classicality) is equivalent to the subspace forbidden by the post-selection being invariant under the initial state's action. Failure of this invariance certifies contextuality.

**Method.** The analysis uses the positivity of the smoothed state $\Phi_s(E, ho)$ parameterized by $s$, comparing it to necessary conditions derived from the invariance of the kernel of the post-selection effect $E$.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum state smoothing, characterized by the interpolation between the Kirkwood–Dirac operator and its adjoint, applied to an initial state $ho$ and a post-selection effect $E$.

**Key observables.** Positivity of $\Phi_s(E, ho)$, the dimension parity of forbidden subspaces (even for Haldane, odd for trivial), and the magnitude of negative eigenvalues of $\Phi_0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The smoothing parameter $s \in [0, 1]$, the critical point $\lambda^* = \pi/4$, and the structure of the symmetry group $G$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the modular flow formalism and specific bounds derived from noncontextual models, though the converse of some theorems are noted as false.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates quantum smoothing, the condition for admissibility, and the parity of dimensions. Figure 2 shows a first-order transition of the record's class mixing two sectors at a critical point.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the necessary and sufficient condition for positivity ($\Phi_s \ge 0$), linking this to $ho$-invariance of $	ext{ker } E$. It then quantifies contextuality via negative eigenvalues of $\Phi_0$ and discusses the topological implications of the resulting class structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A system between a preparation and a post-selection has had no agreed state since 1964. With positivity as the criterion, a post-selection admits an interval of orderings around the symmetric one exactly when the subspace it forbids is spanned by eigenvectors of a full-rank filtered state. Otherwise it certifies contextuality, testable on a qubit. The averaged filtered state carries the entanglement spectrum of the record, so the smallest forbidden subspaces a symmetry allows are even-dimensional in the Haldane class and odd in the trivial one. That parity is the record's topological class.

</details>
