**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum-Harbor, a virtual laboratory, and QIQCBench, a comprehensive benchmark to test AI agents in quantum engineering. It moves beyond simple testing by requiring agents to prove their results by recomputing them from raw data. The work highlights that while agents can show capability, achieving reliable, verified autonomy in complex quantum workflows remains a significant challenge.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, rigorous testing ground for AI agents interacting with quantum hardware simulations, which is crucial for advancing practical quantum computation beyond theoretical models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is systematically evaluating the reliable, end-to-end performance of scientific AI agents in complex, multi-stage quantum engineering workflows.

**Main result.** The benchmark reveals a substantial gap between an agent's demonstrated capability and its ability to achieve reliable, verified operation in real-world quantum tasks.

**Method.** The authors developed Quantum-Harbor, a virtual lab, and QIQCBench, a benchmark of 49 expert tasks, to rigorously test agents' ability to plan, execute, and verify results from raw data.

**Model / system.** The system involves simulating various quantum platforms (transmon, trapped ion, etc.) within the Quantum-Harbor framework. Agents interact with this environment to perform tasks like logical CNOT gate operations and surrogate model fitting.

**Key observables.** Per-cycle error rates (e.g., $	ext{error} < 10^{-3}$), Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) for model prediction, and overall task pass/fail rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The benchmark covers multiple layers of the quantum stack, including calibration, error correction, and compilation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The evaluation relies on a 'sidecar' component and an evidence-bound grader to ensure results are verifiable against the raw execution log, preventing agents from cheating.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the agent workflow (plan $	o$ run $	o$ analyze $	o$ adapt), the architecture of Quantum-Harbor (agent environment, sidecar, evidence log), and the structure of QIQCBench across various quantum computing layers.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the necessity of autonomous quantum engineering, details the Quantum-Harbor framework and QIQCBench benchmark, presents results showing performance gaps, and establishes the framework for measuring verified autonomy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reliable quantum engineering is essential for turning quantum phenomena into practical technologies. As quantum platforms grow in scale and complexity, their characterization and operation require increasing human effort and coordination. Scientific artificial intelligence agents, which can plan experiments, operate instruments, and analyze observations, offer a promising route towards autonomous quantum engineering. Yet whether current agents can perform reliably in this setting has not been systematically established. To fill this gap, we developed Quantum-Harbor, a virtual laboratory that provides a controlled execution environment for agents to interact with quantum systems. This design enables direct verification of both the actions taken and the conclusions drawn. Building on this framework, we introduce QIQCBench, a benchmark of $49$ expert-authored tasks spanning multiple layers including calibration and control, error correction and compilation, sensing and networking. Across $17$ frontier agentic systems, QIQCBench reveals wide variation in verified performance. These results expose a substantial gap between demonstrating capability and achieving reliable operation, and establish Quantum-Harbor as a foundation for measuring progress towards verified autonomy in quantum engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an exact, nonperturbative method to calculate the ionization survival amplitude for a particle in a delta-function well under a strong DC electric field. By reformulating the problem using the Kramers-Henneberger representation, the authors derive the result analytically in closed form, avoiding approximations common in this field. This provides a fundamental analytical tool for studying driven quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, exact analytical technique for calculating non-perturbative ionization dynamics in driven quantum systems, which is highly relevant to understanding tunneling and field ionization in atomic and molecular physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the exact physical bound-state survival amplitude for ionization from a delta-function well subjected to an arbitrary-strength DC electric field at finite time.

**Main result.** The authors derive an exact, nonperturbative solution for the survival amplitude, expressed in closed form using the Faddeeva function and explicitly evaluable time integrals.

**Method.** They employ an amplitude-centered strategy within the Kramers-Henneberger representation, which reduces the complex dynamics to a closed Volterra equation, bypassing the need to construct the full time-dependent propagator.

**Model / system.** The system is a particle confined by a one-dimensional attractive delta-function potential and driven by a uniform DC electric field. The Hamiltonian is analyzed in both scalar and vector potential gauges.

**Key observables.** Bound-state survival amplitude, $a_b(t)$, and the ionization probability, $P_{ion}(t) = 1 - |a_b(t)|^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Arbitrary DC electric field strength ($F_0$) and finite observation time ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is designed to be exact, avoiding weak-field expansions, rescattering truncations, or asymptotic-time approximations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts the intractable conventional propagator-first approach with the novel amplitude-centered strategy. It derives the solution by factorizing the rescattering history into a convolution hierarchy, yielding closed-form expressions for the contact-free and contact contributions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate finite-time field-induced ionization from a one-dimensional attractive delta-function well in a uniform dc electric field of arbitrary strength. Our aim is to determine the physical bound-state survival amplitude \(a_b(t)\) at any observation time without constructing the complete time-dependent propagator or wavefunction. In the gauge-equivalent Kramers--Henneberger representation, the field appears as motion of the contact point, and the resulting dynamics reduces to a closed Volterra equation. Exact endpoint-phase factorization organizes the full chronological rescattering history into a relative-time convolution hierarchy and an exact resolvent. By introducing the accumulated bound-state amplitude and reorganizing its two-time domain in relative and complementary center times, the final contact contribution becomes an explicit boundary integral. All spatial integrations are carried out analytically. The field-driven contact-free term is obtained in closed form using the Faddeeva function, while the direct and repeated-rescattering terms are expressed as explicitly evaluable time integrals. The result applies at arbitrary dc-field strength and finite time, with no weak-field expansion, rescattering truncation, or asymptotic-time approximation. It is verified through the exact field-free limit and an independent numerical solution of the original physical Volterra equation. The formulation gives an exact nonperturbative solution of a fundamental ionization model and reveals otherwise hidden analytical structure in driven quantum dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced, integrated digital architecture for NV-diamond magnetometers designed for stable operation in unshielded environments. By employing differential readout, second-derivative tracking, and a magnetic eigenvector transformation, the system successfully suppresses both high-frequency laser noise and slow thermal drifts. The results confirm that the magnetometer achieves near-fundamental shot-noise limits while maintaining exceptional long-term stability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant engineering achievement in quantum sensing, providing robust methods to mitigate environmental noise sources (laser noise, thermal drift) that typically limit the practical deployment of quantum sensors, which is highly relevant to AMO physics applications.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving stable, drift-free, and high-sensitivity quantum magnetometry using NV-diamond magnetometers in unshielded, long-term, real-world environments.

**Main result.** The proposed integrated digital architecture achieves an off-resonance noise factor matching the fundamental limit (1.0 $\pm$ 0.1) and demonstrates a ten-fold suppression of long-term drift.

**Method.** The authors combine a dynamic differential readout for laser noise suppression, second-derivative Lorentzian tracking for gain correction, and a magnetic eigenvector transformation to eliminate slow, common-mode thermal drifts.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes ensemble nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond for three-axis magnetic field sensing at room temperature. The measurement relies on tracking multiple Zeeman-split ODMR transitions to isolate the external magnetic signal from environmental drifts.

**Key observables.** Off-resonance noise factor, three magnetic projections ($B_1, B_2, B_3$), and the residual noise floor over long time scales.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Off-resonance noise factor of $1.0 \pm 0.1$; ten-fold suppression of long-term drift; ambient unshielded conditions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Conventional stabilization techniques are insufficient for long-term, unshielded operation, necessitating intrinsic resonance tracking for drift correction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup schematic for differential optical readout, the use of six simultaneously addressed ODMR transitions in three Zeeman-split pairs, and noise density plots comparing various filtering techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the limitations of existing magnetometers, introduces the novel digital architecture (differential readout, second-derivative tracking, eigenvector transformation), presents the experimental results showing noise floor matching and drift suppression, and concludes with the feasibility of field-ready quantum magnetometry.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ensemble nitrogen-vacancy (NV) diamond magnetometers combine high sensitivity with vector-field reconstruction, but practical deployment is limited by errors arising from high-frequency laser noise during short-duration operations and slow-varying gain fluctuations and offset drift during long-term operation. Here, we present an integrated digital architecture for achieving NV magnetometry stability across distinct timescales. A dynamic differential readout continuously balances fluorescence and reference channels to suppress correlated laser noise. Second-derivative Lorentzian lineshape tracking enables in-situ correction of slope variations arising from slow changes in gain and optical excitation. We further identify temperature-induced bias-magnet fluctuations as a dominant source of long-term drift and introduce a magnetic eigenvector transformation that uses the intrinsic response of NV resonances to eliminate these variations. We show, under unshielded ambient conditions, that this architecture achieves an off-resonance noise factor of 1.0 $\pm$ 0.1, matching the fundamental limit with ten-fold suppression of long-term drift, enabling stable, field-ready quantum magnetometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies unsupervised machine learning (PCA and VAE) to analyze the active-absorbing phase transition of the Contact Process. The authors overcome technical hurdles—such as the positive-definite nature of the data—by modifying the ML inputs (e.g., sign-reversing data augmentation or using adaptive priors). This demonstrates that successful ML analysis of complex physical transitions requires deep incorporation of the system's underlying physical symmetries and constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a blueprint for applying advanced ML techniques to non-equilibrium statistical physics problems where standard equilibrium assumptions break down, offering insights into characterizing critical phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Characterizing the active-absorbing phase transition in the Contact Process using unsupervised machine learning methods.

**Main result.** The success of ML methods in phase transitions critically depends on tailoring the representation to respect the system's physical constraints, such as data augmentation for PCA or using parameter-dependent priors for VAEs.

**Method.** The study employs Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) to analyze the system's configuration space.

**Model / system.** The Contact Process (CP) is studied on lattices (1D, 2D, triangular), which exhibits a continuous active-absorbing phase transition belonging to the Directed Percolation universality class.

**Key observables.** Order parameter ($ho$), finite-size scaling ratios (e.g., $\Lambda_2/\Lambda_1$), and reconstruction errors ($\mathcal{L}_{	ext{MSE}}$, $\mathcal{L}_{	ext{BCE}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control parameter $\lambda$ (infection rate relative to recovery rate) and system size $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard ML techniques fail due to the positive-definite nature of CP configurations, necessitating data augmentation (sign-reversal) or specialized priors (heteroscedastic Gaussian prior).

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate the finite-size scaling collapse of various observables (eigenvalue ratios, order parameter estimates, reconstruction errors) for both PCA and VAE analyses across the critical point.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem (CP transition) and the failure modes of standard ML techniques. It then details the necessary methodological fixes (data augmentation for PCA, heteroscedastic prior for VAE) and demonstrates their success by showing the recovery of known critical scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how unsupervised machine-learning methods can characterize the active-absorbing phase transition in the contact process in one and two spatial dimensions. Our analysis focuses on principal component analysis (PCA) and variational autoencoders (VAEs), and we show that direct applications of these methods encounter a central difficulty: contact process configurations are intrinsically positive-definite. In the PCA case, standard centering procedures fail to isolate the order parameter in the leading component. To address this, we augment the data with sign-reversed configurations, creating a balanced dataset with zero mean while preserving the density information required to describe the transition. This preprocessing allows the dominant principal component to recover the order parameter and reproduce the expected finite-size scaling near criticality. For VAEs, we find a related limitation: a fixed Gaussian prior does not provide sufficient latent regularization across the full range of control parameters and system sizes for positive-definite data. We overcome this by adopting a heteroscedastic Gaussian prior that adapts to the control parameter, leading to a substantial improvement in the finite-size scaling of reconstruction-based observables near the critical threshold. Taken together, these results highlight a key lesson: the success of machine learning in phase-transition problems does not rely on algorithmic complexity alone, but on tailoring the representation to respect the physical structure of the system, including its intrinsic constraints and symmetries.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a rigorous analysis procedure for extracting the true source noise spectrum from measurements taken using large ensembles of quantum sensors. By mathematically incorporating the spatial distribution of the sensors, the authors overcome ambiguities inherent in ensemble averaging. This methodology paves the way for quantitative, broadband noise characterization using solid-state quantum platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics as it deals with using quantum systems (NV centers) as highly sensitive probes to characterize the local electromagnetic environment (noise spectroscopy), a core topic in quantum sensing and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately reconstructing the true source noise spectrum $S_0(\omega)$ from ensemble coherence measurements $\langle C(T) angle$ when the sensing ensemble has a variable coupling depth distribution.

**Main result.** The authors present an analysis procedure that incorporates the coupling distribution $P(d)$ to accurately extract the source noise spectrum, demonstrating its applicability to NV centers in diamond.

**Method.** The method involves analyzing the ensemble coherence decay, which is modeled as an integral over the depth distribution, and using approximations (like the short-T limit) to invert the measured decay into the underlying noise spectrum.

**Model / system.** The system studied is quantum sensing ensembles, specifically using Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond. The noise environment is modeled as separable, $S(\omega, d) = S_0(\omega)D(d)$, where $D(d)$ describes depth variation.

**Key observables.** Ensemble coherence $\langle C(T) angle$, the source noise spectrum $S_0(\omega)$, and the depth distribution $P(d)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Measurement time $T$, depth distribution parameters (e.g., depth range, width), and noise frequency $\omega$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include modeling the noise as separable across depth and assuming specific functional forms for the depth distribution (e.g., $d^{-3}$ scaling). A major limitation is the inherent ambiguity between the amplitude of $S_0$ and the scale of $P(d)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the accuracy of fitting methods for depth profiles and source spectra, comparing 'naive' fitting against reconstruction methods that account for the depth distribution. Specific comparisons are made between 2 keV and 4 keV ensembles.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework for ensemble averaging, analyzes the ambiguities in parameter extraction, and then applies this procedure to model systems (like NV centers) to demonstrate the quantitative extraction of broadband noise spectra.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Noise spectroscopy with quantum sensors is a powerful tool for characterising the properties of the sensor environment and detecting critical magnetic phenomena. While replacing single sensors with large ensembles promises substantial gains in measurement sensitivity and field of view, the output becomes difficult to interpret when there is a variable coupling to targets across the sensing ensemble. In this work we present an analysis procedure for accessing the true source noise spectrum using ensemble coherence from dynamical decoupling measurements. We show that sequence timing plays a dual role in ensemble noise spectroscopy, setting both the frequency selectivity and the weighted collection of signal across the ensemble, and demonstrate how incorporation of the coupling distribution allows accurate parameter extraction of the noise. We apply this procedure to a model system, shallow nitrogen-vacancy centres in diamond, and show that consistent information about the spectral character of MHz-range surface noise can be obtained from two sensing ensembles featuring distinct depth distributions. Our results pave the way for using large quantum sensor ensembles for quantitative, broadband noise spectroscopy, with straightforward extension to other solid-state and molecular sensing platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops an edge-KPZ theory to explain the anomalous scaling of the first passage time in neutral population evolution. By combining coalescent theory with the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang equation, the authors show that the time to reach a distant phenotype scales anomalously with mutation variance. This work provides a deep link between population genetics and non-equilibrium statistical physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between population genetics (Wright-Fisher model) and non-equilibrium statistical physics tools like KPZ theory provides a powerful, universal framework for understanding how collective fluctuations dictate macroscopic evolutionary rates.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the anomalous scaling of the first passage time (FPT) in evolutionary dynamics, specifically how the time required for a phenotype to reach a distant state is determined by mutation and population fluctuations.

**Main result.** Neutral Wright-Fisher evolution exhibits an anomalous FPT scaling proportional to $	ilde{L}^3$ in an intermediate regime, which is described by an edge-KPZ theory.

**Method.** The authors combine coalescent theory with the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) equation, analyzing the evolution of the population density via stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) and using Cole-Hopf transformations.

**Model / system.** The model is neutral Wright-Fisher evolution, where phenotypic change is driven by Gaussian mutations ($\sigma^2$) and population dynamics are governed by density evolution equations. The analysis focuses on the separation distribution of two lineages over time.

**Key observables.** Mean first-passage time ($\langle T_L angle$), population density $ho(x, t)$, and the separation probability $p_{	ext{pair}}(S)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mutation variance ($\sigma^2$), population size ($N$), and the target phenotypic distance ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the diffusion approximation for smooth densities and uses scaling limits ($	ilde{t} = t/N$) to derive continuum equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the anomalous scaling of $\langle T_L angle$ vs. $\sigma^2$ (showing $\propto (\sigma^2)^{-3/2}$) and confirm the scaling collapse of FPT data onto a master curve.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem (FPT in evolution) to deriving the continuum SPDE for density evolution, linking this to coalescence theory, and finally applying the KPZ framework to establish the three distinct scaling regimes ($	ilde{L}^2 	o 	ilde{L}^3 	o 	ilde{L}^2$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The pace of evolution depends on how rapidly new phenotypes arise. We show that neutral Wright-Fisher evolution exhibits anomalous first passage despite diffusive mutations. The mean time for the first individual to reach a prescribed phenotypic distance scales approximately as $(σ^2)^{-3/2}$ with mutation variance $σ^2$. Two crossovers bound this regime, with inverse-variance scaling on either side. Combining coalescent theory with Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) fluctuations at the dilute population edge, we develop an edge-KPZ theory of all three regimes. The anomaly persists under weak selection.

</details>

**Summary.** This work classifies quantum computation capabilities based on how a processor interacts with a thermal bath while monitoring its environment. It establishes a critical parameter ($\chi$) that dictates whether the system is classically simulable or capable of universal quantum computation. The key finding is that even minimal environmental records can preserve enough quantum information to enable quantum advantage.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative link between the thermodynamics of open quantum systems (thermal decoherence) and the fundamental limits of quantum computation, suggesting that environmental monitoring itself can be a resource.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the computational classification (classically simulable vs. quantum universal) of a stabilizer processor coupled to a monitored thermal bath, based on the environmental records retained by its controller.

**Main result.** Quantum universal computation is possible if the system parameters exceed a critical boundary ($\chi > 0$), even when only retaining minimal environmental records like whether any exchange occurred.

**Method.** The analysis uses exact computational classification based on energy-counting thermal-idle instruments, deriving time-dependent stabilizer margins ($\gamma$) for different readout protocols.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum processor with ideal stabilizer control coupled to independent local Markov baths, undergoing thermal idle periods where energy exchange is monitored.

**Key observables.** Signed stabilizer margins ($\gamma_q, \gamma_{par}, \gamma_{\emptyset}$), the calibration number ($\chi$), and the computational boundary $\chi=0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relaxation time ($T_1$), homogeneous coherence time ($T_2$), and equilibrium excited-state population ($p_e$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes ideal stabilizer control and independent local Markov baths; the analysis compares resource-side records versus the averaged channel.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the computational boundary ($\chi_{\max} \le 0$ vs. $\chi > 0$) and compare the signed stabilizer margins for different readouts (quiet branch vs. terminal parity).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the computational boundary using the ratio $(1-p_e)T_2/T_1$. It then compares the information retained by different readouts (full record, terminal parity, no exchange bit) to show that retaining minimal information can still herald quantum advantage when $\chi > 0$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

At a fixed thermal exposure, the same stabilizer processor can be classically simulable or quantum universal, depending on which environmental records its controller retains. We give an exact computational classification of energy-counting thermal-idle instruments in a quantum processor with ideal stabilizer control and independent local Markov baths. The relaxation time $T_1$, homogeneous coherence time $T_2$, and equilibrium excited-state population $p_e$ determine an exact computational boundary at $(1-p_e)T_2/T_1=1$. If every location lies at or below it, branchwise nonnegative stabilizer decompositions give an explicit efficient classical sampler for the adaptive circuit and its full time-resolved exchange record. Above it at a single repeatedly accessible location, a suitable idle duration and no-exchange conditioning supply distillable ancillas and enable universal quantum computation with polynomial overhead. At finite temperature on the resource side, erasing the record at a sufficiently long, unsplit exposure makes the averaged channel stabilizer measure-and-prepare, and even a terminal parity check then yields only simulable branches. Yet at that same exposure, retaining only the bit recording whether any exchange occurred still heralds distillable ancillas, because a thermal round trip restores the parity after its first exchange has already removed the coherence.

</details>

The red line shows the theoretical prediction 2χαα obtained from Eqs. (51) and (85), and the green line shows 2χαβ (α ̸= β) from Eq. (85). The simulation parame- ters are as follows: System size Lx×Ly = 128×128, ﬁnal time T = 500, mass chipping (retention) parameters λ1 = 0.4, and λ2 = 0.6.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes mass transport on a lattice driven by chiral hopping, which breaks fundamental symmetries. The key finding is that this 'odd diffusion' mechanism alone can generate universal, power-law decay in density correlations, even when the underlying hopping rates are isotropic. This establishes a new theoretical link between chirality, transport coefficients, and long-range spatial correlations in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between microscopic chiral transport rules and macroscopic power-law correlations in non-equilibrium steady states provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding fluctuation phenomena in driven many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how chiral mass-transport processes generate scale-invariant spatial correlations in non-equilibrium steady states, particularly in systems that are macroscopically isotropic.

**Main result.** Odd diffusion provides a mechanism for algebraic decay in two-point density correlations, $C(r) \sim |r|^{-4}$, which is distinct from correlations arising from simple anisotropic hopping.

**Method.** The authors employ exact calculations for steady-state density-density correlations and derive a non-equilibrium analog of the Green-Kubo relation by analyzing the continuum limit of the lattice dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model is a mass-conserving Markov jump process on a square lattice, termed a chiral mass chipping model (MCM). The dynamics are explicitly chiral, breaking time-reversal and mirror symmetries.

**Key observables.** Two-point density-density correlation function $C(r)$, structure factor in the zero-wavenumber limit, and the odd diffusion coefficient $D(	ext{odd})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chirality strength, the odd diffusion coefficient $D(	ext{odd})$, and the system size/boundary conditions (periodic vs. open).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation of the hydrodynamic equation relies on a gradient expansion, and the model assumes mass conservation throughout the process.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the chiral dynamics via an L-shaped path, while Figure 2 shows the relaxation of a localized density profile, indicating transient circulating currents.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the microscopic model dynamics, derives the macroscopic hydrodynamic equation via gradient expansion, calculates the steady-state correlation functions, and analyzes the dual role of odd diffusion on fluctuations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study mass-conserving Markov jump processes on a square lattice, where masses hop with a preferred rotational sense, thus breaking both time-reversal and mirror symmetries. We consider closed systems with both periodic and open (reflecting) boundaries, the latter supporting a steady-state edge current. We show that odd diffusion, arising from the chiral transport, generically provides a mechanism for the emergence of scale-invariant two-point density correlations in nonequilibrium steady states even in the presence of lattice rotation symmetry -- a mechanism that is qualitatively distinct from the well-known mechanism of anisotropic hopping. We exactly calculate the steady-state equal-time density-density correlations, which exhibit an algebraic decay, $C(\mathbf{r}) \sim |\mathbf{r}|^{-4}$ for $|\mathbf{r}| \gg 1$. This algebraic behavior results from the interplay between the off-diagonal components of the diffusion and mobility tensors, demonstrating that chiral transport alone can generate power-law correlations in isotropic driven systems. Remarkably, the structure factor in the zero-wavenumber limit and the amplitude of the power laws depend nontrivially on chirality. While increasing chirality initially suppresses large-scale density fluctuations, the fluctuations beyond a threshold odd-diffusion strength vary nonmonotonically with chirality and develop a cusp singularity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a method to implement Quantum Error Correction in superconducting transmon circuits using fixed, open-loop control pulses instead of traditional syndrome measurement and feedback. By compiling the recovery logic into a single waveform, the authors demonstrate protection against noise for a four-bit code ring, offering a scalable, hardware-efficient path toward fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, hardware-native blueprint for implementing fault-tolerant quantum computation by replacing complex, time-consuming feedback loops with fixed, engineered control pulses.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the overhead associated with traditional Quantum Error Correction (QEC) by developing a method to compile syndrome measurement and recovery into a fixed, open-loop control waveform.

**Main result.** A local QEC rule can be compiled into a fixed, open-loop control cycle for transmon circuits, successfully slowing logical coherence decay and stabilizing logical populations for a four-bit repetition code ring.

**Method.** The authors use spectral compilation, designing a composite waveform consisting of a swap operation and a conditional auxiliary transition to implement the recovery logic without real-time feedback.

**Model / system.** The work is based on interacting transmon circuits, modeled as a four-site ring structure for a four-bit repetition code. The Hamiltonian and evolution are analyzed using time-dependent control pulses.

**Key observables.** Logical coherence ($C_Y$) and logical-one population ($P_{1L}$) decay rates, and the population transfer map for all 16 computational-basis inputs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system uses a fixed two-color scheduling for the ring update, and the cycle time is characterized by a $\mu$s scale.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The residual error is assumed to be dominated by leakage due to spectral crowding, and the analysis focuses on the Z-basis rule, requiring further steps for phase-error correction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical process of fixed open-loop control transferring error records, detail the local compilation waveform, show the two-color pipelined schedule for the ring, and plot the decay of logical coherence and population under noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops models for local recovery subpulses, extends this to a full ring computation using a two-color schedule, and finally analyzes the performance of the resulting fixed waveform in a repeated-cycle error correction scheme.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction commonly relies on syndrome measurement, decoding, and conditional feedback. We numerically show that the conditional spectrum of an interacting transmon circuit can compile a local recovery rule into a fixed open-loop control cycle. In a four-bit repetition-code ring, the resulting input-independent control slows the decay of logical coherence under Pauli-\(X\) noise and stabilizes logical-one population under data relaxation relative to uncorrected references. Its local, bounded-degree architecture provides a hardware-native framework for extending compiled recovery control to larger quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This course material provides a detailed theoretical review of quantum gases, covering the transition to BEC in trapped systems using the Gross-Pitaevskii equation. It emphasizes the unique physics of two-dimensional gases, deriving the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless mechanism and analyzing the resulting quasi long-range order through correlation functions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a comprehensive theoretical treatment of dimensionality effects on superfluidity, which is a cornerstone topic in modern AMO physics, bridging mean-field descriptions with fluctuation-dominated regimes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental physics of Bose-Einstein Condensation (BEC) and superfluidity in quantum gases, with a specific focus on the unique behavior observed in two-dimensional (2D) systems.

**Main result.** It establishes quantitative criteria for BEC based on the density of states and derives the governing Gross-Pitaevskii Equation (GPE) for the condensate wavefunction. Furthermore, it details the quasi long-range order characteristic of 2D superfluids via the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) mechanism.

**Method.** The analysis employs a combination of mean-field theory (GPE derivation), semi-classical approximations for thermodynamic quantities, and linear response theory/Bogoliubov approach for analyzing phase fluctuations.

**Model / system.** The primary system is a weakly interacting Bose gas, modeled in various dimensionalities (3D, 2D, quasi-2D) confined by isotropic power-law traps. The interactions are modeled via an effective contact potential, leading to the GPE.

**Key observables.** Condensate fraction ($N_0/N$), first-order correlation function ($G_1(r)$), critical temperature ($T_c$), and the superfluid order parameter.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality ($D$), trap exponent ($\alpha$), interaction strength ($g$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include the semi-classical limit for thermodynamics and the assumption that density fluctuations are negligible when analyzing phase correlations in 2D.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference figures showing the BEC fraction dependence on temperature for different trap geometries and the decay of the correlation function $G_1(r)$ in 2D systems.

**Paper structure.** The content is structured logically, moving from the general theory of BEC in trapped gases (ideal gas, interactions, GPE) to the specific, advanced topic of 2D systems, culminating in the analysis of phase fluctuations and the BKT transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This course on two-dimensional quantum gases, prepared for the S{ã}o Paulo ICTP-SAIFR IVth School on Light and Cold Atoms, is organized into three lectures. The first introductory lecture addresses Bose-Einstein condensation in an ideal gas, the effect of interactions in the weakly interacting limit, Gross-Pitaevskii equation and its hydrodynamic formulation, as well as superfluidity. The second lecture is devoted to the main features of two-dimensional quantum gases: importance of the interactions, enhanced phase fluctuations and the emergence of a quasi long-range order, superfluidity through the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless mechanism, scaling symmetry. The third lecture has taken the form of a seminar on fast rotating quasi two-dimensional Bose gases and thermal melting of the vortex lattice. The slides accompanying these notes can be found on the school website: https://www.ictp-saifr.org/slca2025/.

</details>

### [Orbital-angular-momentum partition in hydrogen photoionization by a monochromatic vortex beam](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16825v1)

**Authors:** Zhongchen Xing, Chengyin Wu, Zheng Li, Marcelo F. Ciappina  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16825v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `interference shaping light` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

As shown in the later discussion, the photoelectron can carry OAM exceeding the photon’s total angular momentum, while the ion acquires oppositely directed OAM, preserving the total angular momentum of the system.</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes the transfer of orbital angular momentum from a vortex laser beam to a hydrogen atom during photoionization. By developing a center-of-mass-resolved theory, the authors demonstrate that ignoring atomic recoil leads to incorrect predictions for the electron's OAM. They show that the OAM is largely transferred to the recoiling center-of-mass motion, making recoil a key element in understanding this quantum process.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum mechanical treatment of light-matter interaction where recoil effects are non-trivial, which is highly relevant for understanding quantum control and momentum transfer in strong-field atomic physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the orbital angular momentum (OAM) carried by a monochromatic vortex beam is transferred to the matter (hydrogen atom) during photoionization, specifically accounting for the crucial role of atomic recoil and translational motion.

**Main result.** The transfer of OAM is highly dependent on the initial state of the atomic center-of-mass (CM); in the small-transverse-retardation regime, OAM is predominantly transferred to the CM motion (the proton), not solely to the electron.

**Method.** The authors developed a center-of-mass-resolved quantum theory, analyzing the photoionization process using the full Hamiltonian that explicitly includes CM motion, and comparing results across different initial CM state approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is the photoionization of a hydrogen-like atom by a monochromatic Laguerre-Gaussian (vortex) beam. The model uses a Hamiltonian incorporating the free evolution of the atom's CM motion and the light-matter interaction in the velocity gauge.

**Key observables.** Photoelectron angular distribution, OAM content ($\langle L_{e,z} angle$, $\langle L_{p,z} angle$), and the correlation term between electron and proton angular momenta.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of initial CM momentum definition (momentum eigenstate vs. spatially localized wavepacket) and the transverse retardation scale.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the strong-field approximation (SFA) and treats the light-ion interaction as negligible compared to the electron-light interaction.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general Hamiltonian to specific theoretical treatments, contrasting idealized fixed-target models with the full CM-resolved theory to derive the correct OAM partitioning.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how optical orbital angular momentum (OAM) is transferred to matter requires treating recoil and translational motion alongside the internal electronic dynamics. We develop a center-of-mass-resolved theory of one-photon ionization of hydrogen by a monochromatic Laguerre--Gaussian beam and show that the Bessel-vortex photoelectron predicted in fixed-target models is a preparation-dependent limit. For a sharply defined atomic center-of-mass momentum, the recoil records the photon-cone azimuth, and tracing over it generally destroys the coherence required for a pure electron vortex. In the small-transverse-retardation regime, the optical OAM is transferred predominantly to the center-of-mass motion and hence, in the laboratory frame, to the proton. Finite-retardation corrections redistribute angular momentum between center-of-mass and relative motion, while an additional correlation contribution to the electron and proton angular momenta can be tuned through the spatial uncertainty of the atomic center of mass. These results reveal atomic recoil as a key element of OAM transfer in photoionization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy ($	ext{Tr-ARPES}$) to directly map the Berry curvature in a Floquet-Chern insulator on $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Se}_3$. By exploiting the double-helicity dichroism signal, the authors demonstrate that the geometric response is sensitive to the driving field and evolves differently in time compared to the spectral replica intensity. This establishes a powerful experimental technique for probing light-induced topological properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides a direct, momentum-resolved probe of Berry curvature induced by time-periodic driving, which is crucial for understanding light-matter interactions in topological materials and Floquet engineering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to directly measure and map the light-induced Berry curvature in a Floquet-Chern insulator state.

**Main result.** The study successfully mapped the Berry curvature using double-helicity dichroism, showing its systematic evolution with pump strength and distinguishing the driven topological state from static bands.

**Method.** The measurement relies on circular-dichroism time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (Tr-ARPES) by comparing spectra taken with opposite pump helicities to isolate the geometric response.

**Model / system.** The system is the surface of $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Se}_3$, driven by mid-infrared circularly polarized light, which opens a topological gap and creates a Floquet-Chern insulator.

**Key observables.** Double-helicity dichroism signal, Berry curvature distribution ($\Omega$), Floquet-Bloch states, and the time-dependent gap splitting ($\Delta E_{split}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump photon energy ($\hbar\omega$), pump field strength (fluence), and time delay ($\Delta t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The double-helicity dichroism method is assumed to isolate the pump-induced Berry curvature, and the temporal asymmetry is attributed to electronic heating and dephasing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup, 2D curvature maps showing alternating-sign features, fluence dependence of dichroism, and temporal evolution showing strong asymmetry in the Berry curvature versus the replica intensity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for direct Berry curvature measurement to detailing the $	ext{Tr-ARPES}$ methodology, presenting results on periodicity and field dependence, and finally analyzing the time-resolved asymmetry between geometric response and spectral weight.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Circularly polarized light can open a topological gap in a gapless Dirac system, creating a Floquet-Chern insulator. However, its Berry curvature has not been directly resolved. Here we use circular-dichroism time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (Tr-ARPES) to map the light-induced Berry curvature of Floquet-Bloch states on the surface of Bi$_2$Se$_3$. By comparing spectra for opposite helicities of the mid-infrared pump, we isolate the induced geometric response. We observe alternating-sign Berry-curvature features at Floquet gaps that repeat at intervals set by the pump photon energy and redistribute with pump field strength in agreement with calculations for a periodically driven Dirac Hamiltonian. Both the induced Berry-curvature and the Floquet Dirac gap decay faster than the replica intensity, showing that spectral replicas can persist even after coherent Floquet hybridization weakens. These results establish momentum-resolved Berry-curvature mapping as a direct probe of light-induced topology and coherent Floquet band formation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the design and hardware implementation of digital filters necessary to correct signal distortion in superconducting qubit flux control lines. By performing rigorous error-bounded fixed-point analysis, the authors ensure that quantization noise does not destabilize the compensation filters. The resulting super-sample-rate architecture allows for high-fidelity, real-time operation at speeds required for advanced quantum gate operations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial, hardware-aware engineering details for implementing quantum control pulses, directly impacting the fidelity and scalability of superconducting quantum computation platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need to correct line distortion in flux-activated two-qubit gates within superconducting quantum processors to mitigate errors like detuning and leakage.

**Main result.** The authors derive error-bounded fixed-point designs and successfully implement the required IIR/FIR filter cascade on a high-speed FPGA platform using a super-sample-rate structure.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving coefficient formats based on step response error tolerance and performing a detailed analysis of quantization effects on filter stability and pole/zero locations.

**Model / system.** The system is a superconducting quantum processor utilizing transmon qubits whose flux tunability is controlled via bias tees. The signal processing is modeled using linear time-invariant (LTI) systems requiring digital IIR/FIR compensation filters.

**Key observables.** Residual detuning, conditional-phase error, leakage, and the maximum frequency error bound ($\lVert E Vert_\infty$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Clock rate of 500 MHz, super-sample rate structure, DSP slice consumption (88 DSP slices for a representative cascade).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on bounding quantization-induced displacement of poles and zeros, and assumes the flux tuning line can be modeled as an LTI system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate typical qubit flux tuning line configurations and the detailed cascade structure of the required digital filter (Integrator $ightarrow$ FOS/SOS $ightarrow$ FIR).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically moves from identifying the physical distortion problem to deriving the required digital filter structure, analyzing stability and quantization error bounds for specific filter types (Droop, Oscillation), and finally detailing the high-speed hardware implementation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Flux-activated two-qubit gates in superconducting processors require correction of line distortion, which otherwise produces residual detuning, conditional-phase error, and leakage. Prior demonstrations of cryoscope-based distortion calibration have generally used commercial solutions that specify the filter models but not their FPGA implementation. These details matter for custom and open-source systems, where the fixed-point coefficient and datapath formats set how accurately the correction is realized, and the IIR feedback loop in its standard form does not meet timing at the required clock rate. We derive the coefficient formats of the correction filters from a step response error tolerance, using bounds on the quantization-induced displacement of the poles and zeros over the physical parameter range of each distortion, and the accumulator format from the smallest input change that must remain resolvable at the output. The same analysis gives the conditions under which quantization preserves filter stability. We implement the resulting FIR/IIR cascade on the QubiC platform at a fabric clock rate of \SI{500}{MHz}, using a super-sample-rate structure with scattered look-ahead pipelining, which removes the feedback recursion from the critical path without changing the target transfer function. A representative cascade for our \SI{1}{GS/s} flux lines, comprising an integrator, a second-order section, and a 20-tap FIR, consumes 88 DSP slices and adds \SI{162}{ns} of latency. We confirm the hardware implementation by correcting characteristic bias-tee distortion, an intermediate step toward full cryoscope-based calibration.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes random quantum circuits to determine if rare measurement outcomes encode useful information about the circuit's intermediate dynamics. By comparing peak-selected circuits to random ones, the authors demonstrate that these rare events reveal a persistent, quantifiable structural organization. This finding suggests that quantum measurement can guide future quantum operations, offering a new principle for quantum circuit design.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative framework for extracting non-terminal, predictive structure from complex quantum measurements, which is crucial for designing efficient quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether rare, high-probability outputs observed in random quantum circuits contain predictive structural information useful for guiding subsequent quantum evolution.

**Main result.** Rare outputs reveal intermediate physical organization that is useful for guiding circuit construction, showing a measurable 'fresh-future advantage' when comparing peak-selected circuits to random ones.

**Method.** The authors compare statistics from Random-Normal (RN) and Peak-Selected (RP) random quantum circuits, employing structural observables and developing predictive prefix scores.

**Model / system.** The model uses open one-dimensional brickwall quantum circuits generated by Haar-U(4) nearest-neighbor gates, analyzed across varying system sizes and depths.

**Key observables.** Quantum Mutual Information (IQ), Normalized Cut Entanglement (Ec), Normalized Shannon Entropy (eH1), and the peak-amplification functional ($\Gamma_{	ext{peak}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System sizes $n=8-16$ and multiple nominal circuit depths ($\delta=0, 1, 2, 3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the structural separation and fresh-future advantage persist across different nominal depths, even if the terminal unitary distribution is fixed.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the persistence of the 'fresh-future function' across added depth settings, showing quantitative ratios (RP/RN) and phase interaction effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the problem (rare outcomes), developing comparative metrics (RN vs RP), quantifying structural advantages (prefix scores, phase scrambling), and concluding with applications to circuit construction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rare outputs of random quantum circuits are usually treated as terminal statistics. We ask whether they reveal intermediate organization that remains useful beyond the selecting future. Comparing unconditioned random-normal (RN) and peak-selected random-peaked (RP) circuits from the same local generator, we find stronger pairwise correlations and probability redistribution but reduced bipartite and output spreading in RP across n=8-16. Exact full-suffix decomposition reveals stronger peak-directed interference. The structural separation and fresh-future advantage persist across four nominal depths at n=8-14, with 2.01-7.15 fold RP enrichment in the depth extensions at fixed, independently calibrated thresholds. Within the unconditioned ensemble, a structural prefix score predicts fresh-event probability without using the evaluated circuits' terminal outcomes. Population-preserving phase scrambling identifies a functional contribution from relative-phase organization across the tested settings, including primary-depth held-out confirmation. RP trajectory-guided initialization improves high-peak yield over Haar initialization under common local refinement. Intermediate multivariate guidance also improves the fresh-continuation susceptibility of constructed states beyond peak-only guidance across the same size-depth grid. Rare outputs thus reveal intermediate physical organization that remains useful under new dynamics and can guide circuit construction.

</details>

### [Strictly Localized Mixed States](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17013v1)

**Authors:** Sigurd Sørlie Rustad, Jan Gulla, Johannes Skaar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17013v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.17013_figures/2609.17013_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1. Sketch in 1 + 1 dimensions, with time increasing upwards, showing a bounded spacetime region D, its causal complement D′, and its causal future and past, denoted by J(D).</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper deeply explores strictly localized mixed states in QFT, defining them as states indistinguishable from the vacuum outside a given region. It distinguishes between states resulting from standard non-selective measurements (which are mixtures of pure states) and more general states from unsharp measurements. The key finding is that some strictly localized mixed states defy such a simple decomposition, providing deep insights into the nature of quantum correlations and measurement theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum foundations, as it rigorously defines the boundaries between classical ignorance (mixtures of pure states) and genuine quantum correlations in localized systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mathematical and physical properties of strictly localized mixed states in quantum field theory (QFT). It specifically contrasts states arising from non-selective measurements versus those from unsharp measurements.

**Main result.** While non-selective measurements yield states decomposable into mixtures of pure states, the authors prove the existence of strictly localized mixed states that cannot be written as such mixtures, and they extend Knight's theorem to show these states must have infinite particle number support.

**Method.** The analysis uses the mathematical framework of local algebras $A(O)$ in QFT, employing concepts like Licht maps, Kraus operators, and extensions of particle number counting arguments.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum field theory, modeled using local algebras associated with spacetime regions $O$. The analysis uses analogies to two-qubit systems and focuses on bosonic quantum fields.

**Key observables.** Strict localization condition ($	ext{tr}(Aho) = 	ext{tr}(A\Omega)$ for $A$ outside the region), particle number support, and the structure of the density matrix decomposition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The localization region $O$, the type of measurement (sharp vs. unsharp), and the particle number $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the structure of local algebras and the ability to define non-selective operations (Licht maps).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches spacetime regions ($D$, $D'$, $J(D)$) in $1+1$ dimensions, illustrating the regions relevant to localization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing strict localization, analyzing the non-selective state from local measurements (showing it decomposes into pure states), then constructing counterexamples using unsharp measurements, and finally extending Knight's theorem to mixed states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study strictly localized mixed states in quantum field theory: states that are indistinguishable from vacuum outside some spacetime region. Such states are generated from the vacuum by so-called Licht maps. While the states associated with individual outcomes of local, projective measurements on the vacuum are not strictly localized, the corresponding non-selective states are strictly localized and even admit decompositions into strictly localized pure states. Remarkably, however, there are strictly localized mixed states that cannot be written as mixtures of pure states strictly localized to the same region. Such states can arise from unsharp local measurements. Finally, we extend Knight's theorem to mixed states, showing that strictly localized bosonic mixed states contain terms with arbitrarily high particle numbers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel method to achieve Thouless pumping, a topological transport effect, by replacing the requirement for slow, continuous Hamiltonian variation with discrete parameter jumping. By applying the Hamiltonian only at specific points, the authors successfully realize adiabatic evolution, confirming quantized particle transport while bypassing significant experimental control challenges.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a pathway to implement complex topological quantum phenomena using fast, discrete control pulses, circumventing the stringent experimental limitations associated with slow, continuous Hamiltonian modulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of realizing Thouless pumping, a topological effect, without adhering to the conventional requirement of continuously and slowly varying the Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that Thouless pumping can be successfully achieved using only discrete sampling (jumping) of the Hamiltonian parameters, maintaining adiabaticity and achieving quantized particle transport.

**Method.** The authors propose an adiabatic jumping control scheme where the Hamiltonian is applied only at a finite set of discrete parameter points, effectively realizing the required evolution via geodesic paths.

**Model / system.** The study uses the Rice–Mele model to simulate the quantum system undergoing Thouless pumping. The Hamiltonian is analyzed in momentum space, $H_k(\phi) = h_x(k) \sigma_x + h_y(k) \sigma_y + h_z(k) \sigma_z$.

**Key observables.** Quantized particle transport (characterized by the Chern number $C$), the adiabaticity measure $ar{y}(T)/d$, and the mean displacement $\langle \hat{x}(t) angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of sampling points $P$, the pulse time $t_p$, and the comparison between continuous vs. discrete control parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the necessary and sufficient condition for quantum adiabatic evolution, and the nonadiabatic error is shown to vanish as the number of sampling points $P$ increases.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the control parameter evolution ($\phi(t)$) between conventional and jumping methods, and show the resulting quantized mean displacement $\Delta x(T)/d$ and the temporal evolution of the wave function probability $|\psi_m(t)|^2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of continuous control, then details the discrete jumping approach, providing theoretical analysis (Appendix B) on nonadiabatic corrections, and finally presents numerical results comparing the jumping scheme against the standard adiabatic protocol.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thouless pumping is a celebrated topological effect that enables quantized particle transport and holds promise for applications in quantum technologies. However, the standard adiabatic pumping theory dictate that the Hamiltonian must be varied continuously and slowly throughout the whole pumping process. In this work, we show that Thouless pumping can be realized by using only discrete sampling of the Hamiltonian, removing the conventional constraint of continuous and slow change of control. Specifically, we select particular parameter points of the Hamiltonian for conventional continuous quantum adiabatic evolution, and then apply the Hamiltonian at these parameter points which induces a geodesic evolution for the pseudospin with a given quasimomentum. This realizes an adiabatic evolution for the Thouless pumping according to a necessary and sufficient condition for quantum adiabatic evolution.

</details>

### [Truncated hybrid tensor networks for distributed quantum simulation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16520v1)

**Authors:** Yong Liu, Guangyao Huang, Weixu Shi, Yizhi Wang, Jiandong Ouyang, Zeqian Chen, Junjie Wu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16520v1>  
**Analysis basis:** abstract only; PDF text extraction failed or PDF unavailable
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Truncated Hybrid Tensor Network (THTN) framework to enable the simulation of large quantum many-body systems across multiple, physically separated computing units. It overcomes the exponential cost of current distributed protocols by representing remote gates via classical connecting tensors. The method is validated against TEBD, showing improved scalability, particularly for layered physical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it provides a concrete, resource-aware numerical technique (THTN) to simulate large quantum systems that cannot fit on single devices, directly addressing scalability issues in quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simulating quantum many-body systems on quantum computers is limited by qubit count, necessitating methods for distributed simulation across multiple classical systems.

**Main result.** The authors propose a Truncated Hybrid Tensor Network (THTN) framework that efficiently handles distributed quantum evolution by linking subsystems with classical tensors while retaining crucial Schmidt modes.

**Method.** The THTN framework models remote two-body gates as sums of local unitaries connected by a classical tensor, allowing non-one-dimensional models to be mapped onto a 1D cut.

**Model / system.** The work addresses the simulation of quantum many-body systems, specifically validating the protocol against Time-Evolving Block Decimation (TEBD) on chains and ladders.

**Key observables.** Local observables and the tracking of dynamics compared to TEBD references.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension, number of remote gates across the cut.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes that the physical correlations across the cut can be effectively captured by retaining Schmidt modes via interface truncation.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, only the validation comparison against TEBD on chains and ladders, showing the clearest gain on a layered model.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the THTN framework, details how remote gates are decomposed and how non-1D models are mapped, and validates the protocol by comparing its dynamics to established methods like TEBD.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulation of quantum many-body systems is a principal application of quantum computing, but available devices remain limited by the number of qubits and cannot accommodate systems of the desired size. One approach is to host a large evolution across several modest distributed systems that exchange classical information alone. Current protocols such as circuit knitting incur a quasi-probability sampling cost that grows exponentially with the number of remote gates across the cut, even when physical correlations across the cut are actually bounded. In this work, we present a truncated hybrid tensor network (THTN) framework in which a remote two-body gate is applied as a sum of local unitaries on the subsystems, linked by a classical connecting tensor, and an interface truncation retains Schmidt modes across the cut. Remote gates between the same subsystems can be merged on one connector, so non-one-dimensional models, such as layered partitions with strong intra-layer and weaker interlayer couplings, may be cast onto a one-dimensional cut. The retained non-negative Schmidt coefficients also define a sampling rule for local observables. We validate this distributed protocol by classical simulation against time-evolving block decimation (TEBD) at the same bond dimension. The truncated dynamics track the TEBD references on chains and ladders, with the clearest gain on a layered model.

</details>

### [Beyond Hardware: Adaptive Algorithmic Control by State-Proxy Equalization](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17497v1)

**Authors:** Jianlong Lu, Hongrui Zhang, Vishal Sharathchandra Bajpe, Thorsten Koch, Ying Chen  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17497v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<sub>Fig. 1 State-Proxy Equalization and Adaptive Algorithmic Control (A2C). a, A quan- tum state |ψ(s)⟩evolves along a normalized trajectory s ∈[0, 1], where the computational hardness r(s) varies according to the local quantum dynamics. The trajectory is partitioned into p computa- tional intervals {[sℓ−1, sℓ]}p ℓ=1. b, Variational formulation of the optimal computational allocation. Theorem 1 shows that the optimal computational coordinate is obtained by cumulative state-proxy equalization, leading to equal accumulated hardness in every computational interval. c, Geomet- ric interpretation of the State-Proxy Equalization principle. Instead of equalizing physical time, the optimal schedule...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes Adaptive Algorithmic Control (A2C), a software technique that optimizes quantum computation by allocating resources based on the evolving computational difficulty of the state. By proving that optimal performance requires equalizing cumulative computational hardness, A2C significantly boosts sampling probabilities in quantum optimization benchmarks across large qubit counts.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial algorithmic pathway for quantum computation, showing that software control over resource allocation can yield performance gains comparable to, or exceeding, hardware improvements in near-term quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine how to optimally allocate finite computational resources during a quantum computation to maximize performance, moving beyond reliance solely on hardware improvements.

**Main result.** Adaptive Algorithmic Control (A2C) significantly improves low-energy sampling probabilities in quantum optimization problems by concentrating computational effort where the state dynamics are most difficult.

**Method.** The authors introduce A2C, a software paradigm based on the State-Proxy Equalization theorem, which dictates that optimal resource allocation must equalize cumulative computational hardness rather than physical time.

**Model / system.** The work applies to quantum optimization problems, modeled using frameworks like QAOA, involving state evolution over a normalized trajectory $s \in [0, 1]$. The complexity is assessed by inferring a computational hardness profile $r(s)$ from the evolving quantum state.

**Key observables.** Low-energy sampling probabilities, success probability $P_{	ext{succ}}(ho)$, and the derived computational hardness $r(s)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($p$), number of qubits ($N$ up to 156), and the resource allocation schedule (Adaptive vs. Linear Ramp).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the required computational hardness $r(s)$ can be inferred directly from state-accessible dynamical quantities, avoiding explicit reconstruction of the full many-body spectrum.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the core principle: comparing resource allocation that equalizes physical time versus that which equalizes cumulative computational hardness via an 'adaptive warp'.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the A2C theory using the State-Proxy Equalization theorem, applies it to QAOA benchmarks, and rigorously validates the results by comparing success probabilities and calculating statistical confidence intervals for the performance gains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances in quantum computing have been driven primarily by improvements in hardware. Here we show that substantial gains can instead arise from how finite computational resources are allocated throughout a quantum computation. We introduce Adaptive Algorithmic Control (A2C), a software paradigm founded on a State-Proxy Equalization theorem, which proves that the optimal allocation for a state-derived proxy-error functional equalizes cumulative computational hardness rather than physical time. The required computational hardness is inferred directly from the evolving quantum state, avoiding explicit reconstruction of the exponentially large many-body spectrum. Across quantum optimization problems containing up to 156 qubits, combining exact simulations, large-scale supercomputer computations and IBM quantum hardware experiments, A2C improves the low-energy sampling probabilities by $22\%$ to over $100,000\%$ under matched circuit depths and measurement budgets. These results demonstrate that quantum computational performance depends not only on hardware capabilities, but also on how finite computational resources are organized, establishing adaptive algorithmic control as a complementary software pathway for advancing quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the topological robustness of radiative atomic arrays by studying how exceptional points behave under strong magnetic fields. The authors show that the system's Chern topology remains fixed across various deformations, providing a method to engineer topological states beyond expected strong-field limitations. This work is significant for understanding topological control in engineered quantum optical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses topological phenomena in engineered quantum systems (atomic arrays) using concepts from condensed matter theory (Chern numbers, EPs) but is highly relevant to AMO physics due to the use of atomic lattices and light-matter interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how topological defects, specifically exceptional points (EPs), behave in two-dimensional subwavelength atomic arrays when subjected to strong Hermitian magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that the topological invariants (Chern numbers) remain robust even when the system passes through exceptional regions, establishing a mechanism to engineer topology beyond conventional strong-field limits.

**Method.** The authors employed analytical calculations of the Berry curvature and integrated Berry flux, alongside constructing effective models for phase boundaries near lattice deformation limits.

**Model / system.** The system is a radiative atomic array modeled on a square-to-triangular lattice deformation, governed by a polarization-dependent Hamiltonian that includes radiative dipolar couplings.

**Key observables.** Berry Flux ($\pm 1$), Chern Numbers ($C_1=2, C_2=-2$), and the field-dependent localization of boundary modes (skin localization).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice deformation angle ($	heta$), magnetic field strength ($B$), and the critical Zeeman shift ($\mu_B B_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as first-order deformation expansions for boundary analysis and assuming decoupling from free-space modes for outer contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figure S8 illustrates the analytical exceptional-phase boundaries, mapping the critical Zeeman shift against lattice deformation for both the square and triangular lattice limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving the Hamiltonian and calculating the Berry flux for the quadratic continuum model, followed by determining analytical phase boundaries for the SL and TL limits, and finally analyzing the non-monotonic response of skin localization to the magnetic field.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exceptional points are topological defects of complex band structures that are stable against weak perturbations, yet in bounded two-band systems they are ultimately removed by a sufficiently strong Hermitian field. Here we demonstrate a striking exception in a two-dimensional subwavelength atomic array. Along a continuous square-to-triangular deformation at fixed magnetic field, the bulk passes between line-gapped topological regions with band Chern numbers (C_1,C_2)=(2,-2) through a gapless exceptional region. Within a finite interval of lattice deformation, increasing the magnetic field merely drives the exceptional points toward the light cone because of the singular radiative dipolar couplings. We further show that skin localization toward open boundaries responds non-monotonically to the same field. An intermediate field drives a bipolar skin localization, with bulk modes accumulating at opposite edges, whereas stronger fields suppress boundary localization. Our results establish the interplay of lattice geometry, light-cone singularity, and Hermitian magnetic field as a route to engineering Chern and exceptional topology beyond the conventional strong-field limit.

</details>
