**Summary.** This work proposes a practical quantum simulation framework for interacting fermionic models using the locality-preserving Derby-Klassen mapping. By encoding fermionic physics into a constrained qubit subspace defined by stabilizer codes, it significantly reduces operator nonlocality compared to standard methods. This provides a resource-efficient route for simulating complex, higher-dimensional correlated electron systems on quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of locality-preserving mappings directly addresses a major bottleneck in quantum simulation—the exponential growth of Pauli string length—making complex lattice models more tractable on near-term quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of simulating strongly correlated fermionic systems, such as the $t$-$V$ and Fermi-Hubbard models, which suffer from the fermionic sign problem.

**Main result.** The locality-preserving Derby-Klassen (DK) mapping provides a resource-efficient framework for quantum simulation by reducing operator nonlocality compared to standard Jordan-Wigner transformations, especially in higher dimensions.

**Method.** The authors employ the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) combined with the DK mapping, which encodes fermionic operators into a constrained subspace of a larger qubit Hilbert space defined by stabilizer constraints.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two-dimensional interacting fermionic models, specifically the $t$-$V$ and spinful Fermi-Hubbard models, analyzed on lattices with periodic boundary conditions.

**Key observables.** Low-energy properties, energy spectra, and the ability to resolve distinct symmetry sectors via particle-number conservation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-zero chemical potential ($\mu$), coupling constant ($V$), and lattice size ($L_x 	imes L_y$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the physical fermionic Hilbert space can be accurately represented as the $+1$ eigenspace of local stabilizer operators derived from the DK mapping.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate lattice structures, the checkerboard arrangement of face qubits forming a toric code, and the corresponding Clifford circuits used to diagonalize stabilizer operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the DK mapping and its locality-preserving nature, then details the construction of the Hamiltonian Variational Ansatz (HVA) using stabilizer constraints. It benchmarks this against JW circuits and extends the framework to the spinful Hubbard model, concluding with a discussion of the practical overhead trade-offs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the utility of the locality-preserving Derby-Klassen (DK) fermion-to-qubit mapping [arXiv:2003.06939] for variational quantum simulation of two-dimensional $t$-$V$ and Fermi-Hubbard models. The DK mapping preserves the locality of fermionic interactions with an enlarged Hilbert space, thereby requiring additional constraints that define the physical sector. We incorporate these constraints directly into a Hamiltonian Variational Ansätz (HVA) through Clifford-gate state preparation and use the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) to show that the low-energy properties of the resulting qubit Hamiltonian are accurately reproduced. We further exploit particle-number conservation inherent in the ansätz to resolve distinct symmetry sectors and reliably access degenerate states. Consequently, we benchmark the DK-HVA against Jordan-Wigner-based variational circuits at nonzero chemical potential, where particle-hole symmetry and the associated half-filled sign-free condition are absent. Finally, we demonstrate the advantage of locality-preserving mappings in higher-dimensional fermionic systems, where the conventional Jordan-Wigner (JW) transformation generates increasingly long Pauli strings and corresponding circuit overheads. We further extend the framework to the spinful Fermi-Hubbard model and identify a tradeoff between fermionic-mode placement and the locality of hopping and on-site interaction terms. These results establish a practical framework combining locality-preserving fermion-to-qubit mappings, constraint-preserving Clifford state preparation, and symmetry-preserving variational circuits, that trades auxiliary-qubit and stabilizer-preparation overhead for reduced operator nonlocality, providing a practical route toward resource-efficient quantum simulation of higher-dimensional interacting fermionic systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analytically demonstrates a quantum field theory exhibiting spontaneous parity and time-reversal symmetry breaking that persists even at arbitrarily high temperatures. Using the large-N expansion in 2+1 dimensions, the authors analyze the thermal phase transition, showing that the symmetry breaking emerges upon heating rather than being present at zero temperature. This provides a controlled theoretical framework for studying non-trivial thermal phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of symmetry breaking persistence at high temperatures is a core topic in statistical mechanics, particularly relevant for understanding high-energy phase transitions in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide an analytic realization of spontaneous parity and time-reversal breaking (PSSB) in a quantum field theory without the symmetry breaking being restored by finite temperature.

**Main result.** The theory exhibits a persistent spontaneous breaking of parity and time-reversal symmetry above a critical temperature, surviving to arbitrarily high temperatures.

**Method.** The analysis uses the large-N expansion, finite-temperature effective potential calculations, and renormalization group flow techniques to study the phase structure.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum field theory in 2+1 dimensions, defined by a Yukawa deformation coupling a critical biconical vector model to free massless Dirac fermions.

**Key observables.** The thermal expectation value of the field $\langle\chiangle_T$, and the persistence of a fermion mass odd under parity and time reversal.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical temperature ($T_{crit}$), the large-$N$ limit, and the coupling constants governing the Yukawa interaction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-N expansion and assumes the system is away from narrow parametric windows where finite-N corrections dominate.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the thermal expectation value $\langle\chiangle_T / T$ versus $T/y^2$, marking the critical temperature $T_{crit}$ above which symmetry is spontaneously broken.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the model, analyzing the RG flow from a UV CFT to an IR fixed point, and then performing a detailed finite-temperature analysis using the large-N saddle-point approximation to determine the phase transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We provide an analytic realization of spontaneous parity and time-reversal breaking without thermal restoration in a local, unitary, ultraviolet-complete quantum field theory in $2+1$ dimensions with finitely many fields. The theory is defined by a relevant Yukawa deformation of a product of the critical biconical vector model and free massless Dirac fermions. This deformation induces a renormalization group flow whose infrared endpoint is a decoupled product of the critical vector model and the conformal Gross-Neveu-Yukawa model. At zero temperature, the vacuum preserves both symmetries, whereas upon heating, the theory undergoes an inverse thermal transition at a finite critical temperature. Above this temperature, a fermion mass, odd under parity and time reversal, is dynamically generated, and the broken phase persists to arbitrarily high temperatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces $k$-learnability as a generalized measure for partial quantum state identification, interpolating between full distinguishability and anti-distinguishability. It derives optimal Gram-matrix criteria that provide necessary and sufficient conditions for a set of states to be $k$-learnable. The results are highly applicable to understanding the resource requirements for quantum state characterization in various physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify how 'hard' it is to distinguish or partially identify quantum states, which is fundamental for quantum state tomography and quantum error correction protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a unified framework to quantify the partial identification of a multiset of pure quantum states, parameterized by $k$-learnability.

**Main result.** It establishes universal, optimal Gram-matrix criteria (sufficient and necessary conditions) for $k$-learnability, providing explicit guarantees for known state sets like SIC-POVMs and stabilizer states.

**Method.** The analysis relies on deriving necessary and sufficient conditions based on the Gram matrix of the state set, relating $k$-learnability to concepts like $k$-incoherence and frame potentials.

**Model / system.** The study concerns a general multiset of pure quantum states $\{|\psi_1angle, \dots, |\psi_nangle\}$ in a quantum system, often analyzed in the context of qudit systems or stabilizer states.

**Key observables.** Gram matrix elements $\langle\psi_i|\psi_jangle$, the learning parameter $k$, and the copy complexity $c_2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of states $n$, the dimension $d$, and the learning parameter $k$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are derived under the assumption of pure quantum states and utilize advanced mathematical tools like frame potentials and properties of convex cones.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining $k$-learnability, deriving general bounds using the Gram matrix (Theorem 2 & 3), applying these bounds to specific state types (e.g., equiangular states), and finally analyzing the copy complexity for stabilizer states using recurrence relations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A multiset of pure quantum states is said to be k-learnable if there is a measurement strategy that always narrows an unknown sample drawn from the list down to one of at most k candidates. The parameter k interpolates between distinguishability and antidistinguishability, and provides a unified framework for partial state identification. We prove two universal, and optimal, Gram-matrix criteria for k-learnability: a Frobenius-norm sufficient condition and an entrywise-$\ell_1$ necessary condition. We apply them to derive explicit learnability and copy-complexity guarantees for several well-known sets of states including SIC-POVMs, mutually unbiased bases, and stabilizer states. We further apply our results to zero-error mutation detection problems such as anomaly detection and changepoint detection.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, pump-free method for generating microwave-optical Bell pairs, which is crucial for quantum transduction linking superconducting circuits to optical networks. By leveraging spin or atomic systems coupled to optical and microwave cavities, the authors demonstrate protocols that maintain high fidelity and operational rates while circumventing the noise issues associated with traditional optical pumping techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the fundamental engineering challenge of quantum interconnects by providing detailed theoretical protocols for generating high-quality entangled pairs between microwave and optical domains using solid-state and atomic platforms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving quantum transduction—the coherent conversion of quantum states between microwave and optical frequencies—while avoiding detrimental effects from strong optical pumping.

**Main result.** The authors propose and analyze a pump-free microwave-optical Bell-pair source (MO-NODE) that generates heralded Bell pairs using spin or atomic systems, achieving high fidelity and kilohertz-range rates.

**Method.** The method involves engineering the entanglement generation via resonant coupling between a spin/atom system and microwave/optical cavities, utilizing time-bin encoding and cavity QED principles.

**Model / system.** The work models three distinct physical implementations: single color centers, atomic ensembles (e.g., 87Rb), and phonon-mediated systems. The underlying physics is described using cavity QED Hamiltonians and decay rate analyses.

**Key observables.** Heralded Bell pair rate ($R_{	ext{herald}}$), heralded fidelity ($F_{	ext{herald}}$), and the dark-state leakage probability ($P_D|R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cooperativity ($C$), cavity decay rates ($\kappa_o, \kappa_e$), spin dephasing time ($T_{2,s}$), and coupling strengths ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as the critical-coupling limit, adiabatic elimination of cavity/phonon modes, and assuming the coupling rate exceeds intrinsic decoherence rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare direct transduction, teleportation-based transduction, and the proposed pump-free MO-NODE scheme across various physical platforms (spin, ensemble, phonon).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for pump-free transduction, detailing the single-spin protocol, generalizing to ensemble and phonon-mediated systems, and analyzing leakage probabilities in inhomogeneous environments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The coherent conversion between microwave and optical photons, known as quantum transduction, is critical for connecting superconducting processors to optical networks. Existing methods are limited by complications associated with optical pumping. We propose a pump-free microwave-optical Bell-pair source for teleportation-based transduction. Using a spin or atomic system resonantly coupled to optical and microwave cavities, the scheme generates loss-robust heralded Bell pairs. Across color centers, atomic ensembles, and phonon-mediated systems, this assembly achieves kilohertz-range heralding rates with high fidelity.

</details>

**Authors:** Subham Dutta Chowdhury, Leo Radzihovsky, Luca V. Delacrétaz  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16147v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes quantum critical solids characterized by 'floppy' transverse phonons, leading to a Lifshitz quantum critical point. Using RG techniques in $d=4-\epsilon$, the authors determine universal critical exponents and show how the dimensionality dictates the nature of the critical interactions. The findings are crucial for understanding the non-linear response of quantum materials near phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of nonlinear elastic response and the RG flow near quantum critical points provides a powerful theoretical framework for understanding emergent quantum phases in condensed matter systems, which is relevant to many-body dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the universal critical behavior of quantum solids, specifically focusing on the fate of 'floppy' solids whose transverse phonons have a vanishing velocity.

**Main result.** The system is driven toward a strongly-coupled Lifshitz quantum critical point (QCP) in $d=3$, although the transition is generically first-order. The analysis reveals distinct dimensional behaviors, notably the vanishing of cubic nonlinearity in $d=2$.

**Method.** The study employs the Renormalization Group (RG) technique within an $\epsilon = 4-d$ expansion to analyze the effective field theory of elasticity.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a quantum solid described by an effective Lagrangian for transverse phonons, leading to a Lifshitz QCP. The analysis distinguishes between the vector nature of the phonons, which introduces cubic nonlinearities, and the scalar case.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear elastic response ($\epsilon \sim \sigma^{1/\delta}$), specific heat ($C_V \sim T d/z$), and the dynamic shear modulus ($\mu(\omega, T)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality ($d$), the dynamic exponent ($z$), and the correlation length exponent ($
u$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the $\epsilon$-expansion and assumes that the critical behavior is visible within the Ginzburg region, despite the transition being generically first-order.

**Figures summary.** The figures illustrate the stability regions and RG flows of the coupling constants ($c_2^L, 
u^2, w_3$) in the $d=2$ limit, constrained by stability conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the EFT for floppy solids, analyzing the RG flow in $d=4-\epsilon$ to find critical exponents, and then dedicating significant sections to the specific, non-trivial behavior observed in $d=2$ due to the vanishing of the cubic coupling.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the fate of quantum solids with a vanishing velocity of transverse phonons. Such ``floppy'' solids exhibit a cubic elastic nonlinearity that is relevant in spatial dimensions $d<4$ and drives the system to a strongly-coupled Lifshitz quantum critical point in $d=3$, that we analyze in an $ε= 4-d$ expansion. The associated phase transition is generically first-order, preempting the system's full access to the strongly-coupled fixed point. Nevertheless, the critical data of this quantum critical point is accessible within a Ginzburg region in the vicinity of the transition. This cubic nonlinearity vanishes exactly in $d=2$, where the leading interactions are instead quartic and are marginal, leading to weak logarithmic singularities. We discuss possible realizations of this ``crystal Lifshitz criticality'' in quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the holographic dual of a local Unruh-DeWitt detector in AdS space using the HKLL reconstruction program. It calculates entanglement harvesting by relating bulk detector interactions to smeared boundary operators. The work is significant for understanding how quantum information processes are encoded across the AdS/CFT boundary.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects detector physics (Unruh effect) in curved spacetime to holographic principles, providing a concrete calculation of quantum information measures like entanglement harvesting using advanced mathematical tools.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the boundary dual description for a local Unruh-DeWitt detector situated in the bulk of Anti-de Sitter (AdS) space.

**Main result.** The boundary dual is found to be a smeared detector on the boundary, whose smearing profile is dictated by the HKLL reconstruction kernel. The framework successfully analyzes entanglement harvesting in AdS$_3$.

**Method.** The authors employ the HKLL reconstruction program to map bulk field correlators to boundary correlators, calculating entanglement harvesting via the two-detector density matrix to quadratic order in the coupling constant.

**Model / system.** The system involves Unruh-DeWitt detectors coupled to quantum fields in AdS space, analyzed using the AdS/CFT correspondence framework.

**Key observables.** Entanglement harvesting amount, the two-detector density matrix, and the relationship between bulk microcausality and boundary detector overlap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling constant $\lambda$, the dimension $\Delta$ of the dual operator, and the specific choice of the HKLL smearing kernel.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reconstruction is treated as an approximate notion, valid in a semiclassical regime, and the analysis focuses on free quantum fields on a fixed AdS background.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the support of the kernel $K_{D_i}$ (the causal diamond $D_i$) and the corresponding bulk region $W[D_i]$ (the causal wedge).

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews bulk reconstruction, introduces the detector formalism, performs the entanglement harvesting calculations, and discusses the resulting physical implications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

What is the boundary dual of an Unruh-DeWitt detector in anti-de Sitter space? We argue that this question is naturally answered by the HKLL reconstruction program. A local detector in the bulk can be associated with a smeared detector in the boundary, where the choice of smearing depends on the choice of HKLL kernel. We use this formalism to analyze entanglement harvesting in the Poincaré patch of AdS$_{3}$ for a bulk Unruh-DeWitt detector paired either with a boundary detector or with a second bulk detector. We compute the amount of harvesting in the standard way, namely by evaluating the two-detector density matrix to quadratic order in the coupling $λ$. Additionally, we address an apparent tension between microcausality for spacelike-separated bulk detectors and the fact that the dual HKLL detectors may directly overlap in the boundary theory.

</details>

**Authors:** Kyung-Su Kim, Steven A. Kivelson  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16483v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how entropy can drive first-order thermal transitions between competing Quantum Hall phases, such as integer and fractional QH states. By analyzing the free energy contributions from collective excitations like magnons and magnetorotons, the authors propose a mechanism explaining the observed thermal evolution in moiré graphene. This highlights the crucial role of thermal fluctuations in determining the ground state of strongly correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on entropy-driven phase transitions in strongly correlated electron systems, particularly using soft modes like magnetorotons, provides a rich theoretical tool applicable to understanding emergent quantum phases in novel condensed matter platforms like moiré heterostructures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the mechanism driving first-order, finite-temperature thermal transitions between competing Quantum Hall (QH) regimes, specifically between extended integer and fractional QH states.

**Main result.** The transition is shown to be driven by the difference in the entropy associated with low-energy excitations (like Goldstone or magnetoroton modes), providing a plausible bulk mechanism for thermal evolution in moiré graphene.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the free-energy density difference ($\Delta f$) between the competing states, incorporating contributions from gapless Goldstone modes (e.g., magnons) and soft gapped modes (e.g., magnetorotons).

**Model / system.** The general framework is applied to Quantum Hall systems, with a specific focus on moiré rhombohedral graphene (mRG) exhibiting both integer and fractional QH states. The competition is analyzed using free energy minimization.

**Key observables.** Free-energy difference ($f_F - f_I$), transition temperature ($k_B T_c$), and the Hall conductivity jump at the first-order boundary.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), energy gaps ($\Delta_R$), warping energy ($W$), and interaction strengths ($J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as treating magnetorotons as noninteracting bosons and assuming specific energy scale hierarchies (e.g., $\Delta_R \gg k_B T \gg W$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the T-g phase diagram for two competing QH regimes, and Table I summarizes the entropy-driven mechanisms for various QH transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general theoretical framework for entropy-driven transitions, applies this to specific physical models (Coulomb, short-range, magnetoroton), and concludes with a detailed application to moiré graphene, analyzing the role of different excitation modes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electronic states with coexisting Wigner-crystal order can sometimes exhibit a quantum Hall effect over a finite range of electron densities---i.e., exhibit ``extended'' quantum Hall (QH) plateaus in the absence of disorder. Such an extended quantum Hall state can then compete with other QH states over the same density range, allowing a first-order thermal transition between them. Here, we analyze several settings in which the entropy associated with Goldstone modes (e.g., magnons or phonons) or soft gapped modes (e.g., magnetoroton) drives a finite-temperature transition between competing QH regimes. Applying this framework to moiré rhombohedral graphene, we argue that a soft magnetoroton in a fractional quantum anomalous Hall state provides a plausible bulk mechanism for the observed thermal evolution from an extended integer QH to a fractional QH regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, systematic computational method to calculate nonlinear electron-phonon interactions of any order directly from first principles. By extending beyond linear approximations, the authors show that these higher-order couplings are crucial for accurately predicting key properties like polaron binding energies. This methodology promises to advance the study of charge transport and excited-state dynamics in various materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the rigorous, systematic, and *ab initio* nature of developing a general computational framework for higher-order interactions is highly relevant to modeling complex, non-adiabatic dynamics in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a first-principles theory and computational method to calculate nonlinear electron-phonon interactions of arbitrary order, overcoming the limitations of existing linear-coupling approximations.

**Main result.** The authors present a systematic framework to compute second-order (and higher) electron-phonon coupling matrix elements, demonstrating that these nonlinear terms are essential for achieving quantitative accuracy in polaron formation energies.

**Method.** The method combines unit-cell calculations of electron wavefunctions with supercell calculations of phonon perturbations, allowing for the calculation of complex Born-von-Kármán integrals.

**Model / system.** The physics centers on electron-phonon coupling, which governs phenomena like superconductivity, polaron formation, and carrier dynamics. The calculations are applied to representative materials including diamond, lithium fluoride, and graphite.

**Key observables.** Second-order electron-phonon coupling matrix elements ($g^{(2)}$), electron quasiparticle energy shifts (e.g., Debye-Waller correction), polaron formation energies, and hopping barriers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The calculation is sensitive to the order of coupling (second-order being a key focus) and the nature of the exchange-correlation functional used (semilocal or nonlocal).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is designed to overcome the limitations of the rigid-ion approximation (RIA) and the computational difficulty of calculating off-diagonal, nonlinear coupling terms.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the methodology involves mapping unit-cell wavevectors to the reciprocal space of the BvK supercell.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical necessity of nonlinear couplings, then details the novel computational framework combining unit-cell and supercell calculations to evaluate the required coupling integrals, and finally validates the method by computing $g^{(2)}$ for three distinct material classes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron-phonon interactions underpin a variety of phenomena, ranging from transport and superconductivity to polarons and ultrafast carrier dynamics. Despite being one of the most intensely studied subjects in condensed matter physics, research on electron-phonon physics mostly focused on linear, first-order couplings. Second- and higher-order nonlinear couplings are commonly ignored because their calculations are too demanding and we lack computational frameworks that can provide both diagonal and off-diagonal coupling matrix elements. In this work, we report a theory and computational method for computing nonlinear electron-phonon interactions of any order in real materials. Our approach combines the advantages of unit-cell calculations of electron wavefunctions and supercell calculations of phonon perturbations, is systematically improvable, can be used with either semilocal or nonlocal exchange-correlation functionals, and is amenable to Wannier-Fourier interpolation. As a first proof of concept, we illustrate this method by computing second-order electron-phonon coupling matrix elements in diamond, lithium fluoride, and graphite as representative nonpolar semiconductors, polar semiconductors, and metals, respectively. Furthermore, we generalize the ab initio polaron equations to second-order electron-phonon couplings, and we show that second-order couplings are essential to achieve quantitative accuracy in polaron formation energies and hopping barriers. The present methodology will find application in the study of all properties and phenomena that are currently being investigated within the linear electron-phonon coupling approximation, from phonon-mediated superconductivity to excited-states dynamics, both in harmonic and anharmonic systems.

</details>

### [Development of a Physics-Informed Neural Framework, MEOWN, for Rapid Prediction of Muon Stopping Sites in Crystalline Materials, for understanding Quantum Magnet employing Muon Spectroscopy](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17063v1)

**Authors:** A. Pandey, K. Sharma, S. Ghosh, G. Roy, T. Basu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17063v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

Starting from an input CIF file, MEOWN identifies crystal symmetry, con- structs physics-based field tensors, generates candidate muon sites using a physics-informed CNN and seeding strategies, and refines them through optimization to predict equilibrium muon stopping sites, energies, and local dipolar fields.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces MEOWN, a novel, physics-informed machine learning framework designed to predict where a muon will stop inside a crystal lattice. By combining a 3D-CNN with fundamental physics models, it achieves accuracy comparable to expensive DFT calculations but in minutes. This drastically accelerates the analysis of quantum materials using muon spectroscopy.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the core methodology—integrating complex, multi-scale physics models (like polarization and screening) directly into a neural network architecture for rapid prediction—is highly relevant to developing ML tools for complex quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is to rapidly and accurately determine the equilibrium muon stopping site in crystalline materials to interpret Muon-spin rotation/relaxation ($\mu$SR) experiments.

**Main result.** The developed framework, MEOWN, predicts stopping sites and local dipolar fields for benchmark materials with first-principles accuracy in minutes, significantly faster than conventional DFT methods.

**Method.** MEOWN is a physics-informed machine learning framework that embeds multiple physical interactions (electrostatic, polarization, etc.) into a 3D-CNN to guide the prediction of the potential energy landscape.

**Model / system.** The system involves a positive muon ($\mu^+$) implanted into a crystalline solid. The effective potential energy is modeled as a sum of several physical terms, including Hartree-Fock, polarization, overlap repulsion, and zero-point energy.

**Key observables.** Equilibrium muon stopping site (coordinates), local dipolar magnetic field, and total energy (including ZPE correction).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The calculation incorporates effects like electronic screening ($\epsilon_{\mu}(r)$), polarization ($V_{Pol}$), and zero-point motion ($V_{ZPE}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method operates within the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, and it is designed as a fast, reliable alternative, not a replacement for full self-consistent DFT.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the MEOWN workflow from CIF input to site prediction. Figure 2 shows the GUI output, displaying predicted sites, energy landscapes, and calculated dipolar fields for various materials.

**Paper structure.** The paper first details the physical components contributing to the muon potential energy. It then introduces MEOWN, which embeds these physics into a 3D-CNN. The workflow involves multi-stage seeding, CNN inference, structural optimization, and final site ranking based on total energy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Muon-spin rotation/relaxation is one of the most powerful microscopic probes for understanding magnetic order, spin dynamics, superconductivity, and complex magnetic phases in emerging quantum materials. Quantitative interpretation depends critically on the accurate identification of the muon stopping site, a problem traditionally addressed using density functional theory-based structural relaxation. However, conventional DFT approaches are computationally demanding, time-consuming, and require extensive calculations. Here, we develop MEOWN (Muon Engine for Optimized Weighted Networks), a machine-learning-based, physics-informed computational framework that combines a Polarizable Unperturbed Electrostatic Potential (P-UEP) model with machine-learning-guided optimization and symmetry-driven relaxation to predict energetically favorable muon stopping sites in crystalline solids. MEOWN explicitly incorporates electrostatic interactions, electronic screening, polarization effects, and zero-point motion into the learning workflow, providing physically interpretable predictions with substantially reduced computational cost while maintaining physical consistency and scientific rigor. To validate the framework, we investigated several well-established benchmark materials, including MnSi, CoF2, CaF2, LiF, and NaF. The predicted muon stopping sites and dipolar fields are in close agreement with previously reported DFT+mu results, demonstrating the reliability and transferability of the approach across chemically diverse systems. Notably, MEOWN predicts the equilibrium muon stopping site within a few minutes, offering a significant speedup over conventional DFT-based calculations. This manuscript presents the theoretical foundations, computational methodology, and validation of MEOWN as a general-purpose software for rapid and reliable muon stopping-site prediction in crystalline materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a geometric and stability analysis of iterative fixed-node dynamics used to approximate quantum ground states in the presence of the Fermion sign problem. By analyzing the fixed points of the iterative map, the authors prove that the ground state is the stable attractor, while other approximations flow away from the true solution. This clarifies the theoretical foundations for sign-problem mitigation techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical analysis of fixed-point dynamics provides deep insight into the convergence properties of variational methods used to tackle sign problems in strongly correlated quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Fermion sign problem (FSP) in quantum many-body systems, specifically analyzing the convergence and stability of iterative fixed-node approximations.

**Main result.** The self-consistent iteration is shown to drive the system towards the true ground state fixed point, proving that the ground state fixed point is stable while excited state fixed points are unstable.

**Method.** The analysis involves studying the fixed points and stability of an iterative map derived from the lattice fixed-node Hamiltonian, utilizing linearization techniques and spectral radius analysis.

**Model / system.** The study applies to quantum many-body systems governed by Hamiltonians, comparing continuum electronic structure models with lattice models (e.g., spin-1/2 models, $J_1-J_2$ chains).

**Key observables.** Sign structure, energy convergence ($E_k - E_{k+1}$), spectral weight concentration, and the spectral radius ($ho(J)$) of the Jacobian matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The sign structure (chamber) of the ground state, the system size ($L$), and the initial sign-flip probability ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the flow of the iterative map independent of practical algorithmic implementation, and relies on linearization around fixed points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the iterative flow towards the ground state in the correct sign chamber, show energy and spectral weight convergence, and plot the spectral radius $ho(J)$ to confirm stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the iterative map, proves the stability of the ground state fixed point, analyzes the instability of excited states, and confirms the reconstruction of ground state amplitudes through self-consistent iteration.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The sign-structure of a quantum many-body ground state is a central quantity in fixed-node approaches to mitigating the Fermion sign problem. For continuum electronic-structure Hamiltonians, the correct sign-structure is sufficient to compute exact ground state properties; on the other hand, lattice fixed-node approaches retain an additional dependence on trial wave-function amplitudes which are improved upon non-optimally while preserving their sign structure. Here we consider an iterative map obtained by repeatedly replacing the fixed-node trial state with the ground state of its associated lattice fixed-node Hamiltonian (independent of whether and how a practical algorithm could implement this iteration). We show the fixed points of this map are eigenstates of the Hamiltonian and reduced Hamiltonian resulting in at most one fixed point in the interior of each sign chamber. We prove that the ground state fixed point is stable and that the boundary of the ground state sign chamber is repulsive. This shows the amplitude dependence beyond having the correct ground state sign-structure disappears under self-consistent iteration. In other sign chambers, energy descent can be obstructed by the imposed sign structure; this tension leads to the iteration driving select amplitudes to zero producing `support collapse' onto chamber boundaries and corresponding to reduced Hamiltonians. Excited-state fixed points are therefore provably unstable and flow toward lower-energy boundary fixed points. We show sign chamber boundaries have a directional stability; boundary points attractive in one direction are repulsive under a sign flip. By revealing the geometry and stability structure of iterative fixed-node dynamics, our results clarify the foundations of a central approach to the Fermion sign problem and motivate potential new algorithmic strategies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a quantum metrology protocol for atomic clocks limited by spontaneous emission noise. It identifies Spin Grid States (SGSs) as near-optimal probes that surpass standard entangled states like GHZ states. The authors propose a practical readout technique that leverages the grid structure of SGSs to nearly saturate the ultimate quantum sensitivity limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, experimentally implementable quantum states (SGSs) that overcome the limitations imposed by realistic noise channels (spontaneous emission) in quantum sensing platforms like atomic clocks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of maximizing precision in phase and frequency estimation using atomic clocks that are fundamentally limited by spontaneous emission noise.

**Main result.** Spin Grid States (SGSs) are identified as practical, near-optimal quantum probes that significantly enhance metrological sensitivity compared to standard states, especially for intermediate and large atomic ensembles.

**Method.** The authors optimize the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) over a variational class of states and propose a sequential readout strategy using an OAT echo to distinguish decay events.

**Model / system.** The system is an ensemble of N identical two-level atoms (qubits) undergoing spontaneous emission, modeled by a master equation incorporating jump operators. The analysis compares SGSs to GHZ and SSS states.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information ($F_\phi$), phase estimation variance ($\Delta\phi^2$), and the success probability of state discrimination ($P_{	ext{succ, PGM}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spontaneous emission strength ($\eta = \gamma T$), ensemble size ($N$), and the crossover point where state optimality changes ($N=51$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on permutationally symmetric states and utilizes the Holstein-Primakoff approximation to relate optimal states to spin GKP states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two-step creation of SGSs on the Bloch sphere, compare the QFI for various states (GHZ, SGS, SSS) versus noise ($\eta$) and size ($N$), and show the high distinguishability of SGS jump terms.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the metrological problem under noise, identifying SGSs as superior probes over GHZ states at intermediate noise levels, detailing the generation protocol using OAT operations, and finally presenting a readout strategy that achieves near-perfect distinguishability of decay events.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement can enhance precision in phase estimation and in frequency estimation with atomic clocks, but it remains a central challenge to identify useful states and measurements under realistic noise processes. Here, we study quantum metrology with an ensemble of atoms subject to spontaneous emission, which limits frequency estimation by constraining the useful interrogation time. We identify spin grid states (SGSs) as the relevant near-optimal probes beyond a crossover at an ensemble size of 51, where GHZ-like states cease to be optimal. SGSs display a periodic grid on the Bloch sphere and can be generated with two one-axis-twisting (OAT) operations separated by a collective rotation. By optimizing the quantum Fisher information over permutationally symmetric states, we find that SGSs perform close to the globally optimal probes for intermediate and large ensembles, which turn out to be spin GKP states. We further present a sequential readout strategy that nearly saturates the corresponding quantum Fisher information. This strategy uses an OAT echo to map spontaneous-emission events onto collective rotations, making the corresponding jump sectors distinguishable and allowing the measurement basis to be conditioned on the number of decay events. Together, these results show that SGSs provide a practical route to quantum-enhanced phase and frequency metrology in atomic ensembles limited by spontaneous emission.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the fundamental limits of frequency stability in mechanical resonators, finding that the dominant $1/f$ noise floor scales universally with the material and geometry, independent of the device's physical volume. The research establishes a key invariant quantity that suggests the noise is governed by intrinsic material properties rather than simple geometric scaling. This has implications for designing next-generation, ultra-sensitive quantum sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical mechanics and materials science, the analysis of noise sources, scaling laws, and fundamental limits of measurement precision touches upon concepts of decoherence and noise floor characterization relevant to quantum sensing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Mechanical frequency-shift sensors are limited by a persistent, high $1/f$ noise floor that exceeds fundamental thermomechanical or quantum limits.

**Main result.** The excess $1/f$ noise floor, characterized by the product $h^{-1} V_{\epsilon}$, is found to be remarkably independent of the device's physical volume over many decades of size.

**Method.** The study analyzes a large dataset of mechanical resonators, fitting the scaling of the noise floor ($\sigma_A$) against device volume ($V$) and comparing results against theoretical scaling predictions.

**Model / system.** The system involves mechanical resonators (NEMS/MEMS) whose frequency shifts are monitored. The noise is modeled as arising from an ensemble of fluctuating defects (fluctuators) within the material.

**Key observables.** The excess $1/f$ frequency noise ($\sigma_A$), characterized by the Allan deviation plateau $\sigma_A \propto h^{-1}$, and the invariant product $h^{-1} V_{\epsilon}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Device volume ($V$), strain-weighted volume integral ($V_{\epsilon}$), Quality Factor ($Q$), and the noise scaling exponent ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The noise is assumed to originate from an ensemble of fluctuators with log-uniformly distributed correlation times, and that the noise plateau is parametric in origin.

**Figures summary.** Figures plot the $1/f$-floor noise versus participation volume, demonstrating a volume-invariant trend, and analyze scaling trends against theoretical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of noise limitation, surveys existing data to test scaling laws (volume vs. Q), derives the volume-invariant scaling law, and discusses the implications for mass resolution and noise cancellation techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mechanical frequency-shift sensors have never reached their fundamental thermomechanical or quantum limits. Every material, size, and transduction scheme yet examined yields a $1/f$ floor 1-3 orders higher, with a flicker-noise coefficient $h_{-1} \propto 1/V_{\varepsilon}$. Surveying the published record, I find the invariant $h_{-1}\,V_{\varepsilon} \equiv σ_A ^2\, V_{\varepsilon}/(2\ln 2)$ independent of device size across 13 decades; volume-independent noise is excluded by 9$σ$, site-averaging by 3.8$σ$. No $Q$ dependence appears: reported $Q^{-n}$ laws scale equivalently when $Q$ and volume co-vary; only volume scaling survives when they do not. Mass resolution follows as $|δm| \propto \sqrt{h_{-1}\,V_{\varepsilon} \cdot V}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes weak ferromagnetism in a complex Heisenberg model on a square lattice. The authors find that the asymmetry between two next-nearest-neighbor couplings ($J_2 
eq J_2'$) is the source of temperature-dependent net magnetization. The findings suggest a material mechanism for weak ferromagnetism that can be detectable in real quantum magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism for weak ferromagnetism—arising from distinct superexchange pathways mediated by different nonmagnetic atoms—offers a novel theoretical pathway distinct from conventional interactions like the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the origin and temperature dependence of weak ferromagnetism in a specific Heisenberg spin model on a square lattice.

**Main result.** Weak ferromagnetism at finite temperatures is primarily caused by the asymmetry between two distinct next-nearest-neighbor exchange couplings ($J_2 
eq J_2'$), while the single-ion anisotropy ($D$) only provides a background for long-range order.

**Method.** The research employs numerical calculations using the Double-Time Green's Function (DTGF) method, incorporating Random Phase Approximation (RPA) and Anderson-Callen's (ACs) decoupling.

**Model / system.** The system is the square-lattice Heisenberg $J_1$-$J_2$-$J_2'$ model, including nearest-neighbor ($J_1$), two alternating next-nearest-neighbor ($J_2, J_2'$), and easy-axis single-ion anisotropy ($D$).

**Key observables.** Sublattice magnetization, net magnetization ($M$), and critical temperature ($T_C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative difference $|J_2 - J_2'|$ is key; $D$ controls the background ordering; $S$ (spin quantum number) and $T$ (temperature) are varied.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on the RPA and ACs decoupling approximations of the DTGF method, and the model is analyzed for $S \ge 1$ due to the anisotropy term.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of magnetizations for various coupling ratios, and show how ground-state magnetizations and maximum net magnetization scale with the spin quantum number $S$ and anisotropy $D$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the magnetic behavior by first establishing the model and methods, then demonstrating that $J_2 
eq J_2'$ drives finite-temperature weak ferromagnetism, and finally quantifying the dependence of $M_{max}$ and $T_C$ on the coupling parameters and spin quantum number.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by recent experimental realizations of the Heisenberg model and two-dimensional altermagnetism using ultracold atoms in optical lattices, we systematically investigate the magnetic properties of the square-lattice Heisenberg $J_1$-$J_2$-$J_2^\prime$ model in the presence of easy-axis single-ion anisotropy $D$. Here, $J_1 (>0)$ is the nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic exchange parameter, while $J_2$ and $J_2^\prime$ are two distinct next-nearest-neighbor superexchange parameters that alternate on the lattice. We perform numerical calculations of the sublattice magnetization, net magnetization, and critical temperature. Interestingly, the net magnetization is not identically zero at finite temperatures; its absolute value first increases and then decreases with increasing temperature, revealing a temperature-dependent weak ferromagnetism. We find that $J_2\not=J_2^\prime$ is the primary factor responsible for weak ferromagnetism at finite temperatures, while $D$ provides the necessary background for two-dimensional long-range magnetic ordering. Moreover, for a fixed $J_2+J_2^\prime$, we observe that a larger $|J_2-J_2^\prime|$ leads to a greater maximum net magnetization and critical temperature, as well as a broader temperature range for weak ferromagnetism. The difference between $J_2$ and $J_2^\prime$ originates from the distinct superexchange pathways between magnetic atoms, with these pathways mediated by two different types of nonmagnetic atoms. This mechanism, which differs from conventional ones such as the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, may be detectable in real materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper classifies when quantum systems can violate classical constraints defined by linear systems over $\mathbb{Z}_n$. It establishes that a quantum-classical gap exists if and only if the dimension $d$ is large enough and shares a common factor with the modulus $n$. The methodology relies on advanced algebraic tools applied to the structure of quantum contextuality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical constraints on the feasibility of quantum correlations (contextuality) that cannot be captured by classical probability distributions, directly impacting the understanding of quantum information protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a complete classification of the existence of finite-dimensional quantum solutions to inconsistent linear constraint systems (LCS).

**Main result.** The existence of such systems with a quantum-classical gap is characterized by the condition that the dimension $d$ must be at least 3 and $\gcd(n, d) > 1$.

**Method.** The authors use algebraic methods, relating LCS to finite arrangements of rank-one projectors and analyzing obstructions using group theory and cohomology.

**Model / system.** The model involves Linear Constraint Systems (LCS) defined over $\mathbb{Z}_n$, which are used to characterize nonlocal games and contextuality in quantum systems.

**Key observables.** The context phase vector ($b_C$), the Pauli multiplication cocycle ($eta$), and the greatest common divisor $\gcd(n, d)$ are key diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The modulus $n$ (e.g., $\mathbb{Z}_n$), the Hilbert space dimension $d$, and the prime $p$ are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between odd and even moduli, and the results are often derived by associating the LCS to specific 'ray-context arrangements'.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Analysis relies heavily on algebraic structures and theorems rather than described figures).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first analyzing the general structure of obstructions to classical solutions, then specializing to tensor-product structures for odd moduli, and finally establishing the necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of quantum-classical gaps using projector resolutions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Linear constraint systems (LCS) provide a compact algebraic language for nonlocal games and state-independent contextuality. In the binary case the Mermin--Peres square shows that inconsistent LCS can be solved by promoting variables to local Pauli operators, whereas a series of no-go results excludes analogous Pauli-, Clifford- and monomial unitary-based constructions at odd prime modulus. We first isolate the reason for this difference: for tensor-product quantum solutions, the obstruction to a classical solution of a LCS over $\mathbb{Z}_n$ decomposes into obstruction classes carried by the individual factors. As a consequence, a genuinely global obstruction cannot arise solely by tensoring locally unobstructed systems. The argument is specific to odd modulus; at even modulus the reordering phase need not vanish, as in Pauli-based examples.   Given this distinction, we instead associate LCS to finite arrangements of rank-one projectors and resolutions of the identity in finite-dimensional vector spaces. Quantum solvability for such LCS is automatic and the search for a quantum-classical gap rests entirely on proving classical unsatisfiability of the underlying modular incidence problem of the arrangement. By relating such LCS with group-valued frame functions, we identify a family of examples in the work of [Harding, Jager, and Smith, Int. J. Theor. Phys. 44, 539 (2005)]. To establish the (non)existence of classically unsatisfiable LCS over $\mathbb{Z}_n$ with quantum solutions in all dimensions $d$ and for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, we further construct explicit examples for the cases $d=n$ prime. Our main result thus positively resolves the existence problem of such LCS beyond the binary case: such systems exist if and only if $d\geq 3$ and $\gcd(n,d)>1$. Moreover, we establish the existence of LCS with a quantum-classical gap over finite fields if and only if $d\geq 3$ and $p\mid d$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously investigates 'antidistinguishability' within the general framework of physical theories (GPTs). It develops mathematical tools to characterize this property, particularly for polygonal state models. The research shows that while these models can sometimes outperform quantum predictions for small systems, their performance converges back to the quantum limit as the system size increases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to foundational quantum physics, as it extends the operational constraints beyond standard quantum mechanics (GPTs) to characterize when and how non-quantum theories can mimic or surpass quantum predictions in specific tasks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the concept of 'antidistinguishability' of quantum states within the general framework of General Probabilistic Theories (GPTs). It refines this notion to study its mathematical properties and physical implications.

**Main result.** The authors establish general bounds on the size of equally antidistinguishable sets and demonstrate that polygonal models can exhibit nonclassical behavior by outperforming the optimal quantum limit in certain coding tasks, though this advantage vanishes in the large-polygon limit.

**Method.** The methodology involves defining and analyzing refined notions of antidistinguishability (strong and equal), applying these concepts to specific models like polygonal theories, and using Random Exclusion Codes (REC) to probe nonclassicality.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the General Probabilistic Theory (GPT), which encompasses quantum mechanics. Specific applications are studied using polygonal state spaces (n-gons) and the associated measurement effects.

**Key observables.** Antidistinguishability, strong antidistinguishability, equal antidistinguishability, and the success merit of Random Exclusion Codes ($S_{\max}^{	ext{REC}}(n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of sides of the polygon ($n$), the affine dimension of the state space ($d$), and the quantum limit value ($S_C^{	ext{REC}} = 8/9$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is conducted within the operational framework of GPTs, assuming the state space $\Omega$ is convex and compact.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 plots the optimal success merit of REC ($S_{\max}^{	ext{REC}}(n)$) versus $n$ for even and odd $n$-gons, comparing it against the established qubit line. Table I summarizes the antidistinguishability properties for various $n$-gon models.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of antidistinguishability in GPTs, deriving general relationships between its refined notions. It then applies these concepts to polygonal theories, proving specific results about the antidistinguishability of pure states. Finally, it uses these results to analyze the performance of Random Exclusion Codes, comparing them to the quantum limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate antidistinguishability of states within the framework of general probabilistic theories (GPTs). We formulate antidistinguishability, strong and equal antidistinguishability as refined notions that imposed additional constraint on the measurement effects. We establish general results relating these notions of antidistinguishability and derive an upper bound on the cardinality of equally antidistinguishable sets in terms of the affine dimension of the state space. We then study antidistinguishability in polygonal theories, obtaining conditions for antidistinguishability of a set of states. In consequence, we show that the set of all pure states in a polygon model is antidistinguishable. Additionally, we identify broad families of strongly and equally antidistinguishable states. Finally, using Random Exclusion Codes, whose success probability is governed by the antidistinguishability of different sets of encoding states, we probe the nonclassicality of polygon theories. We find that certain polygon models can outperform the optimal quantum value, while their optimal performance converges to the quantum limit in the large-polygon limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel, mathematically rigorous framework for verifying the integrity of hybrid quantum-classical workflows against potential adversarial attacks. It defines 'claim-relative' evidence by identifying structural blind regions and establishing necessary reference points (scalar, aggregate, item-level) to guarantee the trustworthiness of computational conclusions. This work is crucial for establishing verifiable trust in future quantum computing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract and focused on security protocols, the rigorous mathematical framework for verifying computational integrity in quantum processes is foundational for building reliable quantum algorithms and quantum information processing pipelines.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a framework to ensure integrity and detect adversarial manipulations within complex hybrid quantum-classical workflows.

**Main result.** It introduces a 'claim-relative evidence/reference' lattice that quantifies different levels of integrity protection (conclusion, aggregate, item-identity) against structural blind regions.

**Method.** The authors use formal methods, defining structural blind regions and testing integrity using specific reference mechanisms like trusted scalars ($R_0$) and adaptive stress tests.

**Model / system.** The model is a general hybrid quantum-classical workflow involving classical record acquisition, quantum evidence generation, kernel learning, and final reporting.

**Key observables.** Conclusion-changing label interventions, structural blind regions, and the degree of required reference granularity (scalar vs. aggregate vs. item-aligned).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required reference granularity (e.g., $R_0$ for conclusion integrity) and the detection rates of label interventions (e.g., 343/2,700).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the existence of a 'claim-relative' view, but it notes limitations regarding the need for an external root and the violation of exchangeability in the design.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured around defining the integrity lattice, analyzing different types of observational indistinguishability (structural vs. statistical), and demonstrating the sufficiency of different reference mechanisms ($R_0, M_0$, item-aligned binding) through rigorous testing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a claim-relative evidence/reference framework for hybrid quantum-classical workflow integrity. Observational indistinguishability yields structural blind regions, distinct from finite-batch statistical misses. Within the declared lattice, a trusted same-batch scalar $R_0$ suffices for conclusion integrity, aggregate $M_0$ for aggregate plus conclusion integrity, and item-aligned binding for item identity. In 3,600 label interventions, feature/prediction views realize exact label-path invariance; all 764 geometry-aligned aggregate-blind rows equal their paired-clean responses, giving zero attack-only increment. For statistical response, the geometry-aligned construction detects 343/2,700 conclusion-changing ($τ\to 0^+$) label interventions with the conformal rule and 1,183/2,700 with the uncorrected union; the original frozen same-item geometry yields 11/2,617 and 43/2,617, respectively. The executed conformal clean false-action rates are 0.048--0.059 descriptively; its finite-sample guarantee requires exchangeability, which the overlapping-draw design violates. The cluster-preserving adaptive stress test (Gate A) reduces response versus matched controls in 25--40 of 40 environment/split cells while retaining conclusion changes. A bounded 165-design-cell ideal-statevector and finite-shot-emulation branch directly instantiates semantic, estimated and observed kernel transitions. The fixed equal-weight design estimates neither deployment prevalence nor QPU, provider or deployed-service assurance.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes the maximum achievable rates for converting unknown mixed qubit states between different purities (concentration and dilution). The optimal rates are fundamentally governed by the eigenvalues of the RLD Fisher information matrix. Crucially, the authors show that these state conversions can be implemented using only SWAP tests, offering a deep operational understanding of quantum state geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel operational interpretation of the full complex RLD Fisher information matrix, linking its geometric properties to fundamental quantum information tasks like state concentration and dilution.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the maximum linear rate at which unknown mixed qubit states can be converted between different purity levels (concentration or dilution).

**Main result.** The optimal conversion rates are determined by the eigenvalues of the complex right-logarithmic-derivative (RLD) Fisher information matrix, and these conversions can be achieved using only SWAP tests.

**Method.** The analysis involves establishing a three-step protocol (Schur sampling, applying a specific channel like cloning/discarding, and inverse Schur sampling) and proving the maximum rate by showing the trace distance vanishes in the infinite copy limit.

**Model / system.** The system involves unknown mixed qubit states ($ho$) and their conversion between states of different purities ($\lambda_{in} 	o \lambda_{out}$). The analysis utilizes concepts from quantum information theory, including the RLD Fisher information matrix and SWAP tests.

**Key observables.** Linear conversion rates ($R_{	ext{opt}}$), trace distance ($	ext{dTr}$), and the eigenvalues of the RLD Fisher information matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Input and output purity levels ($\lambda_{in}, \lambda_{out}$), and the number of copies ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The asymptotic performance relies on the concentration of Schur sampling outcomes, which makes certain error terms exponentially small as $N 	o \infty$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides a unified presentation of four linear-rate conversion protocols, while Figure 2 illustrates tensor network representations for the symmetric subspace related to the state and the discarding map.

**Paper structure.** The paper first presents four theorems establishing the maximum rate achievement, followed by a detailed proof bounding the trace distance by decomposing the error into parts related to sampling fluctuations and the core channel operation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

By consuming multiple copies of an unknown qubit state, one can modify its purity while preserving the direction of its Bloch vector. We determine the maximum linear rate at which qubit states of different purities can be interconverted, allowing a nonzero error, quantified, for instance, by the trace distance, provided that it vanishes in the limit of infinitely many copies. Interestingly, the optimal conversion rate is determined by the two eigenvalues of the complex right-logarithmic-derivative (RLD) Fisher information matrix associated with $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ rotations of the qubit state. When the output qubits have higher purity, corresponding to concentration, the optimal rate is given by the ratio of the maximum eigenvalues of the input and output RLD matrices. In contrast, when the output qubits have lower purity, corresponding to dilution, the optimal rate is given by the ratio of their minimum eigenvalues. Remarkably, both concentration and dilution can be implemented using SWAP tests as the only nontrivial two-qubit measurement primitive, together with ancillary qubits initially prepared in maximally mixed states, without requiring any additional two-qubit gates. Our work thus provides a novel operational interpretation of the full complex RLD Fisher information matrix. Crucially, its antisymmetric, purely imaginary part encodes geometric information beyond the statistical distance between density operators and plays an essential role in determining the optimal state-conversion rates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates combining Quantum Error Correction (QEC) with Quantum Error Mitigation (QEM) for continuous variable quantum computation using the GKP code. It specifically analyzes the overhead required by Probabilistic Error Cancellation (PEC) when applied after standard GKP error correction protocols. The results provide quantitative estimates for noise resilience in these advanced CV quantum computing schemes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for theoretical physicists working on quantum optics and open quantum systems as it provides concrete, quantitative methods for dealing with realistic noise sources in continuous variable quantum computation architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for fault-tolerant quantum computation by studying the combination of Quantum Error Correction (QEC) with Quantum Error Mitigation (QEM) techniques.

**Main result.** It quantifies the sampling overhead associated with using Probabilistic Error Cancellation (PEC) alongside GKP error correction, demonstrating a nontrivial interplay between these two error handling methods in continuous variable codes.

**Method.** The authors use the stabilizer subsystem decomposition to characterize the effective logical channel and employ PEC, which involves decomposing an ideal gate into noisy basis operations to estimate expectation values.

**Model / system.** The system is based on continuous variable (CV) quantum information encoded using the Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) code, analyzed for both square and hexagonal lattice structures.

**Key observables.** Sampling overhead ($C_{em}$), noise covariance matrices ($\Sigma_{	ext{QEC},\Lambda}$), and the effective logical channel.

**Important parameters / regimes.** GKP squeezing parameter ($\Delta$), noise parameters (mean $\mu$ and covariance $\Sigma$ for Gaussian Random Displacement noise), and the type of GKP gate (single- or two-qubit Clifford gates).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes noise is modeled by Gaussian Random Displacement (GRD) noise and relies on the stabilizer subsystem decomposition to map the noisy process to an effective logical channel.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum circuits for both Steane-type and teleportation-based GKP error correction for a single mode, and the appendices detail the mathematical derivations for noise propagation in both schemes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the GKP code structure (Zak basis completeness) to analyzing the noise propagation through two distinct QEC protocols (Steane vs. Teleportation), and finally applying PEC to estimate the overhead associated with mitigating these residual errors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In order to solve practical problems on a quantum computer, it is necessary to use fault-tolerant quantum error correction schemes to overcome noise: the errors arising from imperfections in physical components. The Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) code aims at achieving this in a hardware efficient manner by encoding finite-dimensional logical subspaces in the Hilbert space of one or more continuous variable modes. In near term implementations, however, it is not feasible to eliminate errors entirely, so it is natural to also employ alternative error mitigation techniques together with error correction. In this work, we study a quantum error mitigation method known as probabilistic error cancellation in the context of the GKP code. We compare Steane-type and teleportation-based GKP error correction, and calculate the sampling overheads associated with the technique for square and hexagonal GKP codes. We employ the stabilizer subsystem decomposition for GKP codes to obtain an effective logical channel for noisy operations that is used to express the ideal one of a target logical unitary. We consider noise from finite squeezing on the data and the two ancilla modes, needed for error correction, and examine the relationship between the sampling overhead and the noise for different decoding methods. Our results are calculated for single- and two-qubit GKP Clifford gates and one round of error correction, and show the nontrivial combination of error correction and mitigation in continuous variable codes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a systematic diagrammatic expansion to calculate single-instance thermal observables in the SYK model, bypassing the need for disorder averaging. By organizing the calculation in powers of $N^{-1/2}$, the authors achieve results that match exact diagonalization for small systems and apply the technique to related models like MQ.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to calculate single-instance, non-averaged thermal properties in highly entangled quantum systems like SYK is crucial for understanding quantum chaos and thermalization dynamics in strongly correlated regimes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a method to compute single-instance thermal expectation values for low-weight Majorana strings in the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, moving beyond standard disorder averaging.

**Main result.** The diagrammatic expansion provides excellent quantitative agreement with exact diagonalization results for the four-body SYK model up to $N=24$, and it extends the analysis to the Maldacena–Qi (MQ) model.

**Method.** The core method is a diagrammatic expansion organized in powers of $N^{-1/2}$, which expresses observables as finite sums of polynomials in couplings multiplied by temperature-dependent kernels derived from the large-$N$ melonic propagator.

**Model / system.** The primary system is the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, involving $N$ Majorana fermions with random $p$-body interactions (specifically $p=4$ is highlighted). The analysis is extended to the Maldacena–Qi (MQ) model.

**Key observables.** Single-instance thermal expectation values ($\xi_X$) of low-weight Majorana string operators, and time-ordered correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $eta J$ (inverse temperature times coupling strength) and the system size $N$. The expansion is organized in powers of $N^{-1/2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on a diagrammatic expansion which is treated as a dressed high-temperature expansion; convergence is noted to be non-uniform at low temperatures ($eta J$ large).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an operator-by-operator preview comparing diagrammatic predictions to exact diagonalization results for $N=20, eta J=2$, showing high fidelity ($R^2_{	ext{id}} = 0.9988$).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the diagrammatic expansion (Section 3), discusses corrections to leading diagrams (weight four study in Section 4), extends to time-dependent insertions (Section 5), and applies the method to the Maldacena–Qi (MQ) model (Section 6).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a diagrammatic expansion for thermal expectation values of low-weight Majorana strings in a fixed realization of the $N$-fermion Sachdev--Ye--Kitaev (SYK) model. At each order in the expansion, the observable is expressed as a finite sum of polynomials in the couplings multiplied by temperature-dependent kernels built from the large-$N$ melonic propagator. At fixed $βJ$, the expansion is organized in powers of $N^{-1/2}$, and each order can be efficiently evaluated with classical resources polynomial in $N$. The expansion also extends to correlations at arbitrary imaginary times and sufficiently short real times. For the four-body SYK model, we show that the diagrammatic predictions give an excellent account of exact diagonalization results at sizes up to $N=24$. We also apply the method to the Maldacena--Qi (MQ) model formed from two identical copies of a fixed SYK realization. This enables the identification of a nearby SYK-like Hamiltonian whose thermofield double state has higher fidelity with the Maldacena--Qi ground state than the thermofield double of the original SYK Hamiltonian.

</details>
