**Summary.** This theoretical work demonstrates that introducing a localized defect into an integrable quantum spin chain is sufficient to generate approximate unitary k-designs. By employing a two-Pauli-kick protocol, the authors derive a selection rule showing that the defect must break integrability to facilitate this process. This highlights the crucial role of non-integrability in achieving thermalization-like quantum scrambling.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, defect-induced mechanism for achieving thermalization-like properties (k-designs) in systems that are otherwise integrable, which is a key topic in non-equilibrium quantum dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether introducing a small, local defect into an otherwise integrable quantum many-body system is sufficient to generate approximate unitary k-designs.

**Main result.** A single bulk defect successfully breaks integrability and enables the formation of approximate unitary k-designs via a two-Pauli-kick protocol, a mechanism that is absent in integrable boundary defect scenarios.

**Method.** The study uses the Two-Pauli-Kick (2PK) protocol and analyzes the system's deviation from the Haar ensemble using the frame potential and an overlap ratio derived from Pauli string calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a spin-1/2 XXZ chain Hamiltonian, which is integrable when clean or only subject to boundary defects. Integrability is broken by a single, localized bulk defect.

**Key observables.** Unitary k-design fidelity (measured via the frame potential $F^{(k)}_E$), the overlap ratio $c$, and the domain-wall number $N_{dw}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The defect strength $h_j$, the kick protocol (2PK vs 1PK), and the system size $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the non-resonance assumption and uses approximations for trace calculations involving Pauli strings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the selection rule for admissible Pauli kicks based on the overlap ratio vs. domain-wall number, and compare the decay of the design fidelity for bulk versus boundary defects.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework for k-designs, analyzes the 2PK protocol, derives an analytical selection rule based on the defect structure, and contrasts the design-forming capability between bulk-defect and boundary-defect scenarios.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that an $O(1)$ local defect is sufficient to generate approximate unitary $k$-designs from an otherwise integrable quantum many-body system. Using a two-Pauli-kick (2PK) protocol with temporal sampling, we demonstrate this mechanism in a fixed-magnetization sector of deterministic XXZ spin chains with a single integrability-breaking bulk defect. We derive an analytical selection rule for the Pauli kicks and show that symmetry-preserving strings with a specific domain-wall count, fixed by the couplings, govern design formation. An exhaustive search over all admissible Pauli strings identifies a broad family of kicks that produce designs in the chaotic regime, whereas no such kicks exist in the integrable limit, including for boundary defects. We further show that the design-forming mechanism remains effective for weak bulk defects as the system size increases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework showing that the mechanical force and power transferred by an active bath to a probe are governed by the bath's single-particle trajectory statistics. By analyzing specific active models, the authors determine the conditions under which positive power transfer (energy injection) can occur, providing quantitative predictions for phenomena like drag reversal.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general theoretical tool—the trajectory-response relation—to predict macroscopic mechanical forces and energy flow in complex active matter systems solely from single-particle statistical measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the mechanical force and power transfer exerted by an active bath on a moving probe, linking this response directly to the single-particle trajectory statistics of the bath particles.

**Main result.** The sign of the mechanical power transfer depends critically on the specific dynamics of the active bath; for instance, 1D run-and-tumble particles and 2D active Brownian particles can exhibit modes that transfer positive mechanical power, while AOUPs and 2D+ RTPs do not.

**Method.** The authors derive a trajectory-response relation that separates the bath dynamics from the wave-vector weighting, allowing the calculation of the force and power using the free-particle self-intermediate scattering function (ISF).

**Model / system.** The system consists of a passive probe moving at velocity V immersed in a dilute, ideal gas of noninteracting active particles, modeled using dynamics like Active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, Run-and-Tumble Particles, and Active Brownian Particles.

**Key observables.** Mechanical force $F_{	ext{bath}}(V)$, mechanical power transfer $\dot{W}_{	ext{bath}}(V)$, density response $\deltaho(\mathbf{r}; V)$, and the self-intermediate scattering function $\Phi_0(q, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Probe velocity $V$, wave vector $q$, and the dimensionality $d$ of the system.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed at the leading weak-probe, ideal-bath limit, linearizing the response in the probe potential, and assuming non-interacting bath particles.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not describe specific figures, but mention that the analysis predicts phenomena like drag reversal, spontaneous probe motion, and motion-induced density distortions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework connecting force to trajectory statistics, derives the power transfer criterion, and then applies this framework to analyze specific active particle models (AOUPs, RTPs, ABPs) to determine the sign of energy transfer.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The mechanical response of a moving probe in an active bath reflects both the dynamics of the bath and how the probe couples to it. For a probe moving at velocity $\mathbf V$ and interacting weakly with an ideal active bath, we show that the trajectory statistics of a free bath particle determine a density response from which the bath force on the probe follows. The probe-particle interaction determines how this response is weighted over wave vector $\mathbf q$, while the probe motion selects frequencies $ω_{\mathbf q}=\mathbf q\cdot \mathbf V$. This trajectory-response relation separates the bath dynamics from the wave-vector weighting, so neither the stationary state nor force correlations need to be recalculated for each probe interaction and velocity. Applying this relation to active-particle models, we prove that positive power transfer is excluded for active Ornstein--Uhlenbeck particles in all dimensions and for complete-reset run-and-tumble particles in $d\geq2$, while one-dimensional run-and-tumble particles and two-dimensional active Brownian particles possess modes that can transfer positive mechanical power. For a given probe, this response predicts drag reversal, spontaneous probe motion, and motion-induced density distortions, all in quantitative agreement with simulations. Varying the probe geometry changes how the same bath response is weighted, thereby selecting different moving states. Beyond the leading weak-probe, ideal-bath limit, higher orders in probe strength involve multi-interval trajectory statistics, while finite-density corrections involve interacting-particle dynamics. Our results connect the trajectory statistics of active bath particles to the mechanical response of a passive probe, providing a route to predict probe response from single-particle trajectory statistics measured in the absence of the probe.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes that a universal, data-free prior exists for syntactic structures, derived from modeling the cognitive process of language production. By comparing this theoretical prior against probabilities derived from dozens of real languages, the authors show a strong correlation, suggesting that language structure is partially constrained by universal generative principles rather than solely by accumulated linguistic experience.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on linguistics, the methodology involves complex statistical inference, modeling emergent structure from local rules (preferential attachment), and comparing theoretical priors against empirical data, which shares conceptual overlap with modeling complex systems in condensed matter or neural networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the probability structure of syntactic rules can arise independently of language-specific statistical learning, suggesting a universal, data-free prior.

**Main result.** A universal prior derived from a cognitively motivated model of language production shows a strong positive correlation with probabilities estimated from actual linguistic data across many typologically diverse languages.

**Method.** The authors compare the probabilities derived from a data-free sublinear preferential attachment (sPA) prior against probabilities estimated using Probabilistic Context-Free Grammars (PCFGs) for various languages.

**Model / system.** The model is a cognitively motivated process of incremental language production, where syntactic structures are built up as a growing network (dependency tree). The probability is calculated by integrating over attachment parameters and production orderings.

**Key observables.** Correlation coefficients between the universal sPA prior probability and language-specific PCFG probabilities; the linear regression coefficient (beta) linking these two measures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The sublinear attachment exponent $\alpha$ (integrated over a Beta(2, 2) prior); the number of nodes $n$ in the dependency tree.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The underlying generative process follows an approximately incremental sublinear preferential attachment model, and language-specific probabilities are adequately approximated by PCFGs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the probability advantage of the sPA model over a uniform prior, and show the positive correlation between the sPA prior and language-specific PCFG probabilities across many languages.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of data-independent syntactic priors, develops the sPA model based on incremental production, calculates the universal prior via Monte Carlo integration, and finally tests this prior by correlating its predictions against empirical PCFG estimates across a large, diverse dataset of languages.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Probability is fundamental to theories of language comprehension, production, acquisition, and evolution, as well as to large language models. Existing theories estimate the probability of syntactic structures from language-specific data. Whether part of this probability structure can arise independently of language-specific experience remains unknown. Here I show that a universal prior over syntactic structures emerges from a cognitively motivated model of incremental language production, in which words are progressively integrated into syntactic structure through network growth. The resulting prior assigns probabilities to syntactic structures --represented as dependency trees-- without fitting parameters to linguistic data, and assigns higher probabilities to attested than to random trees in all 138 typologically diverse languages examined. These prior probabilities correlate positively with probabilities estimated from corpora in 33 of 34 languages. The results indicate that part of the probability structure of syntax can arise independently of language-specific statistical learning. Linguistic experience may therefore refine probabilities that are already structured by the process of language production, rather than create them from an initially uniform space. This identifies a possible cognitive origin for part of the probability distribution over syntactic structures, linking language production and statistical learning while providing a data-independent structural bias for probabilistic models of language.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an improved auxiliary boson method to model strongly correlated quantum dots with finite Coulomb interaction U. By refining an existing theoretical framework, the authors achieve an analytically tractable approach that accurately predicts transport properties. The results are rigorously validated by comparing them against established Numerical Renormalization Group calculations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an analytical alternative to purely numerical methods (like NRG) for studying strongly correlated impurity models, which is valuable for theoretical modeling of nanoscale electronic devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately describing strong electronic correlations in quantum dots (QDs) when a finite Coulomb interaction (U) is present.

**Main result.** The improved auxiliary boson method provides an analytically tractable and robust framework that yields results in good agreement with benchmark Numerical Renormalization Group (NRG) calculations.

**Method.** The authors refine the auxiliary boson formulation (originally by Kotliar and Ruckenstein) and apply a mean-field approximation to derive the system's Hamiltonian and solve for ground state properties self-consistently.

**Model / system.** The system is a single-level quantum dot coupled to two metallic leads, modeled by an Anderson impurity-like Hamiltonian that includes the Coulomb repulsion term U.

**Key observables.** Local Density of States (LDOS), probability of the QD being in empty, singly occupied, or doubly occupied states, and the Kondo temperature ($T_K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Coulomb interaction U, the coupling strength $\Gamma$, and the energy level $\epsilon_0$. The Kondo temperature scale is given by a specific formula involving U and $\Gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the mean-field approximation at low temperatures, and the final comparison is restricted to regimes where U and $|	ilde{\epsilon}_0|$ are significantly larger than $\Gamma$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the probability of the QD occupying different charge states under electron-hole symmetry, and show the LDOS calculated via NRG, which is then fitted by the theoretical model.

**Paper structure.** The paper first sets up the Hamiltonian for the QD in the Kondo regime, introduces the improved auxiliary boson formalism, derives the mean-field Hamiltonian, solves for self-consistent bosonic parameters, and finally compares the resulting spectral functions and transport properties against NRG benchmarks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we present an improved version of the formulation of the four auxiliary bosons originally proposed by Kotliar and Ruckenstein, adapted to describe a quantum dot in the Kondo regime with finite electron-electron interaction U. The proposed refinement provides a more accurate treatment of strong electronic correlations while preserving the analytical tractability of the original approach, thereby enabling a reliable description of transport properties in nanoscale systems. Benchmark calculations show good agreement with the results of the numerical renormalization group, supporting the validity of the improved formulation. Our approach therefore provides an efficient and robust framework for investigating transport through strongly correlated quantum dots.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper solves the microscopic statistics of eigenstate overlaps in random free fermions, going beyond the smooth energy dependence predicted by ETH. By calculating the exact two-point covariance, the authors prove that the fine-grained quantum fluctuations are not determined by the smooth energy envelope. This provides a crucial, exactly solvable benchmark for understanding thermalization in many-body quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, exactly solvable model to test the limits of ETH, offering concrete, non-trivial statistics for quantum fluctuations that are crucial for understanding thermalization in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the microscopic statistics of overlaps between many-body eigenstates and a basis, going beyond the one-point statement provided by Eigenstate Thermalization Hypothesis (ETH).

**Main result.** The two-point covariance closes exactly at the Gaussian fixed point, demonstrating that the smooth ETH envelope does not determine the fine-grained two-point fluctuations.

**Method.** The authors use an exactly solvable random free-fermion ensemble to derive exact moment statistics for channel overlaps, comparing them to established random-matrix theory benchmarks.

**Model / system.** The system is random free fermions whose eigenstates are Slater determinants built from eigenvectors of a Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble (GOE) single-particle Hamiltonian. This ensemble serves as an exactly solvable benchmark for testing theoretical structures.

**Key observables.** Two-point overlap covariance, normalized second-moment ratio ($q$), and the structure of the multi-resolvent correlation levels ($g_{ji}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The depth of the determinant ($k$), the number of fermionic modes ($N$), and the correlation correction terms ($g_{11}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the framework of a 'multi-resolvent dictionary' to separate the ETH function into a smooth baseline and fluctuation terms.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference tables comparing analytic ETH constructions with exact free-fermion results across different theoretical sectors.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds a multi-resolvent hierarchy, first analyzing the one-point statistics (flat intensity distribution), then deriving the exact two-point covariance using random-matrix theory, and finally establishing the structural identities that show the independence of the mean and fluctuation sectors.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Eigenstate thermalization constrains the smooth dependence of observables on energy, but it does not by itself fix the microscopic statistics of the overlaps between many-body eigenstates and a chosen basis: the smooth envelope is a one-point statement, and the fluctuation field it leaves undetermined carries a structured two-point covariance. We establish this distinction, and solve that fluctuation field exactly, in random free fermions---an ensemble of Slater-determinant eigenstates built from a Gaussian orthogonal single-particle Hamiltonian, whose eigenstate thermalization was established by Magán.   In this ensemble every channel overlap is a minor of a Haar-distributed orthogonal matrix, so its statistics follow from classical random-matrix theory. The one-point law is exactly flat for every channel, with an exact moment hierarchy that is not Porter--Thomas: small intensities are enhanced algebraically rather than by the exponential Porter--Thomas form, and the mean sector carries a negative, order-one correlation correction of purely normalization origin. The two-point covariance closes exactly at the Gaussian fixed point: it is organized by the number of one-body modes shared by two eigenstates and by the number of modes shared by two channels, and its energy-resolved form factorizes into this geometry times the classical convolution of the single-particle semicircle, from which the two-resolvent covariance follows by an integral transform. Exact finite-size computations confirm all closed forms, and the structural identities of the dictionary hold at machine precision. The results provide an exactly solvable microscopic realization of the two-point fluctuation sector underlying multi-resolvent descriptions of eigenstate thermalization: a smooth envelope does not determine fine-grained fluctuations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Clifford-stabilizer simulation, a novel, efficient algorithm for classically simulating the noisy preparation of logical magic states crucial for fault-tolerant quantum computation. By mapping complex non-Clifford noisy circuits onto equivalent Clifford circuits, the method allows for the exact benchmarking of large-scale quantum error correction protocols. This provides a vital tool for assessing the feasibility of useful FTQC.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation, the development of efficient classical simulation techniques for complex, error-prone quantum circuits is a significant computational tool relevant to understanding resource scaling in quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing an exact and efficient classical simulation method to predict the performance of fault-tolerant quantum computation (FTQC) by simulating noisy logical magic state preparation.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Clifford-stabilizer simulation, an efficient algorithm that exactly maps non-Clifford noisy circuits to equivalent Clifford circuits, enabling benchmarking of large-scale magic state preparation up to fault distance 7.

**Method.** The method involves tracking the evolution of a Clifford-stabilizer group ($	ext{GC}$) to simulate noisy protocols, mapping non-Clifford operations to an equivalent Clifford circuit representation.

**Model / system.** The context is quantum error correction codes (e.g., surface codes) used in FTQC, focusing on preparing logical magic states required for non-Clifford gates. The simulation incorporates circuit-level Pauli errors and Pauli-stabilizer codes.

**Key observables.** Measurement outcome distributions, logical error rates, and acceptance rates for magic state cultivation protocols.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fault distance ($f=3, 5, 7$), circuit-level Pauli error rates, and the size of the stabilizer group.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation assumes a specific noise model (depolarizing channel) and utilizes a systematic approach to map non-Clifford dynamics onto a Clifford-stabilizer framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate gate-teleportation circuits for T-gate implementation and the structure of surface codes for various distances, while tables detail the cultivation protocols for $f=3, 5, 7$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds conceptually by first defining the problem, introducing the Clifford-stabilizer group ($	ext{GC}$), establishing update rules, and finally applying the simulation framework to detailed protocols for magic state cultivation and error correction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The preparation of high-fidelity logical magic states is a crucial subroutine for universal fault-tolerant quantum computation (FTQC). Predicting the performance of FTQC and developing improved protocols rely on numerical methods to classically simulate logical magic state preparation in the presence of noise. Clifford logic on Pauli-stabilizer codes with circuit-level Pauli errors can be efficiently simulated using Pauli-stabilizer formalism, but the non-Clifford operations required to prepare logical magic states render generic simulation inefficient. We introduce Clifford-stabilizer simulation, an exact and efficient algorithm based on updating a Clifford-stabilizer group to simulate noisy preparation protocols for a broad class of logical magic states used to implement non-Clifford gates in the third level of the Clifford hierarchy under circuit-level Pauli errors. Clifford-stabilizer simulation applies to a range of operations that commonly appear in preparation protocols for such logical magic states, including Pauli-stabilizer measurements, logical Clifford measurements, and transversal non-Clifford gates. Our algorithm for Clifford-stabilizer simulation maps a non-Clifford circuit with sampled circuit-level Pauli errors to a Clifford circuit that exactly reproduces its measurement outcome distribution, achieving time and space complexities polynomial in relevant protocol parameters. We perform exact simulation of magic state cultivation up to fault distance 7 by Clifford-stabilizer simulation. Our method provides a route to perform exact benchmarking of large-scale logical magic state preparation protocols required for useful FTQC.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides an exact analytical method to calculate the characteristic function of work for bosonic systems driven by time-dependent quadratic Hamiltonians. By reformulating the problem using symplectic geometry, the authors bypass the complex time-ordering issues inherent in quantum evolution. This framework successfully reproduces fundamental fluctuation theorems, making it a powerful tool for studying non-equilibrium quantum thermodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general, and exact framework for calculating non-equilibrium work statistics in quadratic bosonic systems, directly connecting quantum dynamics (symplectic geometry) to core principles of non-equilibrium thermodynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Deriving an exact analytical expression for the characteristic function of work in closed many-body bosonic systems governed by general time-dependent quadratic Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** An exact analytical expression for the work characteristic function is derived, which allows for the calculation of key thermodynamic quantities like the free energy and work variance.

**Method.** The authors employ a formalism based on the inhomogeneous Metaplectic group, replacing the difficult time-ordering problem with the determination of a symplectic path in phase space.

**Model / system.** The system consists of closed many-body bosonic systems described by general time-dependent quadratic Hamiltonians, which are analyzed using canonical variables in phase space.

**Key observables.** Characteristic function of work ($\chi(u)$), Helmholtz free energy, mean work, work variance, and the nonequilibrium lag.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The initial thermal equilibrium state ($ho_0$), the time dependence of the Hamiltonian, and the symplectic path parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary derivation assumes the initial state is an instantaneous thermal equilibrium state, although generalizations to non-equilibrium states are discussed.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the theoretical necessity of calculating work statistics in non-equilibrium processes, develops the mathematical machinery using the Metaplectic group and symplectic paths to derive the characteristic function, and finally applies this function to recover fundamental fluctuation theorems and thermodynamic relations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive an exact analytical expression for the characteristic function of work in closed many-body bosonic systems governed by general time-dependent quadratic Hamiltonians. This is made possible by adopting a unified prescription in which all operators appearing in the characteristic function are treated as elements of the inhomogeneous Metaplectic group. The esulting expression involves a single symplectic matrix, a single phase-space displacement, and a single accumulated phase. It holds for any number of degrees of freedom, arbitrary time dependence of the quadratic Hamiltonian, and any initial thermal equilibrium state of the system. The formalism trades the explicit time-ordering problem associated with the unitary evolution for the determination of a symplectic path in phase space, from which the corresponding driving Hamiltonian can be reconstructed. We also discuss the generalization to arbitrary nonequilibrium initial states within the two-projective-measurement scheme for quantum work. The same construction yields Loschmidt echoes and mixed-state total phases. Using the derived characteristic function, we obtain formulas for the Helmholtz free energy, the Jarzynski equality, the mean work, the work variance, and the nonequilibrium lag. Undisplaced Hamiltonians, sudden quenches, constant-Hessian protocols, and the adiabatic limit follow as special cases of the general result. As an illustration, we analyze a one-dimensional system constructed from an unconventional symplectic path and exhibiting physically relevant limiting cases. We then show how affine and purely symplectic contributions affect work statistics and irreversibility.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces GAUGE, a formal framework to measure cryptographic security not by a single bit-count, but by a 'security profile' that maps across all possible adversary cost models. It mathematically proves that different cost models can lead to contradictory security rankings, providing an explicit, auditable method to quantify this model dependence. This is crucial for standards bodies to report security honestly when underlying assumptions about attacker resources are uncertain.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about physical quantum systems, the rigorous mathematical framework for uncertainty quantification and model dependence (risk measures, profile analysis) is highly relevant to any field where underlying assumptions or measurement models introduce systematic, non-Gaussian uncertainty.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Current cryptographic security reporting relies on a single scalar bit-strength number, which is misleading because it depends entirely on the unstated adversary cost model used for pricing resources like time and memory.

**Main result.** The authors introduce GAUGE, a framework that models security as a 'security profile' (a function over all admissible cost models), proving that ranking reversals are structural properties rather than measurement errors.

**Method.** The framework formalizes price functionals over a cone of cost models, proving that security profiles are piecewise-linear and concave, and uses linear programming to certify the robustness of security rankings.

**Model / system.** The model is abstract, dealing with cryptographic schemes and the space of admissible adversary cost models ($\kappa$). It defines security as a function of these models, leading to the concept of a 'security profile' $S_K(\kappa)$.

**Key observables.** Security profile, Rating Trilemma, Robust/Reversing/Incomparable ranking status, Lattice-sieving cost drift (e.g., 9.79 bits/year).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Adversary cost model parameters (time, memory, quantum resources); time horizons ($	au$); decay rates ($\delta$); and the structure of the cost cone.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the existence of a cone of admissible cost models and uses concepts like anchor-relative profiles to normalize comparisons.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually maps cost models to security profiles; Table 1 compares schemes under multiple cost models, showing varying relative security costs.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem with scalar reporting, introduces the GAUGE profile framework, proves mathematical properties (concavity, linearity), develops the linear-programming certification procedure, and concludes with concrete evaluations on PQC standards and risk measures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Standards bodies report cryptographic security as a single number of bits, but this value depends on the adversary cost model used to price time, memory, and quantum resources. Different conventions can therefore produce different rankings of cryptographic schemes. GAUGE represents security as a function over admissible cost models, called a security profile. Comparisons then become comparisons between profiles, and ranking reversals become an explicit structural property rather than a measurement error.   We formalize price functionals over a cone of adversary cost models, show that security profiles are piecewise-linear and concave, and prove a rating trilemma: when two profiles cross, no rating can simultaneously be faithful to underlying costs, total over comparable pairs, and independent of the chosen cost model. We provide a polynomial-time linear-programming procedure that certifies whether the ranking of two schemes is robust, reverses under admissible models, or is genuinely incomparable.   We extend GAUGE with a two-layer risk measure combining stochastic cryptanalytic decay with uncertainty over the appropriate cost model. We evaluate the framework on NIST post-quantum standards, classical anchors, and a 25-year chronology of cryptanalytic breaks. The analysis certifies a ranking reversal for ML-KEM-512 versus AES-128 from a 4-5% shift in memory pricing, and measures a lattice-sieving cost drift of 9.79 bits per year over eight years. A hybrid X25519 + ML-KEM-768 handshake reduces combined-break probability twenty-fold at a 2.3 kilobyte cost. The artifact reproduces all tables and figures in under seven seconds. GAUGE provides an explicit and auditable framework for reporting cryptographic security under competing cost models.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces SpiKIsing, a latent-variable model that separates the collective dynamics of neurons (modeled by an Ising process) from the continuous-time spiking process. It uses a variational EM scheme to jointly infer the underlying network couplings and emission parameters from raw spike data. The method is robustly validated, showing it can accurately recover network structure despite modeling mismatches.

**Why it may be interesting.** The framework uses concepts from statistical physics (Ising models, mean-field theory) to model complex, non-equilibrium biological dynamics, providing a rigorous mathematical structure for analyzing emergent collective behavior.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the inverse problem of inferring the directed effective interactions governing collective neuronal dynamics solely from recorded spike trains.

**Main result.** The proposed SpiKIsing model successfully recovers the sparse connection structure and correctly classifies neuronal types (excitatory/inhibitory) even when tested on data generated by different underlying dynamics.

**Method.** It employs a variational mean-field expectation-maximization (EM) scheme to jointly infer latent network activity (modeled by an Ising process) and continuous-time emission parameters from the observed spike trains.

**Model / system.** The system models neural spiking using a two-level latent variable approach: a discrete-time asymmetric kinetic Ising model governs the unobserved collective state, while continuous-time, history-dependent point processes generate the observed spikes conditioned on this latent state.

**Key observables.** Inferred coupling strengths (J), local fields (h), and emission parameters (e.g., $\lambda^+, \lambda^-$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Latent time step ($\Delta t$), regularization strengths ($\gamma_1, \gamma_2$), and prior parameters ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is using a variational mean-field approach, which treats the continuous-time point-process likelihood as an effective observation field for the discrete Ising dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the full generative mechanism, parameter recovery validation on synthetic data, and the final inferred interaction matrix compared to ground truth.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the latent variable model, derives the variational EM scheme, details the updates (including forward-only approximations), and validates the method using both synthetic and complex conductance-based LIF network simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inferring directed effective interactions from neuronal spike trains is a central inverse problem in statistical physics and computational neuroscience. Kinetic Ising models provide a tractable framework for this task, but their application to neural data typically requires binning spike trains into binary activity variables, discarding within-bin timing and conflating collective network dynamics with single-neuron history effects.   We introduce SpiKIsing, a latent-variable model that separates these two levels of description. A discrete-time asymmetric kinetic Ising model describes collective network activity, while continuous-time, history-dependent point processes generate the observed spikes conditional on the latent states, accounting explicitly for refractoriness and post-spike recovery.   We derive a variational mean-field expectation-maximization scheme in which the point-process likelihood enters as an effective observation field, enabling joint inference of latent activity, network couplings, and emission parameters. The framework extends naturally to maximum-a-posteriori inference with structured priors, including sparsity and a hierarchical extension favouring Dale-consistent outgoing interactions.   We validate parameter recovery on matched synthetic data and test the method on spike trains generated by a recurrent conductance-based leaky integrate-and-fire network. In this setting, SpiKIsing recovers sparse connection structure and correctly classifies all excitatory and inhibitory neurons despite the substantial mismatch between the data-generating dynamics and the SpiKIsing model.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Quantum Lattice Boltzmann Method (QLBM) that replaces the non-unitary BGK collision operator with a unitary rotation operator. This approach successfully addresses the unitarity constraint while maintaining second-order accuracy relative to the classical method. The framework is fully realized at the gate level, providing a pathway to simulate fluid dynamics on quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, unitary quantum circuit implementation for a classical computational method (LBM), which is highly relevant for simulating complex many-body dynamics on near-term quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a unitary quantum analogue for the non-unitary BGK collision operator used in the Lattice Boltzmann Method (LBM).

**Main result.** The authors propose a novel Quantum Lattice Boltzmann Method (QLBM) using a unitary rotation collision operator that correctly approximates the BGK collision to $O(\delta t^2)$ in the continuum limit.

**Method.** The non-unitary BGK relaxation is replaced by a unitary rotation in the amplitude space spanned by the current and equilibrium distribution states, leading to gate-level quantum circuit construction.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to solve fluid dynamics equations using the LBM framework, specifically implemented for the D2Q5 (convection-diffusion) and D2Q9 (Navier-Stokes) models.

**Key observables.** Relative velocity errors ($E_u$), mass conservation ($\sum f^*_i = ho$), and the contraction of the state deviation angle $\phi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relaxation time ($	au$), time step ($\delta t$), and the dimensionality/model (D2Q5 vs D2Q9).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the unitary analogue of the BGK collision is a rotation contracting the angle $\phi$ by a factor of $(1 - 1/	au)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sequential T-step quantum circuit for the D2Q5 model, showing state-dependent collision blocks interspersed with fixed streaming operators, and show error scaling confirming $O(\delta t^2)$ accuracy.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (non-unitary BGK), proposes the unitary rotation operator, derives its form for D2Q5 and D2Q9, details the gate-level circuit construction (including reusable components for D2Q5), and validates the method via numerical error analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the existing quantum algorithms for the lattice Boltzmann method, resolving the unitarity problem of the BGK collision operator from first principles remains a fundamental challenge, and a systematically constructed unitary alternative that reproduces the BGK relaxation structure to leading order has not yet been established in the literature. In this work, we propose a novel quantum lattice Boltzmann method (QLBM) based on the rotation collision operator, in which the non-unitary BGK relaxation is replaced by a unitary rotation in the amplitude space of the distribution function, and the corresponding gate-level quantum circuits are also constructed. Specifically, the rotation collision operator is developed for both the D2Q5 model of the convection-diffusion equation and the D2Q9 model of the incompressible Navier--Stokes equations. It is worth noting that, for the D2Q5 model, the equilibrium-state preparation circuit can be precompiled and reused, and the resulting unitary blocks can be sequentially composed once the state-dependent collision parameters are specified. Finally, some numerical experiments are performed to validate the developed QLBM, demonstrating its accuracy and effectiveness.

</details>

### [$d$-pod realization of nonadiabatic holonomic quantum computation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16216v1)

**Authors:** Oskar Axelsson, Claes Fälth, Elias Henriksson Lindberg, Erik Sjöqvist  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16216v1>

Right panel: The same system expressed in the dark-bright Morris-Shore [66] basis {|b⟩, |D1⟩, . . . , |Dd−1⟩}. Note that only the bright state |b⟩is coupled to |e⟩.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper generalizes Holonomic Quantum Computation to $d$-dimensional qudits using the $d$-pod atomic configuration. It shows that this setup enables universal quantum gate sets for qudits using only simple optical or microwave pulse sequences. The work is significant for its resource efficiency and its systematic extension of geometric quantum control principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, resource-efficient theoretical blueprint for scaling quantum computation beyond qubits, directly relevant to experimental quantum control using trapped atoms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the systematic generalization of nonadiabatic Holonomic Quantum Computation (HQC) from standard qubit systems to higher-dimensional qudits ($d$-level systems).

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the $d$-pod configuration allows for the realization of universal holonomic single- and two-qudit gates using only optical or microwave pulses on trapped atoms or ions.

**Method.** The computation relies on exploiting non-Abelian geometric phases accumulated during cyclic evolution in the $d$-pod system, analyzed using the Grassmannian manifold.

**Model / system.** The physical model is the $d$-pod configuration, consisting of a single excited state coupled to $d$ ground states forming the computational subspace. The dynamics are governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** The gate operation is derived from the time evolution operator $U_{kl}$, and the purely holonomic nature is confirmed by specific geometric conditions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required cyclic evolution condition is $\int_0^	au \Omega(t)dt = \pi$. The complexity increases with $d$, with the qutrit ($d=3$) case analyzed in detail.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of the rotating wave approximation and monochromatic fields, focusing on the $d$-pod generalization of the $\Lambda$-scheme.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the $d$-pod setup, showing the coupling between the excited state and the $d$ ground states, and also depicts the system in the dark-bright Morris-Shore basis.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the $d$-pod model, derives the conditions for holonomic evolution, and then details the construction of universal single- and two-qudit gates, providing explicit examples for the qutrit case.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Holonomic quantum computation (HQC) realizes quantum gates through non-Abelian geometric phases, providing an experimentally accessible approach to quantum control. While the nonadiabatic HQC framework has been extensively developed for three-level $Λ$ systems encoding qubits, its systematic extension to higher-dimensional qudits remains largely unexplored. In this work, we generalize nonadiabatic HQC to a $d$-pod configuration, where a single excited state is coupled to $d$ ground states, the latter forming the computational subspace. This scheme enables universal holonomic single- and two-qudit gates using only optical or microwave pulses on trapped atoms or ions, offering an efficient route to implement a discrete universal gate set with minimal pulse coordination. As an explicit example, we analyze in detail the qutrit ($d=3$) case, demonstrating compact realizations of single- and two-qutrit holonomic gates, each gate requiring at most two loops in the Grassmannian generated by at most three pulses.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the vulnerability of ADAPT-VQE to hardware noise, focusing particularly on the operator selection step. It demonstrates that both coherent and incoherent noise can halt convergence, but the combination of error mitigation techniques like dynamical decoupling, zero noise extrapolation, and Pauli twirling can successfully restore the algorithm's performance. This offers crucial guidelines for implementing quantum chemistry simulations on noisy quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the practical implementation challenges of quantum chemistry algorithms on near-term, noisy quantum hardware by providing a roadmap for error mitigation in the ansatz construction phase.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how hardware noise affects the critical operator selection step within Adaptive Variational Quantum Algorithms (ADAPT-VQE).

**Main result.** While noise can prevent convergence in ADAPT-VQE, combining appropriate quantum error mitigation techniques (like DD, ZNE, and PT) can successfully restore convergence and maintain performance.

**Method.** The study uses ADAPT-VQE to calculate ground state energies and gradient measurements for molecular Hamiltonians under various noise channels.

**Model / system.** The test case is a linear H3 molecule, simulated using a minimal STO-3G basis set represented by 6 qubits. The core methodology involves optimizing the expectation value using the VQE framework.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy (E0), gradient magnitudes, and the convergence profile of the optimization process.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($\gamma$ or $\phi$) tested at multiple levels (e.g., 0.01, 0.1, 0.3) for both incoherent and coherent noise channels.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses specifically on injecting noise into the gradient measurements used for operator selection, rather than the entire VQE process.

**Figures summary.** Figures show energy error plots and gradient heatmaps comparing performance with and without various error mitigation techniques (DD, ZNE, PT) across different noise types.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the impact of different noise channels (incoherent vs. coherent) on ADAPT-VQE, demonstrating the failure modes and then proposing and testing combinations of error mitigation techniques to restore convergence.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hardware noise has been shown to significantly impact the accuracy of ADAPT-VQE, a ground state preparation algorithm. While previous work has studied the impact of noise on its parameter optimization step, its impact on the critical operator selection step remains comparatively unexplored. In this work, we examine the impact of a variety of noise channels on this step, using a linear H$_3$ molecule as a test case. We show that, despite the selection criterion's natural resilience to some noise, both coherent and incoherent noise can prevent convergence for sufficiently high noise rates. We employ quantum error mitigation techniques--dynamical decoupling, zero noise extrapolation, and Pauli twirling--and show that when combined appropriately, these techniques are capable of restoring a successful convergence profile. Our results highlight how error mitigation can improve the performance of ADAPT-VQE and enable convergence in the presence of hardware noise, offering valuable insights into the implementation of the algorithm on near-term quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work quantifies 'quantum imaginarity,' a measure of nonreal coherence, in the quantum atmosphere of a black hole. By analyzing three different imaginarity metrics, the authors map how this resource is modulated by Hawking radiation. The findings reveal distinct radial profiles and show that the local Hawking effect drives a measurable redistribution of quantum information across the black hole's vicinity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of quantum resource theory (imaginarity) to characterize information structure in curved spacetime provides a novel diagnostic tool for understanding black hole thermodynamics and Hawking radiation effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the evolution and redistribution of 'quantum imaginarity'—a metric for intrinsic nonreal coherence—within the quantum atmosphere of a static Schwarzschild black hole.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that the relative-entropy, geometric, and robustness imaginarities exhibit characteristic radial profiles (valley/peak shapes) that are significantly modulated by the Hartle-Hawking constant, with the extremum positions tracking the local Hawking radiation peak.

**Method.** The study employs resource-theoretic measures of quantum imaginarity ($I_r, I_g, R$) applied to reduced bipartite states derived from a four-partite Dirac field state, analyzing their dependence on radial position and phase.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the quantum atmosphere surrounding a static Schwarzschild black hole, modeled using bipartite Dirac field reduced states evolving under the influence of Hawking thermal radiation.

**Key observables.** Relative-entropy imaginarity ($I_r$), Geometric imaginarity ($I_g$), and Robustness of imaginarity ($R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Black hole mass ($M$), event horizon radius ($r_h$), Hartle–Hawking constant, and normalized radial distance ($r/r_h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on bipartite Dirac field reduced states and assumes the initial state is a bipartite entangled pure state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate 3D plots and radial cross-sections of $I_r$ and $I_g$ for different state partitions, showing characteristic modulation patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical framework for calculating imaginarity measures for reduced states, analyzes the radial and phase dependence of these measures, and discusses the implications of the Hartle-Hawking constant on resource redistribution.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum imaginarity, as a critical resource metric for quantifying intrinsic nonreal coherence encoded in quantum states, exhibits nontrivial evolutionary behaviors in curved spacetime backgrounds. This work focuses on bipartite Dirac field reduced states in the quantum atmosphere of a static Schwarzschild black hole, aiming to explore the modulation of three mainstream imaginarity measures by Hawking thermal radiation. We reveal that the relative-entropy imaginarity, the geometric imaginarity and robustness of imaginarity, the fully accessible state presents a valley-shaped radial profile with a local minimum inside the quantum atmosphere, while the cross-coupling and fully inaccessible states follow opposite peak-shaped trends. Further analysis demonstrates that the Hartle-Hawking constant significantly strengthens the redistribution effect of imaginarity across both regions, whereas an increase in the event horizon radius weakens such redistribution and flattens the extremum features. These findings offer a new perspective for decoding the information structure of black hole quantum atmospheres and the intrinsic quantum nature of Hawking radiation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper adapts the Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) algorithm to solve the computationally demanding Hartree-Fock equations for very long chains. By compressing both the electronic state and the long-range interactions, the authors achieve near-linear scaling, making large-scale electronic structure calculations feasible on standard desktop hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates a powerful algorithmic adaptation of DMRG, extending its utility beyond standard correlated wavefunctions to tackle mean-field theories like HF for extended solids, which is crucial for simulating large-scale material properties.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational challenge of applying Hartree-Fock (HF) theory to very large electronic systems, which typically suffers from high polynomial scaling due to long-range interactions.

**Main result.** The authors successfully formulated HF as a Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) sweep, achieving near-linear scaling for chains up to 100,000 atoms in a feasible time frame.

**Method.** They adapt the DMRG framework by compressing the occupied state and the long-range electron-electron interaction separately, reducing the complex HF calculation into a sequence of small, local Fock problems.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on uniform antiferromagnetic hydrogen chains, retaining the physical Coulomb interaction and its $1/r$ tail, using a basis set of Gaussian-type orbitals.

**Key observables.** Convergence time (hours for $10^5$ atoms), memory usage ($\approx 21$ GB), and the linear scaling of computation time with chain length.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chain length up to 100,000 atoms; one-body tolerance of $10^{-8}$ Ha; local Fock matrix size remaining small ($\approx 45$ rows).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of the density-density electron-electron interaction approximation and the assumption that state compression (one-particle and MPO compression) is sufficient for accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the DMRG sweep process, showing the partitioning of the basis into blocks and the storage/retrieval of environments. Other figures detail time/memory scaling and energy convergence plots.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the formulation of HF as a DMRG sweep, describes the specific compression techniques (one-particle, MPO, density-space), presents computational results on scaling, and concludes with analysis of energy convergence and implementation details.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate Hartree--Fock as a density-matrix renormalization group (DMRG) sweep of a single Slater determinant, assuming a density--density electron--electron interaction. We compress the occupied state and long-range interaction separately, reducing each sweep step to a small local Fock problem. Exchange is retained throughout. Uniform antiferromagnetic hydrogen chains provide an almost ideal setting for this approach, while retaining the Coulomb interaction and its $1/r$ tail. We initialize and converge a chain of 100,000 electrons in 1.8 million spatial basis functions in about two hours on a 64-GB Mac mini, using less than a third of its memory. The work per sweep is nearly proportional to chain length, and the number of sweeps needed for convergence changes little from 1000 to 100,000 atoms. DMRG's compression of states and environments thus makes large HF calculations with long-range Hartree and exchange practical on an ordinary desktop computer.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses FRG calculations to map the impact of electron-phonon coupling on magnetism and superconductivity in trilayer nickelates. It finds that the specific out-of-plane oxygen breathing modes create layer-dependent interactions that selectively enhance or suppress competing electronic orders. This establishes a microscopic framework for understanding the observed isotope effects in these high-temperature superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of screening-mediated spin–phonon interplay provides a detailed, microscopic mechanism for how lattice vibrations can tune competing electronic orders, which is relevant to understanding quantum criticality in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how electron-phonon coupling (EPC) influences the competing electronic orders—magnetism (SDW) and superconductivity ($s_\pm$-wave)—in trilayer nickelates.

**Main result.** The relative phases of EPC across the three layers generate distinct, mode-selective retarded interactions that can enhance or suppress SDW order and superconductivity.

**Method.** The research employs ab initio calculations combined with the Functional Renormalization Group (FRG) formalism to treat phonon-mediated and Coulomb interactions simultaneously.

**Model / system.** The system is trilayer nickelates, modeled using a tight-binding approach incorporating various Coulomb interactions. The analysis focuses on out-of-plane oxygen breathing modes which exhibit pronounced EPC.

**Key observables.** Spin-Density-Wave (SDW) order, $s_\pm$-wave superconducting pairing, isotope coefficients ($\alpha_{SC}, \alpha_{SDW}$), and the Eliashberg spectral function ($\alpha^2F(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High pressure ($P=40 	ext{ GPa}$), specific phonon frequencies (e.g., $553 	ext{ cm}^{-1}, 651 	ext{ cm}^{-1}$), and the relative phases of the EPC across the three layers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the full frequency dependence onto static channels within the FRG framework, and the model incorporates specific layer-dependent coupling terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phonon dispersion and EPC strength, while supplementary figures visualize the evolution of superconducting order parameter components and the calculation of isotope coefficients ($\Delta\Lambda_c$ and $\alpha$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by identifying dominant phonon modes (A1g out-of-plane modes), constructing symmetry-resolved EPC terms, and then using FRG to calculate how these terms modify the electronic interactions, leading to mode-selective predictions for SDW and SC.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of high-$T_c$ superconductivity in multilayer nickelates has stimulated intense interest, yet the microscopic origins of their correlated states remain debated. Recently the observed isotope effect of the magnetic state in trilayer nickelates highlights the important role of lattice vibrations in shaping these systems. Here, we investigate the impact of electron-phonon coupling (EPC) on magnetism and superconductivity in trilayer nickelates using ab initio and functional renormalization group calculations. We identify out-of-plane oxygen breathing modes with pronounced EPC and construct symmetry-resolved, layer-dependent couplings. Their relative phases across the three layers generate distinct retarded interactions, producing mode-selective enhancement or suppression of spin-density-wave order and $s_\pm$-wave superconductivity. This selectivity arises from screening-mediated spin-phonon interplay across a hierarchy of energy scales: EPC modifies charge screening at higher energies, reshapes magnetic fluctuations at lower energies, and ultimately influences pairing. Selected modes yield negative isotope coefficients for the magnetic state, qualitatively consistent with experiments. Our results establish how interlayer phonons modify electronically driven order and provide a microscopic framework for understanding isotope effects in trilayer nickelates.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** The authors introduce Min-relative Nonlocal Magic ($	ext{DNL}_{\min}$) as a novel diagnostic tool to characterize the transition from ergodic to Many-Body Localization (MBL) in disordered quantum chains. They show that $	ext{DNL}_{\min}$ captures structural information about entanglement, revealing distinct scaling behaviors in the MBL regime that are missed by standard entanglement entropy. This provides a deeper understanding of ergodicity breaking in complex quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, quantitative tool ($	ext{DNL}_{\min}$) to probe the underlying structure of entanglement (Schmidt spectrum) in strongly correlated and disordered systems, offering complementary insights to standard entropy measures relevant to open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop diagnostics beyond entanglement entropy to characterize the crossover from ergodic to Many-Body Localization (MBL) in interacting quantum systems.

**Main result.** The proposed measure, Min-relative Nonlocal Magic (NLM), reveals a spectral reorganization in the Schmidt spectrum that is invisible to entanglement entropy, showing distinct scaling behaviors in the ergodic and MBL regimes.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the minimum-relative NLM ($	ext{DNL}_{\min}$) for a disordered XXZ chain and comparing its scaling properties across different disorder strengths ($W$) and system sizes ($L$).

**Model / system.** The primary system is the random-field XXZ chain, $H = J \sum (\dots) + \sum h_i S^z_i$, studied in the half-filling sector. The analysis also utilizes a minimal two-configuration cut-hybridization model to derive analytical scaling laws.

**Key observables.** Min-relative Nonlocal Magic ($	ext{DNL}_{\min}$), Von Neumann entropy ($S_A$), and state-resolved probability distributions $	ext{P}_X(x)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder Strength ($W$), System Size ($L$), and the crossover between ergodic and MBL regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing the behavior of $	ext{DNL}_{\min}$ to established scaling laws for entanglement entropy and utilizes effective low-energy models (e.g., two-level Hamiltonian) to explain decay rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of $	ext{DNL}_{\min}$ and $S_A$ versus disorder strength $W$, showing the dome formation for $	ext{DNL}_{\min}$ versus the volume-to-area law transition for $S_A$. Other figures detail quench dynamics and state-resolved probability distributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces $	ext{DNL}_{\min}$ as a diagnostic tool, analyzes its behavior across the disorder landscape (weak $	o$ intermediate $	o$ strong), derives scaling laws in the deep MBL regime using minimal models, and finally examines quench dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlocal magic quantifies the minimum nonstabilizerness attainable under independent local unitary transformations on the two subsystems. Here, we use min-relative nonlocal magic (NLM) to characterize the crossover from ergodicity to many-body localization (MBL) in the random-field XXZ chain. Unlike entanglement entropy, NLM probes how entanglement is organized through the distance of the Schmidt spectrum from dyadic-flat stabilizer spectra. From weak to intermediate disorder, NLM evolves from an $O(1)$ Haar-like value into a size-enhanced dome, while entanglement remains volume-law, revealing a spectral reorganization not visible in the entropy. Deep in the MBL regime, a two-level cut-hybridization model captures the nearly binary Schmidt spectrum and explains why the mean and median NLM decay approximately as $W^{-1}$ and $W^{-2}$, respectively. Following a product-state quench, NLM overshoots and relaxes in the ergodic regime, whereas at strong disorder it grows slowly and approximately logarithmically. These results show that NLM resolves Schmidt-spectrum structure not visible in the entanglement entropy and provides a complementary probe of ergodicity breaking.

</details>

**Authors:** Addison Ballif, Mark Kempton, James B. Larsen, Kellon Sandall, Christino Tamon, Trevor Wai  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16549v1>

**Summary.** This paper establishes conditions for achieving Pretty Good State Transfer (PGST) in quantum walks on complex graphs. It achieves this by constructing the graph as a rooted product involving a circulant graph and a path weighted by a transcendental number. The work demonstrates that the transcendental nature of the weight is crucial for guaranteeing robust quantum connectivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The reliance on transcendental numbers to guarantee quantum phenomena like PGST provides a deep connection between number theory and quantum dynamics, which is highly relevant for understanding robustness in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions under which Pretty Good State Transfer (PGST) can be achieved between nodes in a graph constructed via a rooted product.

**Main result.** It proves that attaching a path with a transcendental weight on its end edge to a circulant graph with universal PST results in PGST between the path's endpoints.

**Method.** The analysis uses spectral decomposition, rooted product constructions, and advanced mathematical tools including orthogonal polynomials and Galois theory to derive necessary congruence conditions for state transfer.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum walk on a weighted graph $Z$, constructed as a rooted product of a circulant graph $X$ (assumed to have universal PST) and a path $P_{\alpha}^m$. The dynamics are governed by the unitary evolution $U(t) = e^{-itA}$.

**Key observables.** Pretty Good State Transfer (PGST), defined by the existence of a time $	au$ such that the transition amplitude $| \langle u|U(	au)|vangle | > 1 - \epsilon$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The transcendental weight $\alpha$ on the end edge of the path, and the eigenvalues $\lambda_k$ of the base circulant graph $X$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The base graph $X$ is assumed to be a circulant graph with universal PST, and the weight $\alpha$ must be transcendental over the rationals.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a graph structure related to the condition for universal PST. Figure 2 visually demonstrates the PGST result for a path with an even number of vertices using a transcendental weight $\alpha$.

**Paper structure.** The paper begins with preliminaries on state transfer and graph products, proceeds to analyze the structure of the adjacency matrix of the rooted product, derives the characteristic polynomial, and culminates in proving the PGST conditions using algebraic and number-theoretic arguments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that if we take a rooted product of a circulant graph with universal perfect state transfer with a path of fixed length whose end edge is weighted with a transcendental number, then there is pretty good state transfer between any pair of endpoints of these paths. As a consequence, in a path with an even number of vertices with transcendental weights on the two edges incident to the endpoints, there is pretty good state transfer.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies Real-Space Renormalization Group techniques to analyze the non-stabilizerness of quantum states in 1D spin chains, such as the TFIM and XY model. By calculating the Stabilizer Rényi Entropy (SRE), the authors quantify 'quantum magic' and demonstrate how this measure reveals the location and nature of quantum phase transitions. This provides a powerful, entanglement-based diagnostic tool for condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of entanglement measures like SRE within a powerful RG framework provides a novel diagnostic tool to characterize quantum phases and critical behavior in 1D quantum magnets, connecting entanglement structure directly to critical exponents.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the non-stabilizerness of quantum states in spin chains and analyzes how this 'quantum magic' evolves under coarse-graining.

**Main result.** The Real-Space Renormalization Group (RSRG) successfully provides closed-form expressions for the Stabilizer Rényi Entropy (SRE) and long-range SRE, allowing the study of quantum phase transitions.

**Method.** The primary method is the Real-Space Renormalization Group (RSRG) applied to entanglement measures, specifically the Stabilizer Rényi Entropy (SRE), to study system behavior across different length scales.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on one-dimensional quantum spin chains, specifically the Transverse-Field Ising Model (TFIM) and the spin-1/2 periodic integrable XY chain. Hamiltonians are provided for both models.

**Key observables.** Stabilizer Rényi Entropy (SRE), long-range SRE, and critical exponents ($
u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-energy, large-distance regime; quantum critical point (QCP); magnetic field ($h$) and coupling constants ($J, \gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The RSRG method is used to derive closed-form expressions, and the analysis often focuses on the low-lying spectrum or specific limits of the system.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the RSRG procedure for both TFIM and XY models, showing the block Hamiltonian and inter-block interactions. Other figures plot SRE vs. magnetic field or vs. RG steps to locate critical points.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces SRE as a measure of non-stabilizerness, details the RSRG procedure for both TFIM and XY chains, applies this procedure to calculate renormalized parameters and SRE, and finally analyzes the scaling behavior of these quantities near quantum critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stabilizer states constitute an important class of quantum states that can be generated from computational-basis states using Pauli operators and Clifford gates. Although they may exhibit substantial multipartite entanglement, quantum circuits restricted to stabilizer operations can be efficiently simulated classically and therefore cannot, by themselves, provide a quantum computational advantage. Such an advantage requires non-stabilizer resources, commonly referred to as quantum magic. In this paper, we investigate the non-stabilizerness of quantum states arising in a class of spin Hamiltonians by computing their stabilizer Rényi entropies. Using real-space renormalization-group techniques, we obtain a closed-form expression valid in the low-energy, large-distance regime. We analyze how quantum magic evolves under coarse-graining and explore its behavior across different parameter regimes and quantum phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes how conditioning a Branching Random Walk (a model for log-correlated Gaussian fields) on its endpoints being confined to a small region affects its correlations. Using Renormalization Group techniques, the authors prove that this conditioning induces an exponential decay of correlations with a characteristic length scale proportional to the confinement radius $r$. The results are generalized to important physical models like the sinh-Gordon and $\phi^4$ theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methods developed for analyzing correlation decay under strong spatial constraints (small ball events) are highly relevant for understanding critical phenomena and confinement in quantum field theories, which often involve Gaussian or log-correlated fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the correlation structure of a Branching Random Walk (BRW) when conditioned on all its endpoints lying within a small interval (a 'small ball event').

**Main result.** The conditioning leads to an exponential decay of correlations between the endpoints, with an optimal correlation length of $O(r)$.

**Method.** The core technique employed is the Renormalization Group (RG) analysis, which separates the effect of endpoint conditioning into relevant and irrelevant parts.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the BRW, which serves as a prototypical log-correlated Gaussian field and a mathematical stand-in for the Discrete Gaussian Free Field (DGFF). Generalizations apply to the sinh-Gordon model and $\phi^4$ models.

**Key observables.** Correlation $	ext{Cov}(\phi_{	au_1}, \phi_{	au_2})$ and the correlation length.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The radius $r$ of the small ball event, the depth $n$ of the walk, and the potential parameters ($\lambda, \alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The conditioning on $\|\Phi\|_\infty \le r$ is an extremely low-probability event, and the analysis relies on separating the RG flow into relevant and irrelevant components.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the BRW conditioned on endpoints in $[-r, r]$, showing the initial steps behave like a branching Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) walk.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the main result for the BRW using RG, then generalizes this result to other hierarchical field theories, specifically applying it to the sinh-Gordon model and generalized $\phi^4$ models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Consider a branching random walk (BRW) of depth $n$ with standard Gaussian increments, conditioned on all endpoints lying in an interval of radius $r$ (which may depend on $n$), i.e. a small ball event. We prove that this conditioning results in exponential decay of correlations between the endpoints, with correlation length $O(r)$. Our proof is based on the renormalization group, which in this context captures the effect of the endpoint conditioning on earlier steps in the walk. This effect can be separated into so-called relevant and irrelevant parts, and in our setting the relevant part makes the conditioned BRW behave like a branching Ornstein-Uhlenbeck walk for the first $n-O(r)$ steps, leading to the correlation decay. The irrelevant part shrinks in a nontrivial manner, and the simplest argument to bound it uniformly only yields correlation length $O(r^2)$. To achieve the optimal $O(r)$ bound, we establish that the irrelevant part shrinks horizontally in space before decaying uniformly.   The BRW is a prototypical example of a log-correlated Gaussian field, and it forms the basis of many hierarchical field theories in the mathematical formulation of quantum field theory. As such, we present our results in a general form which is applicable to other hierarchical field theories with confining potentials, of which the small ball conditioned BRW is an example. We apply our general result to two other models: first, the sinh-Gordon model which has a strictly convex potential, so at the level of covariances the model behaves like one with a quadratic potential, i.e. the model has a positive mass. Second, we consider generalizations of the $φ^4$ model with any power $α\geq 2$; as $α$ increases from $2$ to $\infty$, these power potentials naturally interpolate between the massive (quadratic) potential and the infinite square well potential of the small ball event.

</details>

For n uses, the optical splitters act modewise. For a pure input on RAn, the state before the balanced splitters in panel (b) is pure on RF nGn, so RF n...</sub>
