**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves a strong converse for the quantum capacity of the pure-loss bosonic channel. It shows that if the transmission rate exceeds the channel capacity, the achievable fidelity of any quantum code decays polynomially with the block length. The work employs advanced techniques from quantum information theory, linking channel loss to fundamental bounds on quantum state estimation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, quantitative bounds on the fundamental limits of quantum communication through lossy optical channels, which is crucial for quantum networking and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a strong converse for the unconstrained quantum capacity of the pure-loss bosonic channel.

**Main result.** It proves that for any rate above capacity, the entanglement-generation fidelity of any code decays polynomially ($O(1/n)$) with the number of channel uses $n$.

**Method.** The proof combines quantum Chebyshev and hockey-stick testing inequalities with a uniform relative-entropy-variance bound, reducing the general case to the balanced-channel setting.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the pure-loss bosonic channel, which describes optical attenuation via a beam-splitter mixing an input mode with a vacuum mode.

**Key observables.** Entanglement-generation fidelity of codes, the rate gap ($\delta_n$), and the quantum capacity ($q_\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transmissivity ($\eta$), the number of channel uses ($n$), and the rate ($\log_2 M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof is for the *unconstrained* quantum capacity and establishes a polynomial decay rate, not an exponential one.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates two equivalent vacuum-fed beam-splitter dilations for $1/2 < \eta < 1$, relating the pure-loss model to a balanced beam splitter.

**Paper structure.** The argument develops by first establishing tools like the logarithmic trace inequality, then analyzing the balanced-channel state, and finally extending the bound to general transmissivities and arbitrary input states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper reports the proof of a strong converse for the unconstrained quantum capacity of the pure-loss bosonic channel. At every fixed rate above capacity, the entanglement-generation fidelity of every code is bounded by a constant times the reciprocal of the number of channel uses. The bound holds without an energy constraint and for arbitrary encoded states, including states correlated across all input modes, and arbitrary joint decoders. The proof combines quantum Chebyshev and hockey-stick testing inequalities with a uniform relative-entropy-variance bound for the balanced pure-loss channel, corresponding to transmissivity $η=1/2$. The variance bound follows by expressing the balanced beam splitter in bright and dark modes: the dark modes are exactly in vacuum, and any state orthogonal to that vacuum contains at least one dark photon. For general transmissivity, dilating the degrading attenuator reduces the problem to this balanced-channel setting and bounds the decoder test by precisely the factor that produces the known quantum-capacity threshold. The resulting argument establishes the strong converse at the unconstrained quantum capacity for every pure-loss bosonic channel.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how molecular spin coherence decays in photoexcited triplet states using a host-guest molecular system. By applying advanced cluster-correlation expansion methods, the authors pinpoint that decoherence is dominated by hyperfine interactions from the guest molecule. The findings provide quantitative guidelines for synthetically enhancing the coherence time of these promising molecular quantum probes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed, first-principles insights into decoherence in solid-state molecular qubits, which is crucial for designing robust quantum sensors and quantum information devices based on molecular spin states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the decoherence mechanisms limiting the spin coherence time ($T_2$) of optically readable molecular photoexcited triplet states.

**Main result.** Zero-field decoherence is primarily driven by hyperfine-mediated interactions originating from the guest molecule, while the coherence time shows a linear dependence on the zero-field splitting parameter $D$.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles calculations using the generalized Cluster-Correlation Expansion (gCCE) method to model the time evolution of the spin coherence function.

**Model / system.** The system is a host-guest crystal (pentacene guest in a p-terphenyl host) containing optically addressable molecular spin qubits. The Hamiltonian includes terms for zero-field splitting, electronic Zeeman interaction, and hyperfine/dipole-dipole interactions with surrounding nuclear spins.

**Key observables.** Spin coherence time ($T_2$), coherence function $L(t)$, and the decoherence rate ($T_2^{-1}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero-field splitting parameters ($D$ and $E$), hyperfine coupling constants ($A_{ij}$), and the magnetic field regime (zero to high field).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The triplet lifetime is assumed to be much longer than $T_2$, and the analysis relies on the point-dipole approximation for nuclear spin interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system setup, the Hahn-echo sequence, and show the dependence of $T_2$ on parameters like $D$ (linear scaling) and $E$ (weak scaling), confirming the dominance of guest-related decoherence.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the decoherence mechanism by first establishing the theoretical framework using gCCE, then isolating the contributions of guest vs. host nuclei, and finally performing scaling analyses to determine the functional dependence of $T_2$ on key molecular parameters like $D$ and $E$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optically readable molecular spins are attractive as quantum sensors due to their nanoscale modularity, synthetic tunability, and scope for sensitive readout. In particular, photoexcited spin-triplet states in organic molecules are appealing in supporting high optical-spin contrast at room temperature. Their utility is underpinned by their coherence, which warrants a detailed understanding of it. Here, from first principles, we systematically explore the Hahn-echo decoherence of the benchmark molecular system for room-temperature optically detected spin coherence---pentacene guest molecules coupled to a para-terphenyl host. Using generalized cluster-correlation expansion methods, we investigate the mechanisms of nuclear-spin-induced decoherence from zero to high magnetic field, exploring the role of guest vs host molecules, specific nuclei, zero-field splitting interactions, and hyperfine parameters. We describe how zero-field decoherence is driven by ~6 nuclei on the guest, while high-field decoherence is driven by ~600 nuclei in the host; how the longitudinal zero-field splitting parameter, $D$, can prolong $T_2$; and the magnetic-field-dependent processes which drive decoherence. These results advance our understanding of decoherence in optically readable molecular spins, providing insight for their synthetic enhancement and deployment as quantum probes.

</details>

### [Why Are We Unrolling? The Importance of Structured Quantum Programs for Compilation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16171v1)

**Authors:** Damian Rovara, Daniel Haag, Mark Koch, Josh Izaac, Seyon Sivarajah, Robert Wille, Augustín Borgna, Lukas Burgholzer, Brad Chase, Olivia Di Matteo, David Ittah  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16171v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.16171_figures/2609.16171_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 1: Preserving program structure can enable constant com- pilation times, even when the problem instance size increases. On the other hand, unrolling loops generally leads to polyno- mial or even exponentially-increasing program size. Programs used for this comparison were generated using mqt-cc [25]. See section IV for details on benchmark programs.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the scalability bottleneck in quantum compilation by arguing that current methods relying on unrolling quantum programs into straight-line circuits are insufficient for advanced algorithms. The authors introduce the concept of structured quantum programs, which explicitly model classical control flow and dynamism. They challenge the community to develop compilers that natively support this structure for efficient execution on future fault-tolerant hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on compilation theory, the need for robust, structured compilation is foundational for realizing any complex quantum simulation or algorithm targeting physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum compilation must evolve beyond straight-line circuits to efficiently support dynamic algorithms required for future fault-tolerant quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The paper argues that preserving the classical structure and dynamism of quantum programs, rather than unrolling them, is crucial for scalable compilation.

**Method.** The authors propose a formal definition of structured quantum programs and analyze the limitations of current compilation frameworks in supporting these dynamic elements.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the compilation process for quantum algorithms, treating the program itself as the primary model, which must support primitives like mid-circuit measurement feedforward and runtime-dependent control flow.

**Key observables.** Program size (qubits) and number of operations, showing that structured representations scale better than unrolled circuits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity of the algorithm (e.g., GHZ, Shor's) and the degree of structural dynamism (e.g., loop bounds, measurement dependencies).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The assumption is that future quantum hardware and algorithms will necessitate dynamic control flow, and that compilation tools must adapt to this structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares program size for unrolled vs. structured circuits, showing constant scaling for the latter. Table I lists key structured primitives, and Table IV benchmarks various algorithms against required structural features.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of scaling quantum compilation, defines structured quantum programs using primitives (Tier 1 and Tier 2), surveys current framework support, and concludes by presenting a benchmark challenge to the community.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum software stacks scale up to support future fault-tolerant quantum hardware and algorithms, quantum compilation is becoming an increasingly important component of the stack. How do we ensure that our software stacks support dynamic algorithms, including patterns such as mid-circuit measurement feedforward and repeat-until-success, with hundreds of logical qubits and billions of quantum operations? To do so, we must re-think how we represent quantum programs beyond straight-line circuits, to representations that include classical structure and dynamism, and make this the default representation to consider when performing quantum compilation. In this work, we present important patterns and algorithms from fault-tolerant quantum applications which admit a structured representation that we argue is crucial to preserve, and set a challenge to the community to compile such representations without unrolling them into straight-line quantum circuits. We also explore the status quo of structured program support in quantum software, and ask ourselves the rhetorical question: how much more efficient can we make quantum compilation tooling when we take into account the additional information from classical structure?

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the absolute storage capacity of a dense associative memory when the stored patterns exhibit a systematic bias. It finds that this bias fundamentally changes the scaling of the capacity compared to unbiased memories, predicting a distinct crossover regime. The work uses advanced statistical physics techniques to quantify these capacity limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical neural networks, the analysis of capacity limits and stability in the presence of systematic biases mirrors theoretical challenges in quantum error correction and the robustness of quantum information storage against correlated noise.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how introducing a bias in the stored patterns affects the absolute capacity of a dense associative memory, contrasting this with previously studied unbiased cases.

**Main result.** For fixed bias ($q<1/2$), the capacity scales differently ($O(N^{n/2})$ or $O(N^{(n+1)/2})$) than the unbiased case ($O(N^{n-1}/\ln N)$), predicting a bias-induced crossover region.

**Method.** The analysis uses signal-to-noise theory, conditioning on the number of biased sites, and employing Gaussian approximations for the crosstalk contributions in the large-N limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a dense associative memory modeled by an energy function involving polynomial interactions of order $n$ on centered binary patterns, where the bias is controlled by the probability $q$ that a component takes the value $1-q$.

**Key observables.** Absolute capacity ($K_{\max}$), single-site error probability ($P_{	ext{error}}$), and the crossover width.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction order $n$, bias parameter $q$, and system size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the single-site error criterion ($P_{	ext{error}}=1/N$) and approximations such as the Gaussian distribution for the sum of crosstalk terms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the empirical distributions of the energy gap ($\Delta E_{\mu i}$) for different stored symbols, showing separation between conditional distributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the capacity analysis by first establishing the energy gap decomposition, then deriving asymptotic expressions for $K_{\max}$ for $q=1/2$ and fixed $q<1/2$, and finally using asymptotic matching to predict the crossover region and the effect of activity control.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The absolute capacity of dense associative memory has mainly been analyzed for unbiased patterns. Here we examine the effect of bias in centered binary patterns under the Krotov-Hopfield single-site criterion $P_{\mathrm{error}}=1/N$, where $P_{\mathrm{error}}$ is the probability that a single-site flip lowers the energy of a stored pattern and $N$ is the number of neurons. Each pattern component takes $1-q$ with probability $q$ and $-q$ otherwise, where $0<q\le1/2$. For polynomial interactions of order $n$, a signal-to-noise analysis gives an absolute capacity of order $N^{n-1}/\ln N$ at $q=1/2$. For fixed $q<1/2$, however, the capacity is $O(N^{n/2})$ for even $n\ge4$ and $O(N^{(n+1)/2})$ for odd $n\ge5$. For $n=3$, both the unbiased and fixed-bias capacities remain $O(N^2/\ln N)$. For $n\ge4$, these different asymptotic forms imply a nonuniform large-$N$ limit near $q=1/2$. Asymptotic matching predicts a bias-induced crossover in the region $1-2q=O(\ln N/N^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor-1})$. The crossover originates from a bias-dependent crosstalk mean that reduces the stability of sites carrying the more frequent value $-q$. Computer simulations are compared with the finite-size conditioned-Gaussian predictions. An activity-dependent control potential that cancels the conditional crosstalk mean restores the $N^{n-1}/\ln N$ capacity for fixed $0<q<1/2$ within the conditioned-Gaussian approximation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel quantum communication protocol that merges classical error-correcting codes with superdense coding to enhance robustness against photon loss and dark counts. By analyzing the system's performance over short-distance quantum links, the authors demonstrate that their joint coding approach surpasses the theoretical limits of conventional error-corrected superdense coding. This makes it highly relevant for developing practical Quantum Local Area Networks (QLANs).

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of quantum communication by integrating classical coding theory to enhance performance metrics like rate and efficiency in near-term quantum network architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a robust quantum communication protocol that integrates classical error-control coding, entanglement distribution, and superdense coding to combat channel impairments like dark counts and photon losses.

**Main result.** The proposed joint classical-quantum coding protocol can exceed the upper bounds derived for conventional error-corrected superdense coding in terms of both data rate and energy efficiency.

**Method.** The protocol uses classical error-correcting codes to protect data encoded via superdense coding across quantum channels, deriving conditions for successful error correction under a bounded error-frequency model.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as short-distance quantum links, specifically suited for Quantum Local Area Networks (QLAN), operating under physical error sources like fiber attenuation, detector efficiency, and dark counts.

**Key observables.** Data rate (R), Energy efficiency (E), and the effective rate ($R_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fiber length (L), detector efficiency ($E_{det}$), dark count rate ($	au$), and the structure of the employed error-correcting codes (e.g., Hamming, BCH).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes perfect EPR pair generation and is primarily limited to short-distance quantum links due to attenuation being the dominant impairment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the encoding scheme, compare the proposed protocol's performance against superdense coding upper bounds, and show sensitivity analyses (Tornado diagrams, heat maps) regarding physical channel parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the joint protocol, derives performance bounds and conditions on codes, develops a physical error model, and finally compares the resulting effective rate against established superdense coding limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a robust quantum communication protocol that integrates classical error-control coding, entanglement distribution, and superdense coding. Classical error-correcting codes are used to mitigate dark counts and photon losses by determining the positions of qubit transmissions and protecting the data embedded through superdense coding. We derive conditions on the employed codes that guarantee successful error correction under a bounded error-frequency model. Moreover, upper bounds are derived on the performance of conventional superdense coding protected by classical error correction. It is shown that, under the same constraints on error frequency, suitable code configurations of the proposed protocol can exceed those upper bounds both in terms of data rate and energy efficiency. Finally, we develop a physical error model based on fiber attenuation, detector efficiency, dark counts, and time-slot duration, and use it to evaluate the effective performance of different configurations of error-correcting codes. The proposed approach is primarily suited for short-distance quantum links, as in Quantum Local Area Network (QLAN) where it can provide high communication throughput while integrating entanglement distribution directly into the communication process.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a systematic, recursive framework for designing quantum state distillation protocols necessary for fault-tolerant quantum computation. By utilizing code doubling and qubit recycling techniques, the authors construct families of protocols that achieve high code distance with minimal resource overhead. This work advances the theoretical understanding of resource management in quantum error correction.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on resource optimization (qubit recycling) and achieving high-distance distillation protocols is highly relevant for scaling up quantum computation architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the limitation that quantum error-correcting codes alone cannot provide a universal set of fault-tolerant logical gates, necessitating high-fidelity magic state injection.

**Main result.** The authors provide a recursive family of distillation protocols that can produce diagonal states at arbitrary high distance on a fixed number of logical qubits, significantly reducing resource overhead via qubit recycling.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing and analyzing families of weakly r-orthogonal matrices using code doubling techniques and algebraic proofs over F_2.

**Model / system.** The model is based on quantum error-correcting codes and the distillation of magic states (like the |T> state). The mathematical framework uses r-orthogonal matrices G to characterize the resource requirements.

**Key observables.** Distance (d), Support (s), Depth (n), and the minimal recycled qubit support (s*).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The orthogonality level r, the distance d, and the number of output states k.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis primarily focuses on Z-type errors sufficient for stabilizer codes, and the construction relies on the availability of lower-level Clifford operations.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a Table I comparing parameters (s, s*, n, d, effective overhead) of the constructed G(r,d) protocols against known literature protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the problem using r-orthogonal matrices, then introducing the code-doubling construction (Theorem 1), establishing a recursive family construction, and finally proving the properties (orthogonality, distance, support bounds) of the resulting matrices.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In order to circumvent that no universal set of gates can be achieved on a given quantum error-correcting codes, additional gates can be performed through magic state injection. This however requires to have access to high fidelity magic state, which can be obtained by the distillation of low quality states to fewer higher quality ones. In this paper, we provide a family of distillation protocols able to produce diagonal states at every level of the Clifford hierarchy. This family is obtained recursively using code doubling techniques and leveraging qubit recycling, the fact that some idle qubits of the protocols can be measured and re-used. At a given level of the Clifford hierarchy, we show that the protocols we derive can be performed at arbitrary high distance on a fix number of logical qubits. This family recovers many known efficient protocols, and uncovers new ones, such as a $111 \to 1$ protocol for $|T\rangle$ state distillation at distance $7$, which are the most compact in term of volume. Finally, we discuss possible extension of this framework for distillation protocols producing more than one output state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously compares Floquet Theory and Average Hamiltonian Theory for periodically driven quantum systems, using the Floquet-Magnus Expansion to derive higher-order terms for both. The authors conclude that the Floquet-Van Vleck approach is superior for numerical calculations in applications like NMR spectroscopy. It provides a unified theoretical understanding of how these methods relate while offering practical recommendations for implementation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, systematic comparison between two cornerstone theoretical tools (AHT and Floquet theory) used to analyze time-periodic quantum dynamics, offering practical guidance on which formalism yields the best numerical results for complex systems like those in NMR.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a detailed mathematical analysis comparing the equivalence, convergence, and practical application of Floquet Theory and Average Hamiltonian Theory (AHT) for periodically driven quantum systems.

**Main result.** The Floquet-Van Vleck (FVV) approach is recommended over AHT because it is numerically more robust and accurate, despite the formal equivalence of the theories in the infinite limit.

**Method.** The authors utilize the Floquet-Magnus Expansion (FME) to derive systematic, higher-order terms for both AHT and FVV, avoiding explicit time integration.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to periodically driven quantum systems, with a primary focus on Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectroscopy, modeling the time evolution governed by a time-periodic Hamiltonian H(t).

**Key observables.** Effective Hamiltonians (F), the kick operator, time evolution operators, and spectroscopic shifts like the Bloch-Siegert shift.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The driving period T, the ratio of driving frequency to system energies, and the order of the perturbation expansion (up to fourth order).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mathematical framework of the Floquet-Magnus expansion and assumes the validity of perturbative expansions in the driving frequency.

**Figures summary.** Figure 13 shows histograms comparing the relative differences of operators calculated with and without the kick operator for 2-spin and 3-spin MAS systems, highlighting differences between random and MAS Hamiltonians.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general framework using the FME, derives explicit formulas for higher-order terms in both FVV and AHT, and then validates these formulas and compare their predictions using specific NMR examples like the Bloch-Siegert shift.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

An accurate theoretical treatment of periodically driven quantum systems is crucial for various fields in the exact sciences, for instance Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectroscopy. Conventionally, either average Hamiltonian theory or Floquet theory is used to predict or describe experimental outcomes, such as the time evolution or the spectra yielding the information of the sample under study. A detailed analysis of the equivalence of these two approaches with an emphasis on applications in NMR will help to improve the theoretical understanding of NMR experiments.   In this work, we identify the Floquet-Magnus expansion as essential to prove the mathematical equivalence of Floquet theory and average Hamiltonian theory. We advocate a calculation scheme which is less prone to algebraic mistakes because explicit integration is avoided. On this basis, we provide the first four orders of both theories. We further examine their applicability to some experiments in NMR. As examples, we investigate the Bloch-Siegert shift and dipolar coupled spin systems under magic-angle spinning.   Based on our analysis, we recommend the use of the Floquet-Van Vleck approach including both the effective Hamiltonian and the kick operator. The consistent separation of secular and non-secular contributions appears to be especially advantageous for numerical robustness. Its accuracy is about three times better than the one provided by average Hamiltonian theory despite their formal equivalence.   Our findings provide important insights into the theoretical background of Floquet theory and average Hamiltonian theory. This includes the extent of their algebraic and perturbative equivalence, with an emphasis on how these findings are of relevance in the analysis of solid-state NMR experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work generalizes the quantum Stein's lemma to handle quantum hypothesis testing scenarios where the underlying source of quantum states is not strictly independent and identically distributed. By employing the quantum Wasserstein distance, the authors provide a robust, universal bound that applies to sources merely 'close' to i.i.d. This significantly broadens the applicability of fundamental quantum information bounds for technological applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The generalization of fundamental bounds like Stein's lemma to non-i.i.d. sources is crucial for modeling realistic quantum processes where local correlations or non-stationarity are present, which is relevant in quantum communication and sensing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the generalization of quantum Stein's lemma for quantum hypothesis testing, particularly when the source of quantum states deviates from the ideal independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) case.

**Main result.** They establish a universal version of the lemma applicable to sources asymptotically close to i.i.d. with respect to the normalized quantum Wasserstein distance of order 1.

**Method.** The analysis relies on defining and utilizing the quantum Wasserstein distance ($W_1$) to characterize convergence for 'almost i.i.d.' sources, extending results based on relative entropy divergences.

**Model / system.** The framework involves distinguishing between two hypotheses ($ho_n$ vs. $S(n)$) on a tensor product Hilbert space $H^{\otimes n}$, using quantum states and error exponents.

**Key observables.** Type II error exponent ($eta_\epsilon$), regularised relative entropy ($D_\infty(ho \| S)$), and the quantum Wasserstein distance ($W_1$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the Wasserstein distance (order 1), the error tolerance $\epsilon$, and the asymptotic limit $n 	o \infty$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The null source must be asymptotically close to i.i.d. with respect to the normalized quantum Wasserstein distance, and the alternative set $S(n)$ must satisfy several structural axioms (e.g., compactness, convexity).

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes mention Figure 7 illustrating a three-region decomposition of the Hilbert space $\mathbb{H}^{\otimes n}$ using projectors.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the standard GQSL for i.i.d. sources, extends the theory by introducing the Wasserstein distance to handle general sources, and culminates in solving the Stein exponent for arbitrarily varying null hypotheses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The generalised quantum Stein's lemma is a key result in quantum hypothesis testing, and connects this fundamental primitive of quantum information processing with quantum resource manipulation, a task that is central for technological applications. Prior works have proved this statement in the idealised setting of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) sequences of quantum states, and recent extensions consider also sources that are 'close' to i.i.d., according to the strict notion put forth by Mazzola, Sutter, and Renner. For several applications, however, one would need to consider yet more general sources. We establish a version of the generalised quantum Stein's lemma that is conceptually much simpler and general, as it applies to any source that is asymptotically close to an i.i.d. state with respect to the normalised quantum Wasserstein distance of order 1. As an immediate consequence, we solve the Stein exponent of a scenario where the null hypothesis is arbitrarily varying, expressing it in terms of i.i.d. Stein exponents corresponding to arbitrary states in the convex hull of the null hypothesis base set.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a geometric quantifier to measure entanglement in indistinguishable boson systems by quantifying the incompatibility of assigning individual properties to the particles. It extends the initial qubit formulation, which uses the Majorana representation, to higher-dimensional qudits. The resulting measure, $E_N(\Phi)$, provides a powerful, general tool for characterizing quantum correlations in complex many-body quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, geometrically motivated tool to quantify entanglement in systems where standard local operations theory breaks down due to particle indistinguishability, which is crucial for understanding quantum resources in many-body quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to quantify entanglement in indistinguishable boson systems by measuring the incompatibility of assigning well-defined single-particle properties to the constituent particles.

**Main result.** A generalized geometric quantifier, $E_N(\Phi)$, is introduced that successfully extends the incompatibility measure from qubits to qudits, linking it to the geometry of the state relative to the manifold of symmetrized states.

**Method.** The method involves defining the quantifier as a combination of the state's fidelity to the symmetrized state manifold and a measure of pairwise property-attribution incompatibility derived from Fubini-Study angles.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on pure states of $N$ indistinguishable bosons, initially using the Majorana stellar representation for qubits, and generalizing this framework to $d$-level bosons (qudits).

**Key observables.** The geometric quantifier $E_N(\Phi)$, pairwise fidelity $F_{ij}$, and the minimum incompatibility measure $E_{\min}^{N,\Phi}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of particles $N$, the dimension $d$ of the single-boson Hilbert space, and the pairwise fidelity $F_{ij}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending the GMW separability criterion and adapting geometric tools (like the Majorana representation) to higher dimensions where direct factorization is lost.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Majorana constellation for small $N$ (e.g., $N=3, 4$) and show the dependence of the incompatibility measure on system parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the concept using qubits and the Majorana representation, deriving an explicit quantifier $E_N(\Psi)$. It then generalizes this approach to qudits ($d>2$) by defining a composite quantifier $E_N(\Phi)$ that incorporates both fidelity distance and generalized pairwise incompatibility.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement in indistinguishable particle systems can be characterized by the impossibility of unambiguously attributing a complete set of physical properties to the individual constituent particles. In this work, we introduce a geometric quantifier of the incompatibility of such simultaneous property attribution for pure states of $N$ indistinguishable bosons. Using the Majorana stellar representation, any symmetric multi-qubit state can be expressed as the symmetrization of constituent single-particle states, allowing the associated single-particle properties to be directly linked with the corresponding Majorana stars. This establishes a direct connection between the geometry of the Majorana representation on the Bloch sphere and the entanglement criterion based on the attribution of single-particle properties in systems of identical two-level bosons. We then generalize this geometric approach to higher-dimensional bosons, extending the quantifier from qubits to qudits, while retaining a geometric interpretation of property-attribution incompatibility in terms of Fubini-Study angles.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how the initial representation of pairwise sequence relationships impacts the accuracy of phylogenetic tree reconstruction using a recursive normalized-cut (Ncut) approach. By comparing Ncut against Neighbor Joining (NJ) using various affinity matrices, the authors demonstrate that the graph construction method is crucial, requiring a joint evaluation of representation, optimization, and recursive assembly.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is bioinformatics, the methodology involves formulating graph partitioning as a QUBO problem and solving it using optimization techniques, which touches upon computational aspects relevant to quantum optimization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately reconstructing evolutionary relationships (phylogenetic trees) from molecular sequence data.

**Main result.** The choice of graph construction (pairwise affinity representation) is a critical determinant of the accuracy in recursive Ncut-based phylogenetic reconstruction, and this representation must be evaluated jointly with the reconstruction method.

**Method.** The study uses a recursive normalized-cut (Ncut) framework, formulating graph-partitioning subproblems as QUBO models solved via Simulated Bifurcation, and compares its results against Neighbor Joining (NJ).

**Model / system.** The system involves molecular sequence data (amino-acid and nucleotide alignments) used to model evolutionary divergence. Comparisons are made across various distance metrics (e.g., WAG, JC69, BLAST-derived).

**Key observables.** Internal split recovery (measured as percentage of recovered splits), and the mean paired difference in split recovery between Ncut and NJ.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Evolutionary divergence levels (branch lengths), sequence types (nucleotide vs. amino-acid), and the specific affinity representation used (e.g., normalized bit-score, local/global kernels).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the performance of the Ncut framework depends heavily on the quality of the input pairwise distance/affinity matrix.

**Figures summary.** The figures summarize the effect of post-swap refinement on accuracy, compare Ncut vs. NJ performance across different datasets, and show the overall mean paired differences in split recovery.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically evaluates how different pairwise representations influence the recursive Ncut framework, compares these results against established distance-based methods like NJ, and analyzes the impact of refinement steps.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Molecular sequence data are used to reconstruct evolutionary relationships among taxa, but reconstruction accuracy depends not only on the tree-building method but also on how pairwise sequence relationships are represented. We evaluated sequence-to-affinity representations in a recursive normalized-cut (Ncut) framework whose graph-partitioning subproblems were formulated as quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) models and solved using Simulated Bifurcation. Using simulated amino-acid and nucleotide datasets spanning multiple tree-generation settings and evolutionary divergence, we compared normalized bit-score affinities with representations derived from transformed sequence similarities and evolutionary distances, examined post-swap refinement, and used neighbor joining (NJ) as a distance-based comparator.   Affinity representation substantially affected internal split recovery, particularly for nucleotide data. JC69-based local affinities maintained comparatively high accuracy as divergence increased, whereas normalized bit-score and BLAST-derived kernel representations declined more markedly. Post-swap refinement generally improved recovery, but not consistently across individual reconstructions. NJ achieved higher mean split recovery than corresponding recursive Ncut reconstructions for WAG and JC69 distances across all evaluated conditions, whereas recursive Ncut outperformed NJ for BLAST-derived logarithmic distances under some conditions.   These results show that graph construction is an important determinant of recursive Ncut-based phylogenetic reconstruction. A representation that performs well within Ncut does not necessarily provide the most accurate use of the underlying pairwise distances. Pairwise representation, affinity transformation, optimization, and recursive tree construction should therefore be evaluated jointly.

</details>

**Authors:** Shun Hasegawa, Hayata Yamasaki  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16125v1>

(b) To derive a lower bound on the distance of the resulting hyperbolic color code, we transform the color code into a collection of associated toric codes using restriction maps and show that the color-code distance is bounded from below by the distances of these toric codes (Sec. IV). (c) We explicitly determine the scaling of the number of logical qubits k and the code distance d with the number of physical qubits...</sub>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a new family of hyperbolic color codes that overcome previous limitations by achieving both a constant encoding rate and polynomial code distance. The methodology combines advanced algebraic topology with hyperbolic geometry to define these codes. This breakthrough provides a powerful theoretical testbed for developing high-performance, fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the theoretical limits of quantum error correction by providing concrete constructions for high-rate codes with large, polynomially scaling distances, which is crucial for building fault-tolerant quantum computers.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to construct hyperbolic color codes that simultaneously achieve a constant encoding rate and polynomial code distance.

**Main result.** The authors construct a family of hyperbolic color codes that achieve both constant rate and polynomial distance, particularly in even dimensions, establishing a significant improvement over previous logarithmic distance bounds.

**Method.** The construction relies on building codes from arithmetic hyperbolic manifolds that support polynomial-distance hyperbolic toric codes, utilizing algebraic topology concepts like chain complexes and homology groups.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum error-correcting codes, specifically hyperbolic color codes, which are a high-rate quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) code structure relevant for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Key observables.** Code distance ($d$), encoding rate (aiming for constant rate), and the number of logical qubits ($k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The construction applies to arbitrary dimensions $D \geq 4$, with specific results detailed for even dimensions ($D=2m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis builds upon the existence of hyperbolic manifolds supporting the necessary structures and relies on applying advanced theorems like the Lück approximation theorem.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the construction process, showing the use of barycentric subdivision for the color code, the transformation to a toric code for distance bounding, and the explicit determination of scaling exponents for $k$ and $d$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical framework using chain complexes to define code parameters ($k$ and $d$), proves the existence of the desired codes via Theorem 1, and provides detailed algebraic and geometric machinery (e.g., analyzing symmetry groups $SOf$ and spectral gaps) to establish the polynomial bounds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances in quantum hardware relax the strict geometric-locality constraints traditionally imposed on quantum error-correcting codes, motivating interest in high-rate quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes. At the same time, color codes provide a particularly rich setting for fault-tolerant quantum computation, underlying protocols such as single-shot error correction and self-correcting quantum computation. Hyperbolic color codes provide a class of high-rate qLDPC codes that also retain the structural features of color codes relevant to fault-tolerant quantum computation. However, previous constructions of hyperbolic color codes have achieved at most logarithmic code distance. In this work, we construct hyperbolic color codes with both constant rate and polynomial distance by building on arithmetic hyperbolic manifolds that support polynomial-distance hyperbolic toric codes. Our construction applies in arbitrary dimension \(D\geq 4\). In even dimensions, the resulting type-\(D/2\) color codes have constant encoding rate and polynomial distance, while in other cases the number of logical qubits and the code distance both exhibit polynomial scaling. We further derive explicit exponents for polynomial lower bounds as functions of the dimension and code type. These results establish a family of hyperbolic color codes simultaneously achieving constant rate and polynomial distance and providing a testbed to explore fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols that combine high-rate quantum codes with the structural advantages of color codes.

</details>

### [Improved Separations between Quantum and Classical Communication Complexity of Total Functions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16726v1)

**Authors:** François Le Gall  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16726v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper significantly advances the understanding of the gap between quantum and classical communication complexity for total functions. By refining existing frameworks, the authors achieve stronger lower bounds for randomized communication relative to the quantum communication required. This work is a major theoretical result in quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical, the techniques used—especially the construction of complex functions and the use of extractors—are foundational tools in quantum information theory that underpin the understanding of computational limits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve the known separations between the quantum and classical communication complexity of total Boolean functions.

**Main result.** It establishes significant separations, showing that for two quantum messages, the randomized communication complexity $R(f)$ can be exponentially larger than the quantum complexity $Q_2(f)$, achieving $R(f) = e\Omega(\sqrt{n})$ versus $Q_2(f) = 	ext{polylog}(n)$.

**Method.** The improvements rely on refining previous constructions by replacing lookup tables with seeded linear extractors and constructing total functions $F_{	ext{tot}}$ from partial functions $F$ to lift the separation bounds.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on the communication complexity of total Boolean functions $f: \{0, 1\}^n 	imes \{0, 1\}^n 	o \{0, 1\}$, involving quantum and randomized communication protocols.

**Key observables.** Randomized communication complexity $R(f)$, quantum communication complexity $Q_r(f)$, and the input size overhead $N$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The separation scales involve polylogarithmic quantum communication versus bounds like $e\Omega(\sqrt{n})$ or $e\Omega(n^{1-\varepsilon})$ for randomized communication.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof heavily relies on the existence of strong seeded extractors and the ability to construct total functions $F_{	ext{tot}}$ from partial functions $F$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon previous work by first establishing general conversion theorems (Theorem 4) relating partial to total function complexities, and then applying these techniques to specific functions (like Shapem or k-Forrelation) to derive concrete, improved separation bounds (Theorems 2 and 3).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We refine Gavinsky's framework (arXiv:2608.18784) for exponential separations between quantum and randomized communication complexity of total functions and obtain larger separations: polylogarithmic quantum communication versus $\tildeΩ(\sqrt n)$ randomized communication with two quantum messages, and versus $Ω(n^{1-\varepsilon})$ for every fixed $0<\varepsilon<1$ with more quantum messages.

</details>

**Summary.** This work systematically studies the effect of La substitution on the heavy-fermion superconductor CeRh2As2. By measuring transport and magnetic properties, the authors find that while the electronic state remains largely intact, the superconducting and magnetic ordering temperatures are rapidly suppressed. This suppression is primarily attributed to disorder-induced pair breaking, offering insights into the nature of unconventional superconductivity in correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The clear demonstration that disorder (from substitution) can suppress superconductivity in a manner distinct from hydrostatic pressure provides a powerful experimental tool for understanding unconventional pairing mechanisms in correlated electron systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how substituting Cerium (Ce) with Lanthanum (La) affects the low-temperature superconducting and magnetic phases in the heavy-fermion superconductor CeRh2As2.

**Main result.** La substitution rapidly suppresses both the superconducting transition temperature (Tc) and the magnetic ordering temperature (T0), suggesting that disorder-induced pair breaking is the dominant mechanism, rather than simple lattice expansion effects.

**Method.** The research employed single-crystal measurements, including electrical resistivity, specific heat, and magnetic susceptibility, complemented by theoretical modeling using the Abrikosov-Gor'kov formalism and Crystal Electric Field (CEF) theory.

**Model / system.** The system is the heavy-fermion superconductor CeRh2As2 and its doped derivative Ce(1-x)La(x)Rh2As2. The physics is governed by the interplay between localized Ce 4f moments and itinerant conduction electrons, characteristic of heavy fermion behavior.

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperatures (Tc, T0), resistivity coherence maximum (T*max), specific heat divided by temperature (C/T), and magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** La concentration (x up to 0.1), effective negative pressure (-0.3 GPa), Kondo energy scale, and superconducting gap anisotropy parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The rapid suppression of Tc is attributed to substitution-induced disorder causing strong pair breaking, and the analysis relies on fitting magnetic data to CEF models.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the monotonic increase of lattice parameters with La substitution, the suppression of T*max, Tc, and T0 with increasing x, and the evolution of C/T vs. T, illustrating the impact of disorder.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically examines the structural changes (lattice parameters), electronic state preservation (valence, non-Fermi liquid behavior), and the dramatic suppression of superconducting and magnetic ordering temperatures as a function of La doping concentration.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

CeRh$_2$As$_2$ has been receiving considerable attention due to its unusual two-phase superconductivity. The superconducting (SC) phase appears at $T_{\mathrm{c}}$ = 0.35 K in an ordered state (phase I) of the Ce-4$f$ moments, which develops below $T_{\mathrm{0}}$ = 0.55 K. The microscopic nature of phase I has not been fully established yet. We report a single-crystal study of the effect of La substitution on these low-temperature phases in Ce$_{1-\textit{x}}$La$_\textit{x}$Rh$_2$As$_2$ up to $x$ = 0.1. The lattice parameters increase monotonically with $x$, corresponding to an effective negative pressure of approximately -0.3 GPa for $x$ = 0.1. While the Ce$^{3+}$ local valence state and the non-Fermi-liquid behavior are preserved, the resistivity coherence maximum $T^{*}_{\mathrm{max}}$ $\approx$ 45 K in the pristine sample shifts to lower temperatures with increasing $x$, indicating a suppression of the Kondo energy scale upon lattice expansion. On the other hand, both $T_{\mathrm{c}}$ and $T_{\mathrm{0}}$ are rapidly suppressed to below 0.1 K for $x$ $>$ 0.05. Notably, such a rapid decrease of $T_{\mathrm{c}}$ under moderate negative pressure is unexpected, but is rather consistent with substitution-induced disorder effect which leads to strong pair breaking in unconventional superconductors. The observed fragility of phase I under both pressure and disorder may imply its itinerant origin.

</details>

In this example, ˆv(Fσ) = 1 and ˆe(Fσ) = 4, and the bounds say that synthesis requires n −ˆv(Fσ) = 3 “link” CNOTs and ˆe(Fσ) −ˆv(Fσ) = 3 “cut” CNOTs. The middle invariant is obtained as the rank of the matrix...</sub>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced mathematical tools to find lower bounds on the minimum number of CNOT gates needed to implement any Clifford quantum circuit. By extending the Link-Middle-Cut framework to use symplectic tableaux, the authors derive multiple, computable invariants. These bounds are shown to be tight for simple cases like qubit permutations and significantly improve heuristic search algorithms for quantum circuit optimization.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical tools developed (LMC extension, determinantal invariants) provide powerful, computable lower bounds for circuit synthesis, which is fundamental to understanding the efficiency limits of quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is minimizing the number of CNOT gates required to synthesize a given Clifford operator on $n$ qubits.

**Main result.** The authors derive efficiently computable lower bounds for the CNOT complexity using an extension of the Link-Middle-Cut (LMC) framework, proving tightness for qubit permutations as $3(n-k)$.

**Method.** The method extends the LMC framework by mapping the problem to symplectic tableaux and introducing block-support connectivity graphs and determinantal invariants to establish rigorous lower bounds.

**Model / system.** The system is an $n$-qubit quantum circuit implementing Clifford operators, which are analyzed using the mathematical structure of the symplectic group $	ext{Sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_2)$ via symplectic tableaux.

**Key observables.** CNOT-complexity (minimum CNOT count), link/cut invariants ($\hat{v}(F), \hat{e}(F)$), and determinantal invariants ($	ext{rank}(M(F) + I_n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$) and the number of cycles ($k$) in the corresponding permutation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to extend the LMC framework from CNOT-only circuits to general Clifford operators, and it addresses the case where output qubit relabeling is free.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the link–middle–cut bound for a four-cycle permutation, explicitly calculating the required links, middles, and cuts to match the known lower bound of 9 CNOT gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first extending the LMC framework to Clifford operators using symplectic tableaux. It then derives multiple, efficiently computable lower bounds based on graph invariants (links/cuts) and determinantal invariants (middle contribution). Finally, it demonstrates the utility of these bounds by using them as an admissible heuristic in an A*-based search algorithm.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Minimizing the number of CNOT gates required to synthesize a Clifford operator is a central problem in quantum circuit optimization. We extend the link--middle--cut (LMC) framework for linear reversible circuits to Clifford operators represented by binary symplectic tableaux. By introducing block-support connectivity graphs and determinantal invariants, we obtain efficiently computable lower bounds on ancilla-free CNOT complexity. We prove that these bounds are tight for qubit permutations: a permutation of $n$ qubits with $k$ cycles requires exactly $3(n-k)$ CNOT gates, even when arbitrary one-qubit Clifford gates are available. We also derive efficiently computable bounds for synthesis up to a permutation of the output qubits, corresponding to free qubit relabeling. Finally, we further demonstrate the utility of the bound by using it as an admissible heuristic for $A^*$-based Clifford synthesis and show that, on benchmark instances, the resulting search can prove optimality beyond what was possible with earlier SAT-based methods, and that it may be used in combination with earlier $A^*$ heuristics to improve the CNOT counts obtained through heuristic search.

</details>

The quantum metric dipole vanishes identically at the charge neutrality point (µ = 0) and exhibits odd parity under chemical potential inversion, DQM(−µ) = −DQM(µ). The preserved spatial mirror symmetry My enforces Gxx(qx, −qy) and Gxy(qx, −qy) to transform as even and odd respectively, resulting in a vanishing net quantum metric dipole.</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the nonlinear Hall effect in a hybrid system combining a warped Topological Insulator and a d-wave altermagnet. By breaking spatial mirror symmetry, the authors show that the intrinsic quantum metric dipole (DQM) becomes the dominant, measurable source of the non-linear response. This DQM is highly tunable via the relative angle of the altermagnetic order parameter, suggesting a new spintronic control mechanism.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between magnetic symmetry breaking (altermagnetism) and measurable quantum geometric responses (DQM) provides a clean, tunable platform for studying topological transport effects, which is relevant to understanding exotic quantum states in condensed matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the origin and tunability of the nonlinear Hall effect in topological insulators coupled to altermagnets, aiming to isolate intrinsic quantum geometric contributions.

**Main result.** The intrinsic quantum metric dipole (DQM) drives the non-linear Hall response, and its magnitude and sign can be continuously tuned by rotating the altermagnetic orientation angle φ.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving the Berry curvature and the quantum metric tensor components, followed by calculating the DQM via a Fermi surface line integral.

**Model / system.** The model is a hexagonally warped Topological Insulator (TI) surface state proximity-coupled to a d-wave altermagnet. The system is described by an effective continuum Hamiltonian incorporating warping and d-wave splitting terms.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear Hall conductivities, Berry curvature dipole (DBC), and the intrinsic quantum metric dipole (DQM).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative orientation angle φ of the altermagnetic order parameter, and the chemical potential $\mu$ (where the response vanishes at $\mu=0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the unperturbed system possesses Time-Reversal and Spatial Mirror symmetries, leading to zero net dipoles, which are then broken by the altermagnetic coupling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the anisotropic energy dispersion showing warping and d-wave splitting, and the momentum-space distribution of the DQM integrand.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and symmetry constraints, derives the relevant geometric tensors (Berry curvature, metric tensor), and then analyzes how symmetry breaking via the altermagnetism allows for a finite, tunable DQM that dictates the non-linear Hall response.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Low-dimensional Dirac systems offer a versatile platform for quantum-geometric non-linear transport. Hexagonal warping in topological insulator (TI) surface states generates rich momentum-space geometric textures. However, mirror and time-reversal symmetries strictly constrain both the Berry curvature and quantum metric dipoles to zero. Here, we show that proximity-coupling a hexagonally warped TI to a d-wave altermagnet lifts spatial mirror symmetry via the interplay of C_{3v} warping and d-wave spin-splitting. This symmetry breaking enables finite Berry curvature dipole components, which, however, remain heavily suppressed near charge neutrality, establishing the intrinsic quantum metric dipole as the sole driver of the non-linear Hall response. This scattering-independent response vanishes at μ= 0 and exhibits odd parity under chemical potential inversion. Importantly, rotating the altermagnetic orientation angle φcontinuously tunes quantum metric dipole and induces a sign reversal, peaking near φ=0 and vanishing at φ= π/4. Our findings highlight TI-altermagnet interfaces as ideal candidates for gate- and orientation-tunable quantum metric spintronics.

</details>

(b) Log-log plot of σQ(Lb) versus Lb for the three lattices. Dashed lines are power-law fits σQ ∼L−α b , yielding α = 1.51(1) (VD), 1.41(12) (Smith-hat), and 0.98(1) (diluted, p = 0.75).</sub>

**Summary.** This study investigates how structural disorder—aperiodic, topological, or random—affects the critical behavior of the 2D Ashkin-Teller model. By comparing results against the Harris and HBV criteria, the authors show that geometrically constrained disorder preserves clean critical exponents, while uncorrelated disorder causes a breakdown of this clean behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous testbed for modern critical phenomena theory, specifically comparing the predictive power of the Harris criterion against the HBV criterion in geometrically constrained or disordered systems, which has implications for understanding complex materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the stability of clean critical behavior in the Ashkin-Teller model when subjected to three distinct types of structural disorder: aperiodic, topological, and uncorrelated.

**Main result.** The critical behavior remains stable and consistent with the clean AT universality class for aperiodic (Smith-hat) and topological (VD) lattices, satisfying the HBV criterion. Conversely, the uncorrelated (diluted) lattice shows a crossover to a disorder-dominated fixed line, consistent with the Harris criterion.

**Method.** The research employs Finite-Size Scaling (FSS) analysis on Monte Carlo simulations to determine critical exponents ($
u, \eta_h, \eta_p$) and characterizes disorder using the decay exponent ($\alpha$) of the block-averaged coordination fluctuation ($\sigma_Q$).

**Model / system.** The system is the two-dimensional Ashkin-Teller (AT) model, analyzed on lattices including the Smith-hat aperiodic tiling, Voronoi-Delaunay (VD) random triangulations, and site-diluted square lattices. The Hamiltonian involves two Ising variables per site.

**Key observables.** Critical exponents ($
u, \eta_h, \eta_p$), coordination fluctuation decay exponent ($\alpha$), and the Binder cumulant ($U_m$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The disorder strength parameter ($J_4$) and the occupation probability ($p$) for site dilution are key parameters governing the crossover regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing numerical results against predictions from the Harris and Harris-Barghathi-Vojta (HBV) criteria, and assumes the applicability of the Coulomb-gas self-consistency check for clean universality.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of the Smith-hat and VD lattices, and the analysis uses Binder cumulants ($U_m$) to locate critical points and determine exponents.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the critical behavior across the three disorder types, using the coordination fluctuation exponent ($\alpha$) to characterize disorder, and then applying FSS and theoretical criteria (Harris vs. HBV) to determine if the clean critical exponents are preserved.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using the two-dimensional Ashkin-Teller (AT) model, we compare criticality on three non-periodic lattices: the Smith-hat aperiodic tiling, Voronoi-Delaunay (VD) random triangulations, and uncorrelated diluted square lattices. The decay of the block-averaged coordination fluctuation $σ_Q$ with exponent $α$ is used to describe the connectivity disorder. The first two lattices share the same exponent $α$, which differs from that of the third. We consider the regime where the correlation-length exponent $ν<1$, where randomness is relevant according to the Harris criterion $d ν\le 2$, but should be irrelevant in cases of the Smith-hat tiling and VD triangulations, where $αν>1$, according to the Harris--Barghathi--Vojta (HBV) criterion. For the diluted lattice, we indeed find that the clean universality behavior breaks down along the entire critical line, indicating a crossover to a fixed line dominated by disorder, in line with both the Harris criterion and the HBV criterion. In contrast, both VD and Smith-hat lattices display critical exponents consistent with the clean AT universality class, as validated by a Coulomb-gas self-consistency check, violating the Harris criterion while conforming to the HBV criterion.

</details>

### [One Gate at a Time: Complexity Growth in Random Quantum Circuits](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17457v1)

**Authors:** Zhi Li  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17457v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.17457_figures/2609.17457_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Schematic decomposition of the circuit into R blocks, each consisting of a mixing segment of length m followed by an active segment of length d. Thus, the total number of gates is T = (m+d)R.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the complexity of random quantum circuits, proving that the minimum number of gates needed to approximate the circuit's output grows almost linearly with time, $\Omega(T/\log T)$. It achieves this by developing novel techniques rooted in stochastic calculus and geometric functional analysis to establish strong small-ball estimates. This work significantly improves prior lower bounds on quantum circuit complexity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The result provides a significantly improved lower bound on circuit complexity compared to previous methods, suggesting that random circuits mix rapidly and efficiently explore the unitary space, which is crucial for understanding quantum computation limits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the growth rate of the constant-error circuit complexity required to approximate a random unitary quantum circuit.

**Main result.** The constant-error circuit complexity for an all-to-all 2-local random unitary circuit grows almost linearly with time, specifically $\Omega(T / \log T)$, independent of the system size $n$ up to certain time scales.

**Method.** The analysis combines geometric functional analysis, stochastic calculus (using Itô's formula), and $\epsilon$-net arguments applied to small-ball estimates for the unitary group.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on random unitary quantum circuits, including the all-to-all 2-local random unitary circuit and the random Pauli rotations model, operating on a Hilbert space of dimension $D=2^n$.

**Key observables.** Constant-error circuit complexity $C_\epsilon(U)$, the normalized Hilbert-Schmidt distance $d_2(U, V)$, and the overlap function $	ext{Re} 	ext{Tr}(V^\dagger U(	heta))$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time $T$ (number of gates), system size $n$, and the error tolerance $\epsilon$ (e.g., $\epsilon=1/2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach avoids relying on spectral gaps or unitary designs, instead deriving bounds from small-ball estimates related to the circuit's exploration of the unitary group.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically decomposes the circuit into blocks, showing mixing and active segments, relating total time $T$ to the number of segments $R$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing small-ball estimates for the distance between the random circuit output and any fixed unitary (using stochastic calculus on the overlap function) to deriving the final complexity lower bounds via $\epsilon$-net arguments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A random unitary quantum circuit is expected to be incompressible for exponentially long times. We show that the constant-error circuit complexity of a random unitary circuit grows almost linearly with time as $Ω(T/\log T)$. The bound holds for all $2\leq T\leq 4^n$ where $n$ is the system size, and involves no other $n$-dependence. This improves previous lower bounds derived from spectral gaps and unitary designs by a factor of $\mathrm{poly}(n)$. Drawing on insights from stochastic calculus, geometric functional analysis, and randomized linear algebra, our approach exploits the circuit's response to variations of individual gates and requires no control over convergence to high-order unitary designs.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates the magnetic excitations and topological properties of MXene Fe2C. It successfully links the microscopic superexchange mechanism, detailing how orbital overlaps govern the exchange coupling, to the macroscopic topological features of the magnon bands. This provides crucial theoretical guidance for designing next-generation spintronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between orbital physics (superexchange) and topological band theory in a 2D magnetic material is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to investigate the topological properties of magnon bands and the orbital-resolved superexchange mechanism in magnetic MXene Fe2C.

**Main result.** The research establishes a direct link between the orbital-resolved anisotropic superexchange and the topological properties of magnon bands, identifying a Dirac point at the K point.

**Method.** The authors employed the linear spin wave method, combined with first-principles calculations and a two-band model, to analyze the magnetic excitations and topology.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the magnetic MXene Fe2C. The analysis models the magnetic interactions using a Heisenberg Hamiltonian, incorporating intralayer and interlayer exchange couplings derived from orbital-resolved superexchange paths.

**Key observables.** Spin wave dispersions, Berry curvature, valley Chern number, edge states, and the orbital-resolved anisotropic superexchange coupling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interlayer exchange coupling is found to be stronger than the intralayer coupling, and the topology is protected by the C3v rotational symmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the linear spin wave approximation and utilizes a two-band model for topological calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the structure of MXene Fe2C and different spin configurations; other figures likely show the magnon dispersion and topological band structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first calculating spin wave dispersions using LSWT, then analyzing the topology via a two-band model, and finally deriving a refined model using perturbation theory and tight-binding to microscopically explain the orbital-resolved superexchange mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic MXenes are promising candidates for future spintronics due to their intriguing properties. Nevertheless, the topological properties of magnon bands and superexchange mechanism remain relatively underexplored. In this work, we have calculated the spin wave dispersions for MXene Fe$_2$C using the linear spin wave method, with the exchange coupling parameters obtained from first-principles calculations. Owing to the staggered stacking of two Fe sub-lattices, the interlayer exchange coupling lifts the degeneracy of the ferromagnetic magnon modes. A Dirac point is identified in the magnon bands at the $K$ point. The topological properties of magnon bands, including Berry curvature, valley Chern number and edge states, are further computed by means of a two-band model. We also derive an effective Hamiltonian to explain the magnonic topology, which is protected by the $C_{3v}$ rotational symmetry. To understand the microscopic origin of the exchange couplings that govern the magnon bands, we then construct a refined model integrating perturbation theory and the tight-binding approach. The ferromagnetic superexchange coupling is mediated by the virtual hopping between the d orbitals of two distinct Fe sites via a pair of orthogonal p orbitals at the bridging C site. In detail, the intralayer exchange coupling is governed by the in-plane $p_x$ and $p_y$ orbitals, while the out-of-plane $p_z$ orbital is responsible for the interlayer exchange coupling. Our results establish a direct link between the orbital-resolved anisotropic superexchange and topological properties of the magnon bands in magnetic MXene Fe$_2$C, providing theoretical guidance for future spintronics applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper studies 'phantom codes,' a rare class of quantum error-correcting codes with special symmetry properties. The authors establish that determining if a code is phantom is computationally hard (GI-hard) and provide constructions for optimal families. Crucially, they prove a distance limit, showing that for codes with high logical dimension relative to their check weight, the distance must be severely constrained.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between the algebraic structure of quantum error-correcting codes and computational complexity (GI) provides deep insights into the inherent limitations of quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Characterizing 'phantom codes,' which are stabilizer codes where all logical CNOT gates can be realized by physical qubit permutations, and determining the inherent limitations on their parameters.

**Main result.** The authors proved that recognizing phantomness is as hard as Graph Isomorphism, constructed rate-optimal phantom qLDPC families, and established a distance no-go theorem for such codes.

**Method.** The work combines algebraic coding theory (stabilizer codes, CSS codes) with computational complexity theory (Graph Isomorphism reduction) and group theory (automorphism groups).

**Model / system.** The model is the stabilizer code, specifically CSS codes, defined by their parameters $(n, k, d)$ and analyzed via their automorphism groups $	ext{PAut}(S)$.

**Key observables.** Code parameters: physical length ($n$), logical dimension ($k$), distance ($d$), and check weight ($w$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling of $k$ relative to $n$ (e.g., $k = \Theta(\log n)$ or $k = \omega(\sqrt{\log n})$) is critical for the theorems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes the code is a binary CSS code and relies on the structure of the automorphism group action on the logical Pauli group.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the phantom property, then proving the computational hardness (GI-hardness), followed by constructing optimal qLDPC families, and concluding with the distance no-go theorem.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

An $[[n,k,d]]$ stabilizer code is phantom if every in-block logical CNOT gate can be realized by a permutation of its physical qubits. This eliminates the large and complicated physical overhead normally required for logical entangling gates. Yet this symmetry is highly restrictive: phantom codes are rare, the number of logical qubits is limited to $k=O(\log n)$, and no phantom qLDPC family with growing logical dimension was previously known.   We make three contributions.   1. We prove that recognizing phantomness of a given stabilizer code is at least as hard as Graph Isomorphism, even for CSS codes encoding only $k=2$ logical qubits.   2. We propose the first rate optimal phantom qLDPC families: for every fixed $D$, our CSS families achieve the maximal logical scaling $k=Θ(\log n)$ and distance $d\geq D$.   3. We prove a distance no-go theorem: every phantom code family with $k=ω(\sqrt{\log n})$ and check weight $w=O(1)$ satisfies $d\leq w$ for sufficiently large $n$.   Thus our fixed-distance qLDPC families are distance-scaling optimal at the maximal logical scaling $k=Θ(\log n)$.

</details>
