### [Interplay between Interlayer Shift and Twist: Twisted van der Waals Nanowires Driven by Rotational Twinning](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16771v1)

**Authors:** Dong-gyu Kim, Kisung Kang, Hani Kang, Kihyun Lee, Yangjin Lee, Jinsub Park, Joong-Eon Jung, Min Kim, Myeongjin Jang, Aloysius Soon, Kwanpyo Kim  
**Type:** both · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.16771v1>  
**Analysis basis:** abstract only; PDF text extraction failed or PDF unavailable
**Topic relevance:** `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work investigates the structural control in van der Waals nanowires, focusing on the interplay between interlayer shift and twist. The researchers identify and characterize a novel twisting mechanism driven by rotational twinning in GeSe2-xTe_x. This establishes rotational twinning as a fundamental, intrinsic method for engineering twist in these layered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state materials science, the concept of strain-induced structural transitions and emergent symmetries due to local interactions (twinning) has parallels in understanding quantum phase transitions or engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The interplay between interlayer shift and twist in van der Waals (vdW) layered materials, and reliable mechanisms for fine-tuning these parameters, remain largely unexplored.

**Main result.** The authors report a previously unrecognized twisting mechanism in vdW crystals arising from preferred tilted stacking and rotational twinning, establishing this as an intrinsic route for spontaneous twist formation.

**Method.** The study combines electron diffraction and atomic-resolution STEM imaging with first-principles calculations and structural relaxation simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is group-IV chalcogenide GeSe2-xTe_x grown as nanowires, which exhibit continuous rotation of lattice planes along the growth axis.

**Key observables.** Continuous rotation of lattice planes, twist rates dependent on nanowire radius, and the presence of a continuous rotational twin boundary.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nanowire radius, interlayer stacking registry, and strain cost imposed by rotational twinning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The twisting deformation originates from the energetic competition between the preferred interlayer stacking registry and the strain cost from rotational twinning.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the context of vdW engineering, presents experimental evidence of continuous rotation and twinning via STEM/diffraction, and then uses first-principles calculations to confirm the energetic origin of the twist.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In van der Waals(vdW) layered materials, interlayer shift and twist have enabled the control of material properties through polytype and moire engineering. Various approaches, including bottom-up synthesis and manual layer-by-layer stacking, have been utilized to engineer targeted stacking configurations. However, the interplay between interlayer shift and twist, as well as reliable mechanisms for fine-tuning these parameters, remains largely unexplored. Here, we report a previously unrecognized twisting mechanism arising from preferred tilted stacking and twinning in vdW crystals. Electron diffraction and atomic-resolution scanning transmission electron microscopy(STEM) imaging reveal that the lattice planes of group-IV chalcogenide GeSe_{2-x}Te_x rotate continuously along the nanowire growth axis, with twist rates depending systematically on nanowire radius. Atomic-scale imaging further identifies a continuous rotational twin boundary extending along the central region of the nanowire. First-principles calculations and structural relaxation simulations confirm that the twisting deformation originates from energetic competition between the preferred interlayer stacking registry and the strain cost imposed by rotational twinning. These findings establish rotational twinning as an intrinsic route to spontaneous twist formation and provide a design principle for realizing twist-engineered vdW crystals with compatible crystal symmetries and stacking motifs.

</details>

### [NaFeP: first pairing prediction for an unmade 111 iron phosphide, and the pnictogen-height rule inside one compound](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17028v1)

**Authors:** Reinaldo Inácio  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.17028v1>

**Summary.** This work presents the first gap-symmetry prediction for the unmade iron phosphide NaFeP, classifying it as an s± superconductor. The authors utilize a sophisticated computational 'judge' that tests the material against established structural rules, notably the pnictogen-height rule, providing a detailed, albeit conditional, roadmap for future experimental synthesis.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state superconductivity, the rigorous application of advanced computational diagnostics (like the Stoner gate and symmetry readers) to predict emergent quantum phases in novel materials is highly relevant to understanding strongly correlated electron behavior.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To predict the superconducting gap symmetry for the unmade iron phosphide compound NaFeP using a systematic computational framework.

**Main result.** The paper predicts NaFeP to be an s± superconductor with a near-nodal inner hole sheet, while also testing the pnictogen-height rule within this single compound.

**Method.** The prediction relies on applying a 'physics judge' pipeline, which incorporates DFT calculations, a Stoner gate, and a frame-independent symmetry reader to classify the superconducting gap structure.

**Model / system.** The system is the iron phosphide NaFeP, analyzed using DFT (GGA-PBE, PAW) calculations and a gated Wannier tight-binding model. The analysis compares results derived from $d$-only vs. $d+p$ orbital bases.

**Key observables.** Superconducting gap symmetry ($s\pm$ vs. $d$-wave), nodal status ($\min |g|/\max |g|$ ratio), and the critical interaction strength ($U_{	ext{crit}}$) from the Stoner gate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phosphorus height ($1.16 	ext{ Å}$), empirical structural threshold ($\approx 1.33 	ext{ Å}$), and the nesting vector $(1/2, 1/2, 1/2)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The prediction is conditional on the structural measurement of the nodal/nodeless gap, and no critical temperature ($T_c$) is asserted.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the Fermi surface colored by gap sign (confirming $s\pm$), the nodal ratio across sheets, and the application of manufacturability gates (F0-F5) along with the pnictogen-height scan.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of applying a sophisticated 'physics judge' to a single, unmade candidate (NaFeP), detailing the necessary computational refinements (Stoner gate, symmetry reader), comparing results across different orbital bases, and finally testing the structural pnictogen-height rule within the compound itself.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Companion to arXiv:2609.10614, which built a physics judge that classifies the gap symmetry of a superconductor candidate from its structure and showed that generation in the wire families re-finds the known canon. Here the judge is applied end to end to the single compound that survived its own screen. From 58 iron-pnictide compositions in the electronic window of BaFe$_2$As$_2$ absent from the Materials Project, a zero-cost literature-and-stability screen leaves one with no bulk synthesis and no band-structure, magnetic or pairing calculation: NaFeP (111-type, P4/nmm, Fe$^{2+}$ d$^6$, arsenic-free). The judge, refined with an explicit Stoner gate and a frame-independent symmetry reader, returns a paramagnetic DFT ground state and an s$\pm$ gap: a clean sign reversal between the hole cylinders at Gamma and the electron sheets at the zone corner, at nesting vector (1/2,1/2,1/2), in every basis and cell tried. Whether the gap is nodeless is where the instrument is tested. The phosphorus height (1.16 Å) lies below the empirical threshold of about 1.33 Åseparating nodal from nodeless pnictides, so the structural rule calls NaFeP nodal. The sealed verdict on a metal-only basis said nodeless; a d+p basis that passes the same gates puts the inner hole cylinder at the node threshold (min|g|/max|g| = 0.07-0.10), and a scan of the phosphorus height reproduces, inside one compound, the ingredients of the height rule: a third hole pocket, a switch of the nesting vector to (1/2,1/2,0), a monotonic approach to the Stoner instability, and near-degeneracy of s$\pm$ and d channels beyond 1.33 Å. The prediction we stand behind is the d+p reading: an s$\pm$ superconductor with a near-nodal inner hole sheet, on the LiFeP side of the rule, with the disagreement between bases reported, not resolved by choice. No Tc is asserted; the discriminating measurement, nodal or nodeless, is stated in advance.

</details>
